 Three-edge-coloring projective planar cubic graphs:
 A generalization of the Four Color Theorem

 August 24, 2026 

 Abstract 
    
 We prove that every cyclically 4-edge-connected cubic graph that can be embedded in the projective plane, with the single exception of the Petersen graph, is 3-edge-colorable.
In other words, the only (non-trivial) snark that can be embedded in the projective plane is the Petersen graph. 
    
 This implies that a 2-connected cubic (multi)graph that can be embedded in the projective plane is not 3-edge-colorable if and only if it can be obtained from the Petersen graph by replacing each vertex
by a 2-edge-connected planar cubic (multi)graph.
Here, a replacement of a vertex  v v  in a cubic graph  G G  is the operation that takes a 2-connected planar (cubic) multigraph  H H  containing some vertex  u u  of degree 3, unifying  G − v G-v  and  H − u H-u , and connecting the vertices in  N G ​ [ v ] N_{G}[v]  in  G − v G-v  with the three neighbors of  u u  in  H − u H-u  with 3 edges. Any graph obtained in such a way is said to be  Petersen-like . 
    
 This result is a nontrivial generalization of the Four Color Theorem, and its proof requires a combination of extensive computer verification and computer-free extension of existing proofs on colorability. 
    
 Using this result, we obtain the following algorithmic consequence. 
    
 
 Input : A cubic graph  G G .
 Output : Either a 3-edge-coloring of  G G , an obstruction showing that  G G  is not 3-edge-colorable, or the conclusion that  G G  cannot be embedded in the projective plane (certified by exposing a forbidden minor for the projective plane contained in  G G ).
 Time complexity :  O ⁡ ( n 2 ) O(n^{2}) , where  n = | V ⁡ ( G ) | n=|V(G)| . 
 
    
 An unexpected consequence of this result is a coloring-flow duality statement for the projective plane: A cubic graph embedded in the projective plane is 3-edge-colorable if and only if its dual multigraph is 5-vertex-colorable. Moreover, we show that a 2-edge connected graph embedded in the projective plane admits a nowhere-zero 4-flow unless it is Peteren-like (in which case it does not admit nowhere-zero 4-flows). This proves a strengthening of the Tutte 4-flow conjecture for graphs on the projective plane. 
    
 Some of our proofs require extensive computer verification. The necessary source codes, together with the input and output files and the complete set of more than 6000 reducible configurations are
available on Github 1 1 
           1 
          
          
          
         https://github.com/edge-coloring . Refer to the “README.md” file in each directory for instructions on how to run each program. Moreover, we provide pseudocodes for all our computer verifications, see Section  C , which can be considered as an Addendum to this paper. 
  
 
 
 1  Introduction 

 
 1.1  The four color theorem, snarks, and our main result 

 
 The four-color theorem (4CT) states that every loopless planar graph is 4-colorable.
It was first shown by Appel and Haken  [ 1 ,  2 ]  in 1977, and a simplified version of the proof was given in 1997 by Robertson et al.  [ 17 ] . The latter proof was used by the same authors  [ 18 ]  to obtain a quadratic-time algorithm for 4-coloring planar graphs. 
 
 
 Looking outside the set of planar graphs, it seems extremely difficult to extend the 4CT to larger families of graphs.
Indeed, it has been known that for every surface with positive Euler genus there are infinitely many  5 5 -color-critical graphs 2 2 
               2 
              
              
              
            A graph  G G  is said to be  k k -color-critical  if  G G  is not  ( k − 1 ) (k-1) -colorable, but every proper subgraph of  G G  is.  that can be embedded in the surface  [ 15 ] .
This means that while there are no 5-color-critical planar graphs, there are infinitely many 5-color-critical graphs on the projective plane. When regarding vertex-coloring, there is a major gap between planar graphs and graphs of higher genus. 
 
 
 On the other hand, the 4CT has another equivalent formulation by taking planar duality: 
 
 
 Theorem 1.1 . 
 
 Every  2 2 -connected cubic planar graph is  3 3 -edge-colorable. 
 
 
 
 While the Four Color Theorem has no extension to other surfaces, the version of Theorem  1.1  has some chance to hold under some additional assumptions. This led Tutte in 1966 to propose his famous  4 4 -Flow Conjecture . 
 
 
 Conjecture 1   (Tutte,  [ 20 ] ) . 
 
 Every  2 2 -connected graph with no Petersen graph minor admits a nowhere-zero  4 4 -flow. 
 
 
 
 While this conjecture is still open, its special case restricted to cubic graphs was proved in 1997 by Robertson, Seymour, and Thomas  [ 19 ] . The proof of this major achievement is based on two nontrivial results, one about doublecross graphs  [ 7 ] , and the other one about apex cubic graphs. (The full proof of the latter case was not published.) 
 
 
 For cubic graphs, having a nowhere-zero  4 4 -flow is equivalent to having a 3-edge-coloring.
Motivated by questions about colorings and flows for graphs on surfaces, there is an ongoing effort to formulate which cubic graphs that can be embedded in a fixed surface are 3-edge-colorable.
This question can be reduced to cyclically 4-edge-connected graphs. 3 3 
               3 
              
              
              
            A graph is  cyclically  k k -edge-connected  if after deleting fewer than  k k  edges from the graph, there is at most one component that contains a cycle. See also Section  2 . 
Cyclically 4-edge-connected cubic graphs with girth of at least 5 that are not 3-edge-colorable are called  snarks .
The most famous snark is the Petersen graph, which was the first snark discovered  [ 16 ] .
Snarks are an interesting and important class of graphs because they often appear as counterexamples to various intriguing conjectures. 
 
 
 A well-known conjecture by Grünbaum from 1968  [ 9 ]  asserts that every cubic graph with a polyhedral embedding (i.e., graphs whose dual triangulation is a simple graph) in an orientable surface is 3-edge-colorable.
This conjecture was disproved by Kochol  [ 12 ] , who found a counterexample of genus 5.
However, whether the conjecture holds for surfaces of smaller Euler genus is still unresolved.
Vodopivec  [ 21 ]  proved that there are infinitely many snarks that can be embedded in the torus.
They are certain dot products of copies of the Petersen graph; each of them contains two edges whose removal yields a planar graph.
It is possible that the provided examples are the only ones, and the conjecture of Grünbaum  [ 9 ]  claims precisely that. 
 
 
 Conjecture 2   (Grünbaum  [ 9 ] , 1968) . 
 
 If  G G  is a  2 2 -connected cubic graph embedded in the torus and  G G  is not  3 3 -edge-colorable, then  G G  contains two edges whose removal gives a planar graph. 
 
 
 
 Returning our attention to the projective plane, Mohar conjectured in 2004 (see  [ 14 ] ) that the only snark that is embeddable on the projective plane is the Petersen graph (Indeed, in the mid-1990, Neil Robertson already considered this question, and he made a conjecture which is similar to Conjecture  2  for any surface, see Problem 5.5.19 in  [ 15 ] ). The main result of this paper verifies this conjecture which is tightly related to Grünbaum’s conjecture for the projective plane. 
 
 
 Theorem 1.2 . 
 
 The only snark embeddable in the projective plane is the Petersen graph.
In other words, a  2 2 -connected cubic graph  G G  that can be embedded in the projective plane is not  3 3 -edge-colorable if and only if  G G  can be obtained from the Petersen graph by replacing each vertex by a  2 2 -connected planar cubic graph. 
 
 
 
 By  replacing a vertex  v v  in a cubic graph  G G  by a planar cubic graph  H H , we mean selecting a vertex  u ∈ V ⁡ ( H ) u\in V(H) , then taking the disjoint union of  G − v G-v  and  H − u H-u  and add three edges joining vertices of degree 2 in  G − v G-v  with those in  H − u H-u .
This operation can be conducted repeatedly and can also involve a similar operation of replacing an edge of  G G  with  H H  (where we join  G − r G-r  and  H − f H-f  for some edge  f ∈ E ⁡ ( H ) f\in E(H) ). It is easy to see that making successive replacements can be done with a single step, so there is no need to mention that. 
 
 
 Theorem  1.2  implies a strengthening of the Tutte 4-Flow Conjecture (Conjecture  1 ). 
 
 
 Corollary 1.3 . 
 
 Every cyclically- 4 4 -edge-connected projective planar graph different from the Petersen graph admits a nowhere-zero  4 4 -flow 4 4 
                   4 
                  
                  
                  
                 For the precise definition of nowhere-zero  4 4 -flow, we refer the reader to the textbook by Diestel  [ 6 ] . . 
 
 
 
 Using Theorem  1.2 , we obtain the following algorithmic outcome. 
 
 
 Theorem 1.4 . 
 
 The following algorithmic question can be solved in quadratic time,  O ⁡ ( n 2 ) O(n^{2}) , where  n = | V ⁡ ( G ) | n=|V(G)| .
 Input : A cubic graph  G G .
 Output : Either a 3-edge-coloring of  G G  or an obstruction showing that  G G  is not 3-edge colorable, or the conclusion that  G G  cannot be embedded in the projective plane (certified by exposing a forbidden minor for embedding in the projective plane that is contained in  G G ). 
 
 
 
 The coloring-flow duality that holds for planar graphs no longer holds for graphs on any other surface. However, an unexpected corollary of our Theorem  1.2  is that there is a similar duality on the projective plane. Namely, the following result is equivalent to Theorem  1.2 . 
 
 
 Theorem 1.5 . 
 
 A cubic graph embedded in the projective plane is  3 3 -edge-colorable if and only if its dual is vertex  5 5 -colorable. 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 Let  G ∗ G^{*}  be the dual graph with respect to an embedding of  G G  in the projective plane.
If  G G  has a bridge,  G ∗ G^{*}  has a loop, hence neither  G ∗ G^{*}  is 5-colorable nor  G G  is 3-edge-colorable. Hence we may assume that  G G  is 2-connected.
It follows by Theorem  1.2  that  G G  is not 3-edge-colorable if and only if  G ∗ G^{*}  contains  K 6 K_{6}  as a subgraph.
(This fact, which is not hard to show and whose verification is left to the reader, is based on the fact that the dual of the Petersen graph is  K 6 K_{6} .)
On the other hand, it is known (see  [ 15 ] ) that the only 6-color-critical graph on the projective plane is  K 6 K_{6} .
∎ 
 
 
 
 The above proof shows that Theorem  1.2  implies Theorem  1.5 .
The reverse implication is even easier, and the proof is omitted. 
 
 
 Theorem  1.5  is indeed a surprising outcome since it was believed that there is no coloring-flow duality for graphs on nonorientable surfaces (see  [ 15 ]  or  [ 5 ] ). 
 
 
 Our Theorem  1.2  yields another result about 4-flows in more general (not necessarily cubic) graphs. It can be considered as a very strong version of the Tutte 4-flow Conjecture. For that effect, we extend the definition of  Petersen-like  graphs to allow vertices of higher degrees. We start with the Petersen graph and then we replace some of its vertices by planar graphs  H H  that need not be 3-regular; we only request that the vertex  u u  of  H H  that is split to connect with 3 edges to the Petersen graph is of degree 3. 
 
 
 Theorem 1.6 . 
 
 A 2-connected graph embedded in the projective plane admits a nowhere-zero  4 4 -flow if and only if it is not Petersen-like. 
 
 
 
 The proof of Theorem  1.6  is given in the appendix. 
 
 
 
 1.2  Sketch of the proof: Technical difficulty over the proof of 4CT 

 
 The basic outline of our proof of Theorem  1.2  is similar to the proof of 4CT  [ 17 ]  and its extension to the doublecross case in  [ 7 ] . A description of these proofs is given in  [ 7 ] , and we omit it here. 
 
 
 On the other hand, there are essential differences between the projective plane case and the planar case.
We list below some of the main issues pertaining to the projective plane. 
 
 
 
 1.  
 
 The projective plane has smaller Euler characteristic, so the total charge used in the discharging part of the proof is smaller. 
 
 2.  
 
 It is much harder to obtain a set of valid discharging rules and reducible configurations. 
 
 3.  
 
 The Petersen graph, a snark, is embeddable in the projective plane. By removing a configuration that is otherwise reducible, one can obtain the Petersen graph or a simple extension of it that is not 3-edge-colorable. 
 
 4.  
 
 Two vertices in a reducible configuration may “wrap around” the crosscap, admitting small representativity. 
 
 5.  
 
 Reducibility of configurations on the projective plane is much harder to achieve. Most of the smaller configurations that are reducible when considering 4-coloring of planar graphs are not reducible for the projective plane. 
 
 6.  
 
 One needs reducible configurations whose diameter is at most five. 
 
 
 
 
 The first issue is regarding the  discharging method , which we discuss in Section  5 .
With a cubic graph  G G  polyhedrally embedded to some surface, one can define the dual graph  G ′ G^{\prime}  of  G G , which is a triangulation 5 5 
               5 
              
              
              
            A  triangulation  is a simple graph on a surface, in which all faces are triangles. .
The average degree of  G ′ G^{\prime}  plays an important role in the discharging method.
Since the Euler characteristic of the projective plane is positive, one can show that the average degree of  G ′ G^{\prime}  is strictly smaller than  6 6 , and the discharging method can still be conducted like in the planar case. 
 
 
 The second issue is far more troublesome than the first one.
In Section  5 , we discuss the reducibility of configurations.
Roughly speaking, configurations are subgraphs in cubic graphs, and a configuration in  G G  is  reducible  if it can be replaced by a smaller one (by deleting edges and suppressing the endpoints of one or more edges) without changing the non-3-edge-colorability of  G G .
There are two important differences from the planar case that make the discovery and treatment of reducible configurations much more difficult.
Firstly, it is much harder to prove that the smaller graph is not 3-edge-colorable (see the discussion below), and secondly, there is a possibility that we obtain a Petersen-like graph (see the definition below) and may thus come from a 3-edge-colorable graph to one that is not 3-edge-colorable. 
 
 
 When checking for reducibility of a configuration, we utilize  Kempe chains , which are sets of disjoint paths connecting the outskirts of the configuration, and all edges on each such path are colored with two colors only.
Exchanging the two colors on such a path gives a new 3-edge-coloring.
Kempe chains are much more versatile on the projective plane than on the plane, and as a result, many configurations that are reducible in the planar case are non-reducible on the projective plane.
Therefore, reducible configurations are much harder to find on the projective plane than on the plane.
For example, consider the first 7 configurations in the 4CT proof.
Planar graphs and doublecross graphs both use these configurations, and this is the key that the same rules can apply to both cases.
For projective planar graphs, however, only the first one out of the seven is reducible and the rest are not.
Even the first configuration is harder to reduce for the projective planar case (see Figure  1 ).
For the planar case, no edge is necessary to delete for reduction, whereas for the projective plane case, we need to delete at least six edges, as shown in Figure  1  (the figure shows the dual graph, which is a triangulation).
Deleting a lot of edges in a configuration is dangerous in general because the graph after the reduction may contain bridges, which do not satisfy the induction hypothesis.
However, we believe that it is not possible to find a proof without using this configuration, so we very carefully construct a workaround for this particular configuration, as shown in Section  7  (see Lemma  7.1 ).
One interesting fact is that we need reducible configurations shown in Figure  2  that contain the first graph as a subgraph (they consist of five vertices that are of degree at most six in the dual graph).
Usually, it does not make sense to add such configurations for reducibility, but in our case, we use these reducible configurations because they are D-reducible (i.e., they do not require contracting any edge).
If they appear, then the reduction would not end up with  K 6 K_{6} .
If they do not appear (but the first graph in Figure  1  appears), we analyze the structure of the ring (and conclude that it is not possible to end up with  K 6 K_{6} ). See Lemma  7.1  for more details. 
 
 
 Figure 1 :  The first reducible configuration that consists of four vertices of degree five in the dual graph. The bold lines represent the contraction (which corresponds to edge-deletion in the cubic graph) used for C-reducibility. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 Figure 2 :  The reducible configurations used in the proof of Lemma  7.1 . 
 
 
 The difficulty of finding reducible configurations for the projective planar case has an impact on the overall size of the set of reducible configurations that we construct.
As in the fifth item listed above, more than 80% of reducible configurations for the doublecross case (hence the planar case) are not reducible for the projective plane, so we need to search for a completely different set of unavoidable reducible configurations.
Our full list of reducible configurations consists of approximately 6000 configurations 6 6 
               6 
              
              
              
            Unfortunately, we are not able to provide pictures of all reducible configurations. They are available on the afore-mentioned Google drive. , which is much more than that for 4CT (633 configurations) or for the doublecross case (765 configurations). 
 
 
 The discharging rules require a major revision as well.
Ultimately, we have constructed a list of 169 discharging rules (vs. the 33 discharging rules for planar/doublecross cases). All of the rules are described in Section  B  in the appendix.
More details are provided in Section  3  and Section  5 . 
 
 
 The third issue arises when trying to reduce a configuration whose reducibility is based on having three or more edges deleted.
In previous proofs, it is easy to see that the reduction of configurations is closed under the graph family of bridgeless cubic graphs.
However, in our proof, the graph family we are interested in is the family of bridgeless cubic projective planar graphs that are not Petersen-like. 7 7 
               7 
              
              
              
            A graph is  Petersen-like  if it can be obtained from a Petersen graph with the operations defined in Theorem  1.2 . 
It is not obvious at all whether the reduction of a reducible configuration is closed under such a graph family when three or more edge-deletions are used, and this is a problem that must be addressed in order to provide a valid induction hypothesis. 
 
 
 We take care of this problem by involving the following two ideas. When we delete 3 or 4 edges, we prove that any type of a reduction is safe by enumerating all possible ways that may give rise to a Petersen-like graph. (See Section  4 .)
The original cubic graph that we want to reduce has relatively large cyclic edge connectivity, so there are only a few ways in which the three or four edges in a non-Petersen-like graph can be deleted such that the resulting graph will be Petersen-like.
We enumerate all possible cases of such reductions and prove that in each case, there is another reducible configuration that either (1) has at most two edge-deletions or (2) results in representativity at most 2.
In both cases, the resulting graph is not Petersen-like. 
 
 
 When we need to delete five or more edges, we prove that the types of reductions of configurations used specifically in this paper are safe.
There are over 250 configurations that require five or more edge deletions, and we use various conditions (such as graph size and lengths of faces of  G G ) to check that none of them yields a Petersen-like graph after reduction. 
 
 
 To give more detail, for each configuration  I ⊆ G I\subseteq G  (and its dual  W W  contained in the dual triangulation  G ′ G^{\prime} ), we use the following process. Let  X ⊆ E ⁡ ( I ) X\subseteq E(I) ,  | X | ≥ 5 |X|\geq 5 , be the set of edges that are deleted in the reduction. 
 
 
 
 1.  
 
 Enumerate all possible  ( ≤ 3 ) (\leq 3) -vertex-separations in  G ′ G^{\prime} , after the edge contractions (that correspond to the edge deletions in  G G ). 
 
 
 We utilize a lemma proved in Section  7 : if  C C  is a contractible cycle of length 6 or 7 in  G ′ G^{\prime}  and the number of vertices strictly contained in the disk  D D  bounded by  C C  is at least 5, we can find another reducible configuration that is contained in  D ∖ C D\setminus C . 
 
 2.  
 
 Using the separations obtained above, delete all those vertices that could be potentially removed by a 2 or 3-vertex separation reduction (that corresponds to a 2 or 3-cut reduction in  G G ). 
 
 3.  
 
 Delete all vertices that are still contained in the “small” side of a 2 or 3-vertex cut that contains at most one vertex of the ring or exactly two vertices that are adjacent in the ring. 
 
 4.  
 
 Check if the resulting graph is of order at least 7, or has a vertex of degree 4 or of degree  ≥ 6 \geq 6 . 
 
 
 If either the graph size check or the vertex degree check is satisfied, we can conclude that  G ′ G^{\prime}  would not become the complete graph  K 6 K_{6} , and by considering the dual, we can confirm that  G G  would not become the Petersen graph  P 10 P_{10} . 
 
 
 
 
 We implement the above algorithm and run computer check for each reducible configuration, and almost all configurations pass the criteria.
For a few cases that do not pass, we provide proof for each individual configuration using additional criteria.
The full proof is shown in Section  9 . 
 
 
 Both, the fourth and the sixth issue, are a challenge when trying to reduce a configuration.
When dealing with reducibility, we must confirm that the outskirt of the configuration is induced, meaning that there should be no edges connecting two vertices of the configuration.
In the planar case, this can be avoided fairly easily: by the induction hypothesis, there is no separating cycle of length at most five in the triangulation (which are cuts in the dual cubic graph), except for one single vertex on one side of the cycle, and every reducible configuration is of diameter 4 or less, meaning there is no such an edge.
In our proof, it is possible that there is a cycle of short length that may be  non-contractible , and some configurations are of diameter 5 (but not 6).
Having such a short non-contractible cycle is troublesome, and we must address this issue separately. 
 
 
 The minimum length of such a cycle is called the  representativity  (also called the  face-width ) of the embedded graph, and we address graphs of representativity up to 5. The proof uses a combination of discharging methods and reducibility checking, which also involve some ideas that were developed in  [ 7 ] .
Configurations of diameter 5 are also a problem, and for every pair of vertices  u , v u,v  at distance 5 in a configuration  W W , we find one of the following in  W + u ​ v W+uv : 
 
 
 
 •  
 
 A contractible cycle of length at most five (which we can exclude by applying Lemma  2.4 ). 
 
 •  
 
 A contractible cycle of length exactly six, with both components of  G ′ − C G^{\prime}-C  having at least 4 vertices (which we can exclude by using Lemma  7.4 ). 
 
 •  
 
 A non-contractible cycle of length at most five, which we address in Section  8 . 
 
 
 
 
 The full case analysis is presented in Section  3.1 . 
 
 
 
 1.3  Some technical results and organization of the paper 

 
 In addition to the discharging method and the reducibility check, which are detailed in Sections  3  and  5 , respectively, we utilize the following technical results. 
 
 
 
 (a)  
 
 Let  C C  be a contractible cycle of length  l l  in  G ′ G^{\prime} , where  l ∈ { 6 , 7 } l\in\{6,7\}  and the disk  D D  bounded by  C C  contains at least  l − 2 l-2  vertices. Then there is a reducible configuration that is contained strictly in the interior of  D D . 
 
 
 This is shown in Sections  6  and  7 . 
 
 (b)  
 
 Let  J J  be a projective-planar subcubic graph with all of its degree-two vertices on a face  F F  of  J J , and assume that  G = J ∪ Q G=J\cup Q , where  Q Q  is a 2-connected subcubic planar graph and is embedded in  F F . Suppose, moreover, that  J J  fits one of the following cases: 
 
 1.  
 
 J J  is obtained from the Petersen graph by deleting a vertex and subdividing some edges on the resulting face  F F  (Section  4 ) by adding  t t  subdivision vertices, where  3 ≤ t ≤ 6 3\leq t\leq 6 , or 
 
 2.  
 
 J J  is obtained from the Petersen graph by deleting a vertex and subdividing some edges on the resulting face  F F  (Section  4 ) by adding  t t  subdivision vertices, where  3 ≤ t ≤ 4 3\leq t\leq 4  and then also adding a new edge into one of the faces distinct from  F F  in such a way that the girth of the resulting subcubic graph  J J  is still at least 5, or 
 
 3.  
 
 J J  is either a subdivision of  V 6 V_{6} ,  V 8 V_{8} , or  V 10 V_{10} , where  V 2 ​ k V_{2k}  is obtained from the  2 ​ k 2k -gonal face  F F  by adding  k k  main diagonals in its outside on the projective plane (Section  8 ). 
 
 
 Then  J J  is reducible if the size of the face  F F  is not too large. (The actual value of what it means being too large will be discussed later.) 
 
 
 
 
 The first result (a) helps a lot for our safety check of reducibility. Indeed, in this case we have another reducible configuration  J ′ J^{\prime}  in the disk  D D  bounded by  C C  in  G G , and we manage to show that by making a reduction using  J ′ J^{\prime} , the resulting graph obtained from  G G  is not Petersen-like. More details will be given in Sections  6  and  7 . This means that if  C C  is a separating cycle of length exactly  l l  for  l = 6 , 7 l=6,7 , we may assume that the component of  G ′ − C G^{\prime}-C  strictly inside the disk bounded by  C C  has at most  l − 3 l-3  vertices. 
 
 
 In a small number of special cases we have to consider contractible cycles of length  l = 8 l=8 . 
 
 
 These results imply that as long as we contract at most four edges for C-reducible configurations in  G G , they are safely contracted (i.e., after contractions,  G ′ G^{\prime}  never ends up with  K 6 K_{6} . In other words,  G G  never ends up being the Petersen graph).
More details are given in Section  4 . 
 
 
 We still have 201 reducible configurations that need contraction of five edges or more.
For each of these configurations, we provide a case-by-case analysis using theorems in Sections  7  and  8  to prove that  G G  does not become a Petersen-like graph after contractions.
While the analysis could theoretically be carried out by hand, it is very repetitive and time-consuming to cover all different cases.
So, for the sake of simplicity, we used computer programs to cover most of the cases.
The algorithm used for the analysis is provided in Appendix  C .
There are six configurations where the program did not produce valid output (that means that we could not confirm that  G G  does not become a Petersen-like graph after contractions).
In these cases, we provide a hand written proof, which we present in Lemma  7.1  and Appendix  G . 
 
 
 In Section  2  we shall introduce the main notation.
Discharging method and the reducibility check are detailed in Sections  3  and  5 , respectively.
Finally, in Section  10 , we give the whole proof of Theorem  1.2  and in Section  11  we outline our quadratic-time algorithm for Theorem  1.4 . The algorithm presented here is actually following the same approach as developed in  [ 18 ] . 
 
 
 Some of details that mostly devote to case analysis (including the arguments for Sections  4  and  9 ) or that devote to some arguments that are already known (including the argument for Section  11 ), are deferred to the appendix.
For programs used in our computer verification, their pseudo codes are included in Section  C . 
 
 
 
 
 2  Minimal counterexamples 

 
 Definition 2.1 . 
 
 Let  G G  be a cubic graph. A  cyclic cut  is a set  F ⊂ E ⁡ ( G ) F\subset E(G)  of edges disconnecting  G G , such that at least two components of  G − F G-F  contain cycles. The graph
is  cyclically  k k -edge-connected  if no cyclic cut of size less than  k k  exists. 
 
 
 
 Let  𝒫 1 \mathcal{P}_{1}  be the set of all simple cubic bridgeless graphs embeddable in the projective plane.
For  G ∈ 𝒫 1 G\in\mathcal{P}_{1} , the replacement of any cyclic 3-edge-cut by a vertex is called a  3-edge-cut reduction .
Similarly, the replacement of any cyclic 2-edge-cut by an edge is called a  2-edge-cut reduction .
These two kinds of reductions are also referred to as  low edge-cut reductions .
Since there are exactly two connected components separated by a 2-edge or 3-edge-cut, there are two possible graphs that are obtained by making a low edge-cut reduction.
The following lemma is easy to prove. 
 
 
 Lemma 2.2 . 
 
 A  low edge-cut reduction  for any  G ∈ 𝒫 1 G\in\mathcal{P}_{1}  yields one planar graph and one graph in  𝒫 1 \mathcal{P}_{1} .
Furthermore, the following statements are equivalent for any  G ∈ 𝒫 1 G\in\mathcal{P}_{1} : 
 
 •  
 
 G G  is  3 3 -edge-colorable. 
 
 •  
 
 For any  G 1 G_{1}  obtained from  G G  by zero or more low edge-cut reductions,  G 1 G_{1}  is 3-edge-colorable. 
 
 
 
 
 
 Let  𝒫 0 \mathcal{P}_{0}  be a set of those graphs  G ∈ 𝒫 1 G\in\mathcal{P}_{1}  that can be reduced to a graph isomorphic to  P 10 P_{10}  after a sequence of low edge-cut reductions. Graphs in  𝒫 0 \mathcal{P}_{0}  are said to be  Petersen-like graphs . 
 
 
 Because  P 10 P_{10}  is not 3-edge-colorable, Lemma  2.2  implies that graphs in  𝒫 0 \mathcal{P}_{0}  are not 3-edge-colorable.
These are the obvious non-3-edge-colorable projective planar cubic graphs, and no other examples are known.
In this paper, we prove that these are the only non-colorable graphs. In other words, any graph in  𝒫 1 ∖ 𝒫 0 \mathcal{P}_{1}\setminus\mathcal{P}_{0}  can be 3-edge-colored. While trying to prove this, we consider a hypothetical counterexample and discuss how it should look like until finally reaching a contradiction. 
 
 
 Definition 2.3 . 
 
 A 2-connected cubic graph  G ∈ 𝒫 1 ∖ 𝒫 0 G\in\mathcal{P}_{1}\setminus\mathcal{P}_{0}  is called a  counterexample  if the graph does not admit a 3-edge-coloring. A counterexample is  minimal  if there are no counterexamples of smaller order in  𝒫 1 ∖ 𝒫 0 \mathcal{P}_{1}\setminus\mathcal{P}_{0} . 
 
 
 
 Our main result, Theorem  1.2 , is equivalent to saying that there are no counterexamples in  𝒫 1 ∖ 𝒫 0 \mathcal{P}_{1}\setminus\mathcal{P}_{0} . 
 
 
 The following lemma, whose proof is given in Section  E  (in the appendix), holds. 
 
 
 Lemma 2.4 . 
 
 A minimal counterexample  G ∈ 𝒫 1 ∖ 𝒫 0 G\in\mathcal{P}_{1}\setminus\mathcal{P}_{0}  is cyclically 5-edge-connected. Furthermore, for any cyclic cut  F F  of size  5 5 , one of the connected components of  G − F G-F  must be a 5-cycle. 
 
 
 
 Let  G G  be a minimal counterexample.
Throughout the paper, we will interchangeably consider the cubic graph  G ∈ 𝒫 1 ∖ 𝒫 0 G\in\mathcal{P}_{1}\setminus\mathcal{P}_{0}  and its dual graph  G ′ G^{\prime}  in our statements and proofs.
Since  G G  is cubic and nonplanar,  G ′ G^{\prime}  is a triangulation on the projective plane. The graph  G ′ G^{\prime}  is loopless since otherwise  G G  would either have a cutedge or have representativity 1, in which case it would be planar.  G ′ G^{\prime}  is simple and 3-connected if  G G  is polyhedrally embedded in the projective plane. 
 
 
 For other definitions and notation used in the paper, we refer to Diestel’s book  [ 6 ]  for basic graph theory and to the monograph  [ 15 ]  for notions in topological graph theory. But there is one thing to mention.
A  cycle  in a graph  G G  is a set of vertices  U ⊆ V ⁡ ( G ) U\subseteq V(G)  where the induced graph  G ⁡ [ V ] G[V]  is a connected subgraph whose all vertices have a degree of 2.
A  circuit  in a graph  G G  is a set of vertices that yield a closed walk. Thus a cycle is a circuit, but the converse is not true. 
 
 
 
 3  Reducible configurations 

 
 In this section, we start by considering the dual graph  G ′ G^{\prime} , which is a triangulation of the projective plane.
Let us first recall basic definitions of configurations and reducibility used in  [ 17 ] .
A  near-triangulation  is a plane graph whose faces are triangles with the exception of the infinite face. 
 
 
 Definition 3.1 . 
 
 A  configuration   K K  consists of a near-triangulation  G ⁡ ( K ) = ( V ⁡ ( K ) , E ⁡ ( K ) ) G(K)=(V(K),E(K)) , and a map  γ K : V ⁡ ( K ) → ℤ + \gamma_{K}\colon V(K)\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{+}  satisfying the following conditions. 
 
 (i)  
 
 For every vertex  v ∈ V ⁡ ( K ) v\in V(K) ,  G ⁡ ( K ) − v G(K)-v  has at most two components, and if there are two, then  γ K ​ ( v ) = deg G ⁡ ( K ) ⁡ ( v ) + 2 \gamma_{K}(v)=\deg_{G(K)}(v)+2 . 
 
 (ii)  
 
 For every vertex  v ∈ V ⁡ ( K ) v\in V(K) , if  v v  is not incident with the infinite face, then  γ K ​ ( v ) = deg G ⁡ ( K ) ⁡ ( v ) \gamma_{K}(v)=\deg_{G(K)}(v) , and otherwise  γ K ​ ( v ) > deg G ⁡ ( K ) ⁡ ( v ) \gamma_{K}(v)>\deg_{G(K)}(v) . In either case,  γ K ​ ( v ) ≥ 5 \gamma_{K}(v)\geq 5 . 
 
 (iii)  
 
 K K  has ring-size  ≥ 2 \geq 2  where  ring-size  is  ∑ v ( γ K ​ ( v ) − deg G ⁡ ( K ) ⁡ ( v ) − 1 ) \sum_{v}(\gamma_{K}(v)-\deg_{G(K)}(v)-1) , and the summation runs over all vertices  v ∈ V ⁡ ( K ) v\in V(K)  that are on the infinite face and for which  G ⁡ ( K ) − v G(K)-v  is connected. 
 
 
 
 
 
 In what follows, we will have many figures showing configurations. In all of them, we will use the Heesch notation  [ 10 ]  designating vertex degrees. To help the reader we collect the vertex-shapes in Figure  3 . 
 
 
 Figure 3 :  Shapes used to designate vertices of specific degrees. 
 
 
 Definition 3.2 . 
 
 Let  G ′ G^{\prime}  be a triangulation of some surface  Σ \Sigma . A configuration  K K   appears  in  G ′ G^{\prime}  if 
 
 (i)  
 
 G ⁡ ( K ) G(K)  is an induced subgraph of  G ′ G^{\prime} . 
 
 (ii)  
 
 Every finite face of  G ⁡ ( K ) G(K)  is also a face of  G ′ G^{\prime} . 
 
 (iii)  
 
 Every vertex  v ∈ V ⁡ ( K ) v\in V(K)  satisfies  γ K ​ ( v ) = deg G ′ ⁡ ( v ) \gamma_{K}(v)=\deg_{G^{\prime}}(v) . 
 
 (iv)  
 
 There is a disk  D ⊂ Σ D\subset\Sigma  containing  G ⁡ ( K ) G(K)  in its interior, and the orientation of each oriented finite face of  G ⁡ ( K ) G(K)  is the same in  D D  (either all are clockwise or all are counterclockwise). 
 
 
 
 
 
 Definition 3.3 . 
 
 Let  K K  be a configuration, and  R = ( V ⁡ ( R ) , E ⁡ ( R ) ) R=(V(R),E(R))  be a cycle whose length is equal to the ring-size of  K K  and is disjoint from  G ⁡ ( K ) G(K) . A near-triangulation  S S  is a  free completion  of K with  ring   R R  if 
 
 (i)  
 
 R R  is a cycle in  S S  that bounds the infinite face of  S S . 
 
 (ii)  
 
 G ⁡ ( K ) G(K)  is a subgraph of  S S , induced on  V ⁡ ( S ) ∖ V ⁡ ( R ) V(S)\setminus V(R) , i.e.  G ⁡ ( K ) = S − V ⁡ ( R ) G(K)=S-V(R) , every finite region of  G ⁡ ( K ) G(K)  is a finite region of  S S , and the infinite region of  G ⁡ ( K ) G(K)  includes  R R  and the infinite region of  S S . 
 
 (iii)  
 
 Every vertex  v v  of  S S  not in  V ⁡ ( R ) V(R)  has degree  γ K ​ ( v ) \gamma_{K}(v)  in S. 
 
 
 
 
 
 Definition 3.4 . 
 
 An  island   I I  is a plane graph with the following properties. 
 
 (i)  
 
 I I  is 2-connected. 
 
 (ii)  
 
 Every vertex of  I I  has degree two or three. 
 
 (iii)  
 
 Every vertex of degree two is incident with the infinite region. 
 
 
 An island  appears  in a cubic graph  G ∈ 𝒫 1 G\in\mathcal{P}_{1}  if  I I  is a subgraph of  G G , and every finite face of  I I  is also a face of  G G . 
 
 
 
 For any configuration  K K , the inner dual 8 8 
             8 
            
            
            
          The  inner dual  is obtained from the dual graph by deleting the vertex that corresponds to the infinite face.  of the free completion of  K K  is an island. This island is called the  island of  K K  and will be denoted by  I ⁡ ( K ) I(K) . 
 
 
 Definition 3.5 . 
 
 Let  k k  be a positive integer.
A  match  is an unordered pair  { a , b } \{a,b\}  of distinct integers in  { 1 , … , k } \{1,\dots,k\} .
Two matches  { a , b } \{a,b\}  and  { c , d } \{c,d\}  with  1 ≤ a < c < b < d ≤ k 1\leq a<c<b<d\leq k  are said to  overlap .
A  signed match  is a pair  ( m , μ ) (m,\mu)  where  m m  is a match and  μ = ± 1 \mu=\pm 1 . 
 
 
 
 Definition 3.6 . 
 
 A  signed matching  is a set  M M  of signed matches with the following properties. 
 
 (i)  
 
 Any two distinct signed matches  ( { a 1 , b 1 } , μ 1 ) , ( { a 2 , b 2 } , μ 2 ) ∈ M (\{a_{1},b_{1}\},\mu_{1}),(\{a_{2},b_{2}\},\mu_{2})\in M  satisfy  { a 1 , b 1 } ∩ { a 2 , b 2 } = ∅ \{a_{1},b_{1}\}\cap\{a_{2},b_{2}\}=\varnothing . 
 
 (ii)  
 
 No two matches in  M M  overlap. 
 
 
 
 
 
 Definition 3.7 . 
 
 A  projective signed matching  is a set  M M  of signed matches with the following properties. 
 
 (i)  
 
 Any two distinct signed matches  ( { a 1 , b 1 } , μ 1 ) , ( { a 2 , b 2 } , μ 2 ) ∈ M (\{a_{1},b_{1}\},\mu_{1}),(\{a_{2},b_{2}\},\mu_{2})\in M  satisfy  { a 1 , b 1 } ∩ { a 2 , b 2 } = ∅ \{a_{1},b_{1}\}\cap\{a_{2},b_{2}\}=\varnothing . 
 
 (ii)  
 
 The set of matches  A = { m ∣ ( m , μ ) ∈ M ​  for some  ​ μ ∈ ± 1 } A=\{m\mid(m,\mu)\in M\hbox{ for some }\mu\in\pm 1\}  can be partitioned into  A = A 0 ∪ A 1 A=A_{0}\cup A_{1}  such that the matches in  A 0 A_{0}  do not overlap with any other match in  A A , and any two matches in  A 1 A_{1}  overlap with each other. 
 
 
 If  M M  is a (projective) signed matching, we let  E ⁡ ( M ) E(M)  denote  { e ∣ 1 ≤ e ≤ k , e ∈ m \{e\mid 1\leq e\leq k,e\in m  for some  ( m , μ ) ∈ M } (m,\mu)\in M\} . 
 
 
 
 Let us consider a map  κ : { 1 , … , k } → { 0 , 1 , 2 } \kappa:\{1,\dots,k\}\rightarrow\{0,1,2\} . Since such maps will be related to 3-edge-colorings, we will say that  κ \kappa  is a  coloring . The coloring  κ \kappa  satisfies  the parity condition  if
 | κ − 1 ​ ( 0 ) | ≡ | κ − 1 ​ ( 1 ) | ≡ | κ − 1 ​ ( 2 ) | ( mod 2 ) |\kappa^{-1}(0)|\equiv|\kappa^{-1}(1)|\equiv|\kappa^{-1}(2)|\pmod{2} . We will only consider colorings that satisfy the parity condition since every edge-coloring of edges in a cut satisfies the parity condition. 
 
 
 Definition 3.8 . 
 
 Let  κ : { 1 , … , k } → { 0 , 1 , 2 } \kappa:\{1,\dots,k\}\rightarrow\{0,1,2\}  be a coloring that satisfies parity. For  θ ∈ { 0 , 1 , 2 } \theta\in\{0,1,2\} ,  κ \kappa  is said to  θ \theta -fit  a projective signed matching  M M  if 
 
 (i)  
 
 E ( M ) = { e ∣ 1 ≤ e ≤ k , κ ( e ) ≠ θ } E(M)=\{e\mid 1\leq e\leq k,\kappa(e)\neq\theta\} 
 
 (ii)  
 
 for each  ( { e , f } , μ ) ∈ M (\{e,f\},\mu)\in M ,  κ ⁡ ( e ) = κ ⁡ ( f ) \kappa(e)=\kappa(f)  if and only if  μ = 1 \mu=1 . 
 
 
 
 
 
 Definition 3.9 . 
 
 A set  𝒞 \mathscr{C}  of colorings  { 1 , … , k } → { 0 , 1 , 2 } \{1,\dots,k\}\rightarrow\{0,1,2\}  that satisfy parity is  consistent  if for every  κ ∈ 𝒞 \kappa\in\mathscr{C}  and every  θ ∈ { 0 , 1 , 2 } \theta\in\{0,1,2\}  there is a projective signed matching  M M  such that  κ \kappa   θ \theta -fits  M M , and  𝒞 \mathscr{C}  contains every coloring that  θ \theta -fits  M M . 
 
 
 
 Definition 3.10 . 
 
 Let  I I  be an island. We add a new incident edge to each vertex of degree 2 in  I I  and denote the set of all added edges by  R R .
Let  𝒞 ∗ \mathscr{C}^{\ast}  be the set of all colorings  { 1 , … , | R | } → { 0 , 1 , 2 } \{1,\dots,|R|\}\rightarrow\{0,1,2\}  that satisfy parity.
Let  𝒞 0 \mathscr{C_{0}}  be the set of all  3 3 -edge-colorings of  R R  that are restrictions of  3 3 -edge-colorings of  I ∪ R I\cup R .
Let  𝒞 ′ \mathscr{C}^{\prime}  be the maximal consistent subset of  𝒞 ∗ − 𝒞 0 \mathscr{C}^{\ast}-\mathscr{C_{0}} .
The island  I I  is said to be  D-reducible  if  𝒞 ′ = ∅ \mathscr{C}^{\prime}=\emptyset . We call a configuration  K K   D-reducible  if the corresponding island  I ⁡ ( K ) I(K)  is D-reducible. 
 
 
 
 Definition 3.11 . 
 
 Let  G G  be a subcubic graph, and  F F  be a set of edges in  G G  satisfying the following conditions: 
 
 •  
 
 The endpoints of  f ∈ F f\in F  have degree 3. 
 
 •  
 
 For each  v ∈ V ⁡ ( G ) v\in V(G) , the number of edges adjacent to  v v  in  F F  is not  2 2 . 
 
 
 Let  G ∸ F G\dotdiv F  be the subcubic graph obtained from  G G  by deleting the edges in  F F  and suppressing its endpoints if possible. 
 
 
 
 Note that if  G G  is a cubic graph,  G ∸ F G\dotdiv F  is also a cubic graph. 
 
 
 Definition 3.12 . 
 
 Let  I I  be an island and let  X ⊆ E ⁡ ( I ) X\subseteq E(I)  be such that none of the vertices in  I I  is incident with exactly two edges of  X X .
Let  I ′ = I ∸ X I^{\prime}=I\dotdiv X  .
Let  𝒟 \mathscr{D}  be all restrictions to  R R  of  3 3 -edge-colorings of  I ′ ∪ R I^{\prime}\cup R .
 I I  is  C-reducible  if
 𝒟 ∩ 𝒞 ′ = ∅ \mathscr{D}\cap\mathscr{C}^{\prime}=\emptyset .
The edges in  X X  are called  contraction edges 9 9 
                 9 
                
                
                
               Although the edges in  X X  are not contracted, but rather deleted in  I I , we use the term “contraction” following previous work.
This is because the term originated from definitions using the near-triangulation  K K  rather than the cubic dual  I I .  of  I I , and  X X  is denoted by  c ⁡ ( I ) c(I) .
The size of  X X  is called the  contraction size  of  I I . 
 
 
 We extend these terms to configurations  K K , whose island  I ⁡ ( K ) I(K)  is reducible. We call a configuration  K K   C-reducible  if  I ⁡ ( K ) = I I(K)=I  is C-reducible, we call the edges of  K K  corresponding to the edges in  c ⁡ ( I ) c(I)  the  contraction edges , we denote them by  c ⁡ ( K ) c(K) , and we refer to their number  | c ⁡ ( K ) | |c(K)|  as the  contraction size  of  K K . 
 
 
 
 Let  F F  be a set of edges in  G ′ G^{\prime} . We denote by  G ′ / F G^{\prime}/F  the graph obtained from  G ′ G^{\prime}  by contracting  F F . Suppose that  G G  is embedded in some surface. A separating cycle  C C  of length 2, 3 in  G ′ / F G^{\prime}/F  separates  G ′ / F G^{\prime}/F  into a planar graph  H ′ H^{\prime}  and a non-planar one if the representativity of the embedding of  G ′ / F G^{\prime}/F  is at least 3. We consider the graph and its embedding obtained from  G ′ / F G^{\prime}/F  by deleting  H ′ H^{\prime}  from  G ′ / F G^{\prime}/F . We call this operation a  low-(vertex)-cut reduction . This operation corresponds to a low-edge-cut reduction in the original graph  G G . The symbol  ⟨ G ′ / F ⟩ \langle G^{\prime}/F\rangle  denotes the graph obtained from  G ′ / F G^{\prime}/F  by repeatedly applying low-vertex cut reductions to  G ′ / F G^{\prime}/F  until no 2,3-vertex cut exists. 
 
 
 The definition of reducibility here is harder to satisfy than the definitions of reducibility in the original 4CT paper due to the large amount of projective signed matches exclusively in the projective plane.
We refer to the reducibility defined by using only planar signed matches (i.e. the reducibility used in the original 4CT proof) as the  reducibility on the plane . 
 
 
 Let  I I  be a reducible island appearing in some  G G .
By definition of reducibility, if we can 3-edge color  G ∸ c ⁡ ( I ) G\dotdiv c(I) , we can extend it to a 3-edge-coloring of  G G .
Thus, contracting the edges in  c ⁡ ( I ) c(I)  will not affect the non-3-edge colorability of a graph  G G  in which  I I  appears.
This implies the following lemma. 
 
 
 Lemma 3.13 . 
 
 Let  G G  be a minimal counterexample that is embedded in the projective plane  Σ \Sigma .
Suppose that  C C  is a circuit of  G G  bounding a closed disk  Δ \Delta  such that the subgraph of  G G  formed by the vertices and edges drawn in  Δ \Delta  is a reducible island  I I . Then  G ∸ c ⁡ ( I ) G\dotdiv c(I)  either has a bridge or belongs to  𝒫 0 \mathcal{P}_{0} . 
 
 
 
 Now, we introduce a set of reducible configurations  𝒦 \mathcal{K}  which we have constructed by hand and verified each of its reducibility by computer programs.
There are approximately 5000 reducible configurations in  𝒦 \mathcal{K}  and the reader can find all reducible configurations (including contraction edges for  C C -reducible configurations) on the aforementioned Google Drive.
Together with Lemma  3.13 , let us state a lemma here. 
 
 
 Lemma 3.14 . 
 
 Let  G G  be a minimal counterexample that is embedded in the projective plane  Σ \Sigma .
Suppose that  C C  is a circuit of  G G  bounding a closed disk  Δ \Delta  such that the subgraph of  G G  formed by the vertices and edges drawn in  Δ \Delta  is an island  I I . Then either 
 
 1.  
 
 I I  is not in  𝒦 \mathcal{K} , or 
 
 2.  
 
 I I  is in  𝒦 \mathcal{K} , but  G ∸ c ⁡ ( I ) G\dotdiv c(I)  either has a bridge or belongs to  𝒫 0 \mathcal{P}_{0} . 
 
 
 
 
 
 In fact, the set  𝒦 \mathcal{K}  is an unavoidable set of reducible configurations on the projective plane. We prove unavoidability by the discharging method later in this paper, see Lemma  5.5 . 
 
 
 We have to check that for every single  C C -reducible configuration in  𝒦 \mathcal{K} , it is safe to make a reduction, i.e., we have to make sure that the resulting graph is not in  𝒫 0 \mathcal{P}_{0}  and that it is bridgeless.
As pointed out above, this is almost trivial for the planar case and the doublecross case (because at most four edges are necessary to contract for every single  C C -reducible configuration) but in our case, we need a lot of nontrivial work.
The problem is that the following may happen, which would not happen in the proof of the 4CT. 
 
 
 
 •  
 
 There are 201 configurations that need to have at least five edges contracted.
Indeed, there are  118 , 71 , 5 , 7 118,71,5,7  configurations where the contraction edge size is 5, 6, 7, and 8, respectively. See Table  1 . 
 
 •  
 
 There are around 1000 configurations that contain pairs of vertices at a distance of exactly five (although none of our configurations contain pairs of vertices at distance six or more). 
 
 
 
 
 Table 1 :  The number of configurations that need the contraction size  k k   ( 0 ≤ k ≤ 8 ) (0\leq k\leq 8)  in  𝒦 \mathcal{K} .

 Addressing these two issues makes our proof much harder than that of the 4CT and that of the doublecross case.
We will deal with these difficulties in the following sections. 
 
 
 3.1  Distance five in reducible configurations 

 
 In the proof of the 4CT in  [ 17 ] , there are 633 reducible configurations, and all of them are of diameter at most four.
On the other hand, in our set of reducible configurations  𝒦 \mathcal{K} , there are many configurations whose diameter is five. 
 
 
 If a reducible configuration  K K  in a planar graph is of diameter at most four,  K K  is an induced subgraph (otherwise, there would be a separating cycle of order at most five with both sides having at least two vertices, which would not exist in a minimal counterexample).
This is also the case for doublecross graphs.
However, in the projective planar case, we have to deal with the case when a reducible configuration is not induced. 
 
 
 As before, let  G ′ G^{\prime}  be a triangulation that is embedded in the projective plane, and  K K  be a reducible configuration in  G ′ G^{\prime} .
Let us suppose that there are two vertices  u , v ∈ V ⁡ ( K ) u,v\in V(K)  that are at distance five in  K K , and there is an edge  u ​ v ∈ E ⁡ ( G ′ ) uv\in E(G^{\prime}) .
Then we obtain a cycle  C C  of length exactly six, and have one of the following possibilities: 
 
 
 
 (C1)  
 
 C C  is a contractible cycle in  G ′ G^{\prime} .
In the original cubic graph  G G ,  C C  corresponds to a 6-cut, which yields a contractible curve of order exactly six that crosses all edges in the 6-cut. 
 
 (C2)  
 
 C C  is a noncontracible cycle in  G ′ G^{\prime} .
In  G G ,  C C  yields a noncontractible curve crossing exactly six edges dual to  E ⁡ ( C ) E(C) . 
 
 
 
 
 Using Lemma  2.4  and Lemma  7.4 , which will be proved in Section  7 , we get the following fact. 
 
 
 Fact 3.15 . 
 
 If  G ′ G^{\prime}  is a minimal counterexample, then  G ′ G^{\prime}  contains no cycles with any of the following properties: 
 
 •  
 
 A contractible cycle of length at most four, that divides  G ′ G^{\prime}  into two nonempty components. 
 
 •  
 
 A contractible cycle of length five, that divides  G ′ G^{\prime}  into two components, each of order at least two. 
 
 •  
 
 A contractible cycle of length six, that divides  G ′ G^{\prime}  into two components, each of order at least four. 
 
 
 
 
 
 To handle the two above cases (C1) and (C2), we show the following. 
 
 
 Lemma 3.16 . 
 
 Let  C C  be a cycle in  G ′ G^{\prime}  of length  6 6  as in (C1) and (C2). 
 
 1.  
 
 If  C C  is contractible, it divides  G ′ G^{\prime}  into two components, each of order at least four, and thus  G ′ G^{\prime}  is not a minimal counterexample. 
 
 2.  
 
 If  C C  is noncontractible, there is a noncontractible cycle of length at most five in  G ′ G^{\prime} . That cycle corresponds to a non-contractible curve hitting at most five edges of the cubic dual graph  G G . 
 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 This proof depends on computer-check. In Section  C.2 , we disclose the pseudo-code used to prove the lemma.
By computer-check, there are around 1000 pairs that are at distance exactly five in reducible configurations in  𝒦 \mathcal{K} .
Let  u , v u,v  be the two vertices that are at distance exactly five in a reducible configuration  K K . Let  S S  be a free completion of  K K  with its ring  R R . We now compare the free completion  S S  and its immersion in  G ′ G^{\prime} . 
 
 
 Let the two vertices that constitute a triangle face with  u u  and  v v  in  G ′ G^{\prime}  be  x , y x,y . These vertices exist since  G ′ G^{\prime}  is a triangulation.
In both contractible and non-contractible cases, a vertex of  R R  that is adjacent to  u u  in  S S  corresponds to  v v  (the same thing holds if the roles of  u u  and  v v  are swapped) by assuming that an edge  u ​ v uv  exists, which is denoted by  v ′ v^{\prime}  ( u ′ u^{\prime} , resp.). There are two vertices that correspond to  x x  in  S S , one is adjacent to  u u , and the other is adjacent to  v v . The same thing holds for  y y . The symbols  x u , x v , y u , y v x_{u},x_{v},y_{u},y_{v}  denote a suitable vertex in  S S  described above, e.g.  x u x_{u}  is a vertex of  S S  that corresponds to  x x  and is adjacent to  u u . By these correspondences, other correspondences can be obtained. For example, if a vertex that constitutes a triangle face with  x u x_{u}  and  u u , but different from  v ′ v^{\prime}  exists, and a vertex that constitutes a triangle face with  x v x_{v}  and  u ′ u^{\prime} , but different from  v v  exists in  S S , these two vertices correspond. We calculate these correspondences in the vicinity of  x x  and  y y . 
 
 
 First, we explain the contractible case (C1), when a path of length five between  u u  and  v v , and the edge  u ​ v uv  constitute a contractible cycle  C C  of length six.
We check whether  C C  is a cycle that contradicts Fact  3.15 . To do so, we calculate the number of vertices of the two components separated by  C C . The cycle  C C  corresponds to the path that consists of a path between  u u  and  v v  and two edges  u ​ v ′ uv^{\prime} ,  v ​ u ′ vu^{\prime}  in  S S . We calculate the number of vertices of the two components of  S S  divided by this path. We need to pay attention when counting the number of vertices since  R R  may not be a cycle but a circuit and a vertex of  R R  may correspond to a vertex of  K K . Hence, only counting the number of vertices may cause some overcounting. We need to be careful that double-counting does not happen. The specific counting scheme is explained in the pseudo-code in the Appendix, see Section  C.2 . 
 
 
 Let us consider a cycle that is obtained by finding a path between  x u x_{u}  and  x v x_{v}  in  S S . It is contractible since  C C  is contractible and we know that the graph embedded between  C C  and the cycle is an annulus. We check whether this cycle contradicts Fact  3.15  by using the same method as  C C . We do the same thing about  y y . 
 
 
 As we explained before, we calculated the correspondance of vertices in  S S  in the vicinity of  x x  and  y y . The correspondence of the two different vertices of  K K  in  S S  means that these two different vertices of  K K  are same. This contradicts Fact  3.15  since the distance between them is at most five. 
 
 
 In almost all of the cases, we conclude that the cycle would contradict Fact  3.15 . There are, however, eight cases that we cannot conclude immediately. We show them in Figure  4 . For these eight cases, we actually confirm that there are separating 4-cycles, which would still contradict Fact  3.15 . 
 
 
 Second, we explain the non-contractible case. A path of length five between  u u  and  v v , and an edge  u ​ v uv  constitutes a non-contractible cycle  C C  of length six. We find other cycles by obtaining a path between corresponding vertices in  S S  in a similar way to the contractible case. This cycle is non-contractible since it intersects the non-contractible cycle  C C  only once. We check whether or not the length of these cycles obtained above is at most five. If this is the case, this is a desired cycle. 
 
 
 It turns out that all of the configurations would yield a non-contractible cycle of length at most five by this computer check. 
 
 
 ∎ 
 
 
 
 Figure 4 :  The eight cases that remain after computer-check in the proof of Lemma  3.16 . The dotted line represents edges when we assume two vertices of distance five are adjacent. 
 
 
 Therefore, for the first case, there is a contractible cycle of length at most six in  G ′ G^{\prime}  that would contradict Fact  3.15  (i.e., a  ≤ 6 \leq 6 -cut with one side embedded in the disk). 
 
 
 In the second case, we reduce to the case when the representativity of  G G  in  Σ \Sigma  is at most five (we shall handle in Section  8 ). As a corollary of Lemma  3.14 , we have: 
 
 
 Corollary 3.17 . 
 
 Let  G G  be a minimal counterexample that is embedded in the projective plane  Σ \Sigma . If the representativity of  G G  in  Σ \Sigma  is at least six, then none of our reducible configurations in  𝒦 \mathcal{K}  is a subgraph in  G G . 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 Assume for a contradiction that one of the reducible configurations  I ⁡ ( K ) I(K)  is a subgraph in  G ′ G^{\prime} .
We claim that  I ⁡ ( K ) I(K)  is an induced subgraph of the dual graph  G ′ G^{\prime}  (and hence  I ⁡ ( K ) I(K)  appears in  G ′ G^{\prime} ).
To this end, if the representativity of  G ′ G^{\prime}  (and  G G ) in  Σ \Sigma  is at least six this holds because  I ⁡ ( K ) I(K)  is of diameter at most five in  G ′ G^{\prime}  and hence  I ⁡ ( K ) I(K)  is an induced subgraph
by Lemma  3.16 . 
 
 
 Hence  I ⁡ ( K ) I(K)  is induced and thus  K K  appears in  G G  which is a contradiction by Lemma  3.14 .
∎ 
 
 
 
 
 3.2  Bigger implies smaller 

 
 In Section  D  in the appendix, we prove that the reducibility of a configuration implies the reducibility of another configuration. We use Theorem  D.2  in the appendix by reducing the number of reducible configurations in  𝒦 \mathcal{K} . We prove the following claim. 
 
 
 Claim 3.18 . 
 
 Let  K K  be a configuration in  𝒦 \mathcal{K}  with no cut vertices.
Let  V 0 V_{0}  be a set of vertices of  G ⁡ ( K ) G(K)  such that  γ K ​ ( v ) = d G ⁡ ( K ) ​ ( v ) + 3 \gamma_{K}(v)=d_{G(K)}(v)+3 .
For each  S ⊆ V 0 S\subseteq V_{0} , let  K S K_{S}  be the configuration obtained from  K K  by replacing  γ K ​ ( v ) \gamma_{K}(v)  by  γ K S ​ ( v ) = γ K ​ ( v ) − 1 \gamma_{K_{S}}(v)=\gamma_{K}(v)-1  for each  v ∈ S v\in S . Then  K S K_{S}  can be safely reduced. 10 10 
                   10 
                  
                  
                  
                 We say that  K S K_{S}  is  safely reducible  if it is reducible, and in addition, even when it is C-reducible, contractions of edges would not end up with  K 6 K_{6}  after 2-3-vertex cut reductions of the resulting graph. 
 
 
 
 In Claim  3.18 , we add the condition that  K K  has no cut vertices simply because we did not use it for computer-check of discharging. 
 
 
 As the proof of this claim requires many of the facts proved in this paper, we give it in the appendix (see Subsection  D.1 ). 
 
 
 
 
 4  Reducible configurations with contraction set of size  ≤ 4 \leq 4 

 
 Let  G G  be a minimal counterexample, and let  G ′ G^{\prime}  be the dual of  G G , which is a triangulation of the projective plane.
If a C-reducible configuration appears in any non-3-edge-colorable graph, we can delete some set of edges to obtain a smaller non-3-edge-colorable graph.
Deletions in  G G  (which are contractions in  G ′ G^{\prime} ) result in a smaller graph, but we still need to check whether the deleted graph satisfies the induction hypothesis in order to confirm that it is still a counterexample.
For planar graphs, the induction hypothesis is satisfied if the reduced graph is bridgeless.
This is almost trivial since no reducible configuration needs more than  4 4  edge contractions, and internal 6-connectivity holds for minimal counterexamples. 
 
 
 However, we need additional arguments for the projective planar case.
In addition to the request that the resulting cubic graph must be bridgeless, it should not be a member of  𝒫 0 \mathcal{P}_{0} .
(Let us recall that the definition of “minimal counterexample” is the smallest non-three-edge-colorable graph in  𝒫 1 ∖ 𝒫 0 \mathcal{P}_{1}\setminus\mathcal{P}_{0} , so reducing the graph to a member of  𝒫 0 \mathcal{P}_{0}  does not satisfy the induction hypothesis.) 
 
 
 It is easy to show that any configuration of contraction size at most  2 2  meets the requirements after reduction.
From Lemma  2.4 , the following condition holds. 
 
 
 
 •  
 
 G G  contains no cyclic edge-cut of size 4 or less. 
 
 •  
 
 For any cyclic edge-cut  F F  of size 5, one of the connected components of  G − F G-F  must be a 5-cycle. 
 
 
 If  G ∸ e ∈ 𝒫 0 G\dotdiv e\in\mathcal{P}_{0}  for any  e ∈ E ⁡ ( G ) e\in E(G) ,  G ∸ e = P 10 G\dotdiv e=P_{10} .
Similarly, if  G ∸ e ∸ f ∈ 𝒫 0 G\dotdiv e\dotdiv f\in\mathcal{P}_{0}  for any  e , f ∈ E ⁡ ( G ) e,f\in E(G) ,  G ∸ e ∸ f G\dotdiv e\dotdiv f  is either  P 10 P_{10}  or a snark of order 12 obtained by replacing a vertex in  P 10 P_{10}  with a triangle.
Such a graph can be enumerated quite easily (the largest one is a 16-vertex cubic graph), and it can be shown that every one of them can be 3-edge-colored. 
 
 
 On the other hand, there are infinitely many cubic graphs that can be reduced to a graph in  𝒫 0 \mathcal{P}_{0}  after three or more edge deletions (and endpoint suppressions), making it impossible to enumerate all possible cubic graphs, as done in the above case.
Therefore, we need to do some case analysis.
Before that, let us define some new terms. 
 
 
 Definition 4.1 . 
 
 A  projective island   I I  is a projective planar graph with the following properties. 
 
 (i)  
 
 I I  is 2-connected. 
 
 (ii)  
 
 Every vertex of  I I  has degree two or three. 
 
 (iii)  
 
 Every vertex of degree two is incident to a face that is homeomorphic to a 2-dimensional disk. 
 
 
 
 
 
 Regarding projective islands, we define the terms  appear  and being  reducible  in the same way as for islands defined in Section  3 . 
 
 
 Next, we define some special projective islands. 
 
 
 Definition 4.2 . 
 
 For every  y ≥ 3 y\geq 3 , let  V 2 ​ y V_{2y}  be the cubic graph obtained by adding all main diagonal edges to the cycle  C 2 ​ y C_{2y} .
Let the set of the added  y y  edges be denoted by  E cross E_{\mathrm{cross}} .
Let  Γ y k \Gamma_{y}^{k}  be the set of projective islands obtained from  V 2 ​ y V_{2y}  by subdividing some edges on the cycle  C 2 ​ y C_{2y}  with  k k  new vertices of degree  2 2 .
Let  Π y k \Pi_{y}^{k}  be the subset of projective islands in  Γ y k \Gamma_{y}^{k}  which satisfy the two conditions below: 
 
 1.  
 
 For every pair of opposite edges  e , f ∈ C 2 ​ y e,f\in C_{2y} , at least one of  e , f e,f  has been subdivided. 
 
 2.  
 
 Each path in  C 2 ​ y C_{2y}  of length  s ≤ y − 1 s\leq y-1  joining two vertices on this cycle, contains at least  s − 1 s-1  subdivision vertices. 
 
 
 
 
 
 Suppose that we want to remove a set  E 3 E_{3}  of three edges from  G G . We would like to understand when  G ∸ E 3 G\dotdiv E_{3}  is Petersen-like.
We consider the following possibilities. 
 
 
 
 1.  
 
 When  E 3 E_{3}  is contained in a cyclic 6-edge-cut  F F  of  G G :  The 6-edge-cut will become a 3-edge-cut in  G ∸ E 3 G\dotdiv E_{3} .
Then, one of the connected components of  G − F G-F  must be isomorphic to a graph in  Π 3 6 \Pi_{3}^{6} . 
 
 2.  
 
 When 2 or 3 edges of  E 3 E_{3}  are contained in a cyclic 5-edge-cut  F F  of  G G :  The 5-edge-cut will become a 2- or 3-edge-cut in  G ∸ E 3 G\dotdiv E_{3} .
However, since one of the connected components of  G − F G-F  must be a 5-cycle, the order of  G G  can be upper-bounded. More precisely,  | G | ≤ 18 |G|\leq 18  holds. 
 
 3.  
 
 Otherwise:   G ∸ E 3 G\dotdiv E_{3}  must be isomorphic to  P 10 P_{10} , so  | G | ≤ 16 |G|\leq 16  holds. 
 
 
 
 
 Cases 2 and 3 create an upper bound for  | G | |G| , but for case 1, we need to use the reducibility checker for projective islands (in this case, we only require reducibility on the plane).
There are 14 unique graphs in  Π 3 6 \Pi_{3}^{6} , all of which are either D-reducible or C-reducible with contraction size two or less.
Therefore, whenever a C-reducible configuration  I I  of contraction size three occurs in  G G  and all edges in  c ⁡ ( I ) c(I)  are contained in a particular cyclic 6-edge-cut of  G G , we can use the corresponding reducible projective island  I ′ ∈ Π 3 6 I^{\prime}\in\Pi_{3}^{6}  instead.
(Note that this argument holds for projective islands as well.) 
 
 
 This way, we can ensure that, even after the reduction, the induction hypothesis holds. 
 
 
 Therefore, the following holds. 
 
 
 Lemma 4.3 . 
 
 Any planar or projective island  I I  that is C-reducible with contraction size at most three can be safely reduced. 
 
 
 
 We can also do a case analysis for contraction size 4 in the same manner.
Next, let us define some graph families. 
 
 
 Definition 4.4 . 
 
 Let  P 10 − P_{10}^{-}  be the graph obtained by deleting an edge from  P 10 P_{10} .
Let  C C  be a cycle of length  8 8  in  P 10 − P_{10}^{-}  including the two vertices of degree two.
(There are two such cycles  C C  in the same graph, but they are equivalent through graph automorphism.)
Let  Δ 6 \Delta^{6}  be the set of projective islands that is obtained by subdividing some edges in the cycle  C C  with  4 4  new vertices of degree two.
Since there are two vertices of degree two in the original graph  P 10 − P_{10}^{-} , the constructed island’s ring size is six. 
 
 
 Let  Π ^ 3 6 \hat{\Pi}_{3}^{6}  be a set of graphs  G G  that can be constructed by the following method: 
 
 •  
 
 Pick a projective island  H ∈ Π 3 6 H\in\Pi_{3}^{6} . 
 
 •  
 
 Choose two edges  e , f ∈ E ⁡ ( H ) e,f\in E(H)  sharing the same face but having no common endpoint. 
 
 •  
 
 Subdivide  e e  and  f f . Let the newly formed vertex be  v e v_{e}  and  v f v_{f} , respectively. 
 
 •  
 
 Connect  v e v_{e}  and  v f v_{f}  in  H H  to obtain  G G .
Since  e e  and  f f  share the same face, the resulting graph  G G  is still embeddable in the projective plane. 
 
 
 
 
 
 Now, let  E 4 E_{4}  be the set of four edges we want to delete from  G G .
Let us assume that no cyclic 5-cut  F F  with  E 4 ⊂ F E_{4}\subset F  exists.
(This assumption is to avoid making a bridge in  G − E 4 G-E_{4} .)
Again we would like to understand when  G ∸ E 4 G\dotdiv E_{4}  is Petersen-like. 
 
 
 
 1.  
 
 When  E 4 E_{4}  is contained in a cyclic 7-edge-cut  F F  of  G G :  The 7-edge-cut will become a 3-edge-cut in  G ∸ E 3 G\dotdiv E_{3} .
Then, one of the connected components of  G − F G-F  must be isomorphic to a graph in  Π 3 7 \Pi_{3}^{7} . 
 
 2.  
 
 When  E 4 E_{4}  is contained in a cyclic 6-edge-cut  F F  of  G G :  The 6-edge-cut will become a 2-edge-cut in  G ∸ E 3 G\dotdiv E_{3} .
Then, one of the connected components of  G − F G-F  must be isomorphic to a graph in  Δ 6 \Delta^{6} . 
 
 3.  
 
 When  E 4 E_{4}  is contained in a cyclic 5-edge-cut  F F  of  G G :  This would not happen by our assumption. 
 
 4.  
 
 When three edges of  E 4 E_{4}  are contained in a cyclic 6-edge-cut  F F  of  G G : 
The 6-edge-cut will become a 3-edge-cut in  G ∸ E 3 G\dotdiv E_{3} , and there is another edge in  E 4 E_{4}  somewhere else.
Then, one of the connected components of  G − F G-F  must be either isomorphic to a graph in  Π 3 6 \Pi_{3}^{6}  or  Π ^ 3 6 \hat{\Pi}_{3}^{6} . 
 
 5.  
 
 When two or three edges of  E 4 E_{4}  are contained in a cyclic 5-edge-cut  F F  of  G G : 
Because of Lemma  2.4 , the size of  G G  can be upper-bounded. Precisely,  | G | ≤ 20 |G|\leq 20  holds. 
 
 6.  
 
 Otherwise:   G − E 4 G-E_{4}  must be isomorphic to  P 10 P_{10} , so  | G | ≤ 18 |G|\leq 18  holds. 
 
 
 
 
 Therefore, all we need to check is the reducibility of graphs in  Π 3 7 \Pi_{3}^{7} ,  Δ 6 \Delta^{6} , and  Π ^ 3 6 \hat{\Pi}_{3}^{6} .
All projective islands in these three families are either D or C-reducible.
There are, however, two projective islands in  Δ 6 \Delta^{6}  and three projective islands in  Π 3 7 \Pi_{3}^{7} , where a four-edged contraction is needed for C-reducibility.
In these cases, by showing the existence of non-contractible curves hitting at most two edges, we can show that the representativity of the resulting graph becomes two or less, which would not result in  𝒫 0 \mathcal{P}_{0} . 
 
 
 We would also need to check that the resulting graph is in  𝒫 1 \mathcal{P}_{1} , i.e. the resulting graph is bridgeless.
For the two projective islands  I I  in  Δ 6 \Delta^{6} , we can see that it is impossible to find a 5-edge cut  F F  containing the four edges of  c ⁡ ( I ) c(I) .
This implies that even after deleting  c ⁡ ( I ) c(I)  in the projective island  I I , the resulting cubic graph does not have a bridge.
For the three projective islands  I I  in  Π 3 7 \Pi_{3}^{7} , two of the islands have the risk of forming a bridge after deleting  c ⁡ ( I ) c(I) .
However, we see that in both of the cases, the bridge can be avoided by finding a different projective island in  Δ 6 \Delta^{6} .
Therefore we can conclude that it will not become a Petersen-like graph.
The detailed descriptions of these projective islands, together with their contraction of edges and the non-contractible curve showing low representativity, are shown in Figures  5  and  6 . 
 
 
 
 
 Figure 5 :  Two C-reducible islands in  Δ 6 \Delta^{6} , having contraction size  4 4 .
The contraction edges are depicted by dotted lines and
the non-contractible curve is represented with a blue curved line. 
 
 
 
 
 Figure 6 :  Three C-reducible islands in  Π 3 7 \Pi_{3}^{7} , having contraction size  ≥ 4 \geq 4 .
The contraction edges are depicted by dotted lines and
the non-contractible curve is represented with a blue curved line. 
 
 
 The two cases where a bridge may occur are shown in Figure  7 . 
 
 
 
 
 Figure 7 :  Two cases where a bridge (shown in bold lines) may occur in a previous 5-edge-cut (represented with a dotted curve) in  Π 3 7 \Pi_{3}^{7} .
In both cases, a larger projective island in  Δ 6 \Delta^{6}  is contained (as shown in the image, surrounded by a blue closed curve). 
 
 
 From these facts, the following proposition holds. 
 
 
 Proposition 4.5 . 
 
 Let  I I  be a  C C -reducible (projective or planar) island of contraction size at most four.
If  I I  appears in  G G  and  c ⁡ ( I ) c(I)  is not contained in any cyclical 5-edge-cut of  G G , it can be safely reduced. 
 
 
 
 For every reducible island  I ∈ 𝒦 I\in\mathcal{K} , we have checked whether  c ⁡ ( I ) c(I)  could be contained in any cyclical 5-edge-cut of  G G  and confirmed that no such  c ⁡ ( I ) c(I)  exists (this means that after deleting  c ⁡ ( I ) c(I) , the resulting graph has no bridge).
The checking is conducted using computer programs, and we explain the details in Section  9 . 
 
 
 Thus, we obtain the following result for islands of contraction size at most four in  𝒦 \mathcal{K} . 
 
 
 Lemma 4.6 . 
 
 Let  I I  be a C-reducible island of contraction size at most four in  𝒦 \mathcal{K} .
Then, it can be safely reduced. 
 
 
 
 
 5  Discharging process 

 
 Let  G ′ G^{\prime}  be an internally 6-connected triangulation of the projective plane. We start by assigning to each vertex  v ∈ V ⁡ ( G ′ ) v\in V(G^{\prime})  the value 
 

 
 
 T 0 ​ ( v ) := 10 ​ ( 6 − d ⁡ ( v ) ) , T_{0}(v):=10(6-d(v)), 
 
 
 which we call the  initial charge at  v v . It follows by Euler’s formula (see Lemma  5.4  below) that 
 

 
 
 ∑ v ∈ V ⁡ ( G ′ ) T 0 ​ ( v ) = 60 . \sum_{v\in V(G^{\prime})}T_{0}(v)=60. 
 
 
 Now we redistribute the charge among vertices by applying the  discharging rules  that are shown in Section  B  in the appendix. There are 169 rules (compared to 33 rules for the proof of the 4CT and that for the doublecross case). 
 
 
 Discharging rules are formally defined as follows. 
 
 
 Definition 5.1 . 
 
 A  rule  is six-tuple  R = ( G ⁡ ( R ) , β R , δ R , r ⁡ ( R ) , s ⁡ ( R ) , t ⁡ ( R ) ) R=(G(R),\beta_{R},\delta_{R},r(R),s(R),t(R)) , so that 
 
 (i)  
 
 G ⁡ ( R ) = ( V ⁡ ( R ) , E ⁡ ( R ) ) G(R)=(V(R),E(R))  is a near-triangulation, and for each  v ∈ V ⁡ ( R ) v\in V(R) ,  G ⁡ ( R ) − v G(R)-v  is connected. 
 
 (ii)  
 
 β R : V ⁡ ( R ) → ℕ \beta_{R}\colon V(R)\rightarrow\mathbb{N} ,  δ R : V ⁡ ( R ) → ℕ ∪ { ∞ } \delta_{R}\colon V(R)\rightarrow\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\} , such that  5 ≤ β R ​ ( v ) ≤ δ R ​ ( v ) 5\leq\beta_{R}(v)\leq\delta_{R}(v)  for each  v ∈ V ⁡ ( R ) v\in V(R) . 
 
 (iii)  
 
 r ⁡ ( R ) r(R)  is a positive integer. 
 
 (iv)  
 
 s ⁡ ( R ) , t ⁡ ( R ) ∈ V ⁡ ( R ) s(R),t(R)\in V(R)  are distinct adjacent vertices. 
 
 
 A configuration  K K   obeys  the rule  R R  if  G ⁡ ( K ) = G ⁡ ( R ) G(K)=G(R)  and every vertex  v ∈ V ⁡ ( K ) v\in V(K)  satisfies  β R ​ ( v ) ≤ γ K ​ ( v ) ≤ δ R ​ ( v ) \beta_{R}(v)\leq\gamma_{K}(v)\leq\delta_{R}(v) . 
 
 
 
 A rule is a near-triangulation in which each vertex  v v  is given a range of possible degrees, the interval  [ β R ​ ( v ) , δ R ​ ( v ) ] [\beta_{R}(v),\delta_{R}(v)] . The value  r ⁡ ( R ) r(R)  represents the amount of charge that is sent from the vertex  s ⁡ ( R ) s(R)  to its neighbor  t ⁡ ( R ) t(R) . We have  r ⁡ ( R ) = 1 r(R)=1  or  r ⁡ ( R ) = 2 r(R)=2  for all our rules. We describe the degree range  [ β R , δ R ] [\beta_{R},\delta_{R}]  by the same convention as in configurations. There are three cases. When  β R ​ ( v ) = δ R ​ ( v ) \beta_{R}(v)=\delta_{R}(v) , we describe  β R ​ ( v ) \beta_{R}(v)  by one of the shapes shown in Figure  3 . When  5 = β R ​ ( v ) < δ R ​ ( v ) 5=\beta_{R}(v)<\delta_{R}(v) , we describe  δ R ​ ( v ) \delta_{R}(v)  as the vertex shape and set  − -  next to the vertex. When  β R ​ ( v ) < δ R ​ ( v ) = ∞ \beta_{R}(v)<\delta_{R}(v)=\infty , we describe  β R ​ ( v ) \beta_{R}(v)  as the vertex shape and set  + +  next to the vertex. All our rules have one of these three types of degree ranges. 
 
 
 The set of 169 rules exhibited in Figure  26  in the appendix is denoted by  ℛ \mathcal{R} .
An edge with an arrow represents the direction in which a charge moves. Thus,  s ⁡ ( R ) , t ⁡ ( R ) s(R),t(R)  are two vertices that are incident with an edge with an arrow.
The number of arrows in the figure represents  r ⁡ ( R ) r(R) . 
 
 
 We calculate the amount of charge sent along an edge  s ​ t st  in  G ′ G^{\prime}  by checking which rules can be used on this edge. For a rule  R ∈ ℛ R\in\mathcal{R} ,  r ⁡ ( R ) r(R)  gives the amount of charge to send,  s ⁡ ( R ) s(R)  is the vertex that sends the charge, and  t ⁡ ( R ) t(R)  is the vertex that receives charge. We apply a rule  R R  when a configuration  K K , which obeys  R R  appears in  G ′ G^{\prime} . Note that each 11 11 
             11 
            
            
            
          The very first rule (R1) is the only exception that is used only once.  rule  R ∈ ℛ R\in\mathcal{R}  can be applied at the edge  s ​ t st  in two ways, where the triangles in  G ⁡ ( R ) G(R)  have the same orientation as in  G ′ G^{\prime} , and when they have opposite orientation. 12 12 
             12 
            
            
            
          Of course, there is no global orientation on the projective plane; we just consider orientation of triangles locally around the edge  s ​ t st . 
 
 
 Definition 5.2 . 
 
 For adjacent vertices  u , v ∈ V ⁡ ( G ′ ) u,v\in V(G^{\prime}) , we define  ϕ ⁡ ( u , v ) \phi(u,v)  as the sum of the values  r ⁡ ( R ) r(R)  over all rules  R ∈ ℛ R\in\mathcal{R}  that are applied with  s ⁡ ( R ) = u s(R)=u , and  t ⁡ ( R ) = v t(R)=v . This is the  charge sent from  u u  to  v v . 
 
 
 
 Definition 5.3 . 
 
 For a vertex  u ∈ V ⁡ ( G ′ ) u\in V(G^{\prime}) , we set 
 

 
 
 T ⁡ ( u ) := T 0 ​ ( u ) + ∑ v ∼ u ( ϕ ⁡ ( v , u ) − ϕ ⁡ ( u , v ) ) . T(u):=T_{0}(u)+\sum_{v\sim u}(\phi(v,u)-\phi(u,v)). 
 
 
 The obtained value is said to be the  final charge  at  u u . 
 
 
 
 The discharging method uses the following easy observation. 
 
 
 Lemma 5.4 . 
 
 ∑ u ∈ V ⁡ ( G ′ ) T ⁡ ( u ) = 60 \sum_{u\in V(G^{\prime})}T(u)=60 . 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 By applying any discharging rule, the total sum of all charges remains the same. Thus,  ∑ u ∈ V ⁡ ( G ′ ) T ⁡ ( u ) = ∑ u ∈ V ⁡ ( G ′ ) 10 ​ ( 6 − d ⁡ ( u ) ) \sum_{u\in V(G^{\prime})}T(u)=\sum_{u\in V(G^{\prime})}10(6-d(u)) . If  n n  is the number of vertices of  G ′ G^{\prime} , then Euler’s formula for the triangulation  G ′ G^{\prime}  implies that  1 2 ​ ∑ u ∈ V ⁡ ( G ′ ) d ⁡ ( u ) = | E ⁡ ( G ) | = 3 ​ n − 3 \tfrac{1}{2}\sum_{u\in V(G^{\prime})}d(u)=|E(G)|=3n-3 , and thus we have: 
 

 
 
 ∑ u ∈ V ⁡ ( G ′ ) T ⁡ ( u ) = ∑ u ∈ V ⁡ ( G ′ ) 10 ​ ( 6 − d ⁡ ( u ) ) = 60 ​ n − 10 ​ ∑ u ∈ V ⁡ ( G ′ ) d ⁡ ( u ) = 60 ​ n − 20 ​ ( 3 ​ n − 3 ) = 60 . \sum_{u\in V(G^{\prime})}T(u)=\sum_{u\in V(G^{\prime})}10(6-d(u))=60n-10\sum_{u\in V(G^{\prime})}d(u)=60n-20(3n-3)=60. 
 
 
 ∎ 
 
 
 
 Lemma  5.4  implies that there is a vertex  u u  with  T ⁡ ( u ) > 0 T(u)>0 . Known proofs of the 4CT show that for any vertex  u u  with  T ⁡ ( u ) > 0 T(u)>0 , there is a reducible configuration in the vicinity of  u u . This yields a contradiction to the assumption that we have a minimum counterexample. Indeed we have a similar result on the projective plane, but there is some difference. 
 
 
 Lemma 5.5 . 
 
 Let  G G  be a minimal counterexample. Then there is a vertex  v v  of the dual graph  G ′ G^{\prime}  with  T ⁡ ( v ) > 0 T(v)>0 , and there must exist a subgraph  K ′ K^{\prime}  of  G ′ G^{\prime}  which contains either  v v  or at least one of its neighbors, and is isomorphic to one of the reducible configurations of  𝒦 \mathcal{K}  (but  K ′ K^{\prime}  may not be an induced subgraph of  G ′ G^{\prime} ) 
 
 
 
 So the difference is that  K ′ K^{\prime}  may not be an induced subgraph of  G ′ G^{\prime} .
But, if  K ′ K^{\prime}  is not induced, this is because there are two vertices in  K ′ K^{\prime}  that are of distance at least five in  K ′ K^{\prime} . This issue was already handled by Corollary  3.17 . 
 
 
 We show Lemma  5.5  by computer check, but when  d ⁡ ( v ) = 5 d(v)=5  or  d ⁡ ( v ) = 6 d(v)=6  or  d ⁡ ( v ) ≥ 12 d(v)\geq 12  (in  G ′ G^{\prime} ), we can show this lemma without the help of computers. For other cases, computer-free proof would be too long. 
 
 
 5.1  Final charge at vertices of degree 5 or 6 in  G ′ G^{\prime} 

 
 We now consider the final charge  T ⁡ ( v ) T(v)  at vertices of degree at most six in the dual graph  G ′ G^{\prime} . We show, without using computer, that  T ⁡ ( v ) = 0 T(v)=0  when  d ⁡ ( v ) ≤ 6 d(v)\leq 6 .
A set of reducible configurations used here for the computer check-free proof is given in Figure  9 . The configurations in Figure  9  is contained in  𝒦 \mathcal{K} .
We denote a configuration in Figure  9  by conf(i)  ( 1 ≤ i ≤ 24 ) (1\leq i\leq 24)  in the natural order, from top to bottom in a row, from left to right in a column. 
 
 
 We only use the rules for vertices of degree  5 5  and  6 6 .
They are collected in Figure  8 . We denote the rule in Figure  8  by rule(i)  ( 1 ≤ i ≤ 8 ) (1\leq i\leq 8)  in the natural order. Only rule(1) gives value  r ⁡ ( R ) = 2 r(R)=2 .
The rules of our discharging method are stronger than that of the 4CT since many configurations that are reducible for planar graphs are not reducible for projective planar graphs.
