 Lemma 5.7 . 
 
 Let  G ′ = ( V , E ) G^{\prime}=(V,E)  be an internally 6-connected triangulation. Let  v ∈ V v\in V  be a vertex of degree 5 or 6.
Suppose that configurations that are shown in Figure  9  do not appear in  G ′ G^{\prime} . Then,  T ⁡ ( v ) = 0 T(v)=0 . 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 When  deg G ′ ⁡ ( v ) = 5 \deg_{G^{\prime}}(v)=5 , 
 

 
 
 T ⁡ ( v ) = 10 + I 1 + I 4 − O 1 − O 2 − O 3 − O 5 − O 6 − O 7 T(v)=10+I_{1}+I_{4}-O_{1}-O_{2}-O_{3}-O_{5}-O_{6}-O_{7} 
 
 
 since other  I k , O k I_{k},O_{k}  do not affect charge of  v v .  O 1 = 10 O_{1}=10  since  v v  has five neighbors. By Lemma  5.6  (i), (iii) and  I 5 = 0 I_{5}=0 ,  T ⁡ ( v ) = 0 T(v)=0 .
When  deg G ′ ⁡ ( v ) = 6 \deg_{G^{\prime}}(v)=6 , 
 

 
 
 T ⁡ ( v ) = I 1 + I 3 + I 4 + I 5 + I 6 − O 2 − O 3 − O 4 − O 5 − O 6 − O 7 − O 8 , T(v)=I_{1}+I_{3}+I_{4}+I_{5}+I_{6}-O_{2}-O_{3}-O_{4}-O_{5}-O_{6}-O_{7}-O_{8}, 
 
 
 since other  I k , O k I_{k},O_{k}  do not affect charge of  v v .
By Lemma  5.6  (i), (ii), (iii) and (iv),  T ⁡ ( v ) = 0 T(v)=0 .
∎ 
 
 
 
 
 5.2  Maximum charge that a vertex can send 

 
 Let  n n  be a positive integer. We want to figure out the case that a vertex sends charge  n n  to a vertex of degree at least  7 7 . We calculate it by combining rules of  ℛ \mathcal{R}  and then by checking that none of our reducible configurations of  𝒦 \mathcal{K}  is a subgraph. We use computer-check to calculate it. For this purpose, we provide a precise definition and a pseudo-code in Section  C.3  in the appendix 13 13 
               13 
              
              
              
            In Section  C.3  we use the same notation and terms as rules to explain the case when a vertex sends charge  n n  (e.g., a configuration  obeys  the case). .
We now show the following. 
 
 
 Lemma 5.8 . 
 
 Assume that none of our reducible configurations in the set  𝒦 \mathcal{K}  is a subgraph in  G ′ G^{\prime} . Then, any vertex  v ∈ V ⁡ ( G ′ ) v\in V(G^{\prime})  can send charge at most  6 6  to any one of its neighbors.
If it sends charge  6 6  ( 5 5  respectively), then we have one of the cases shown in Figure  23  (Figure  24 ) in the appendix.
If a vertex of degree  5 5  sends charge  4 4 , then we have one of the cases in Figure  25  in the appendix. 
 
 
 
 We prove Lemma  5.8  by executing Algorithm  8  which is described in the appendix. 
 
 
 We denote the case in row  i i  and column  j j  in Figure  23  by send6( i i ,  j j ). We use the same notation to denote the case in Figure  24  by send5( i i ,  j j ) and Figure  25  by send4( i i ,  j j ), respectively. 
 
 
 Using Lemma  5.8  we prove the following. 
 
 
 Lemma 5.9 . 
 
 Assume that none of our reducible configurations in the set  𝒦 \mathcal{K}  is a subgraph in  G ′ G^{\prime} . Let  v ​ u ​ w vuw  be a triangle face in  G ′ G^{\prime} , and degree of  w w  be at least 7. Then,  ϕ ⁡ ( u , w ) + ϕ ⁡ ( v , w ) ≤ 10 \phi(u,w)+\phi(v,w)\leq 10 . If the equality holds,  ϕ ⁡ ( u , w ) = 6 \phi(u,w)=6  and  ϕ ⁡ ( v , w ) = 4 \phi(v,w)=4  (or the symmetric case with  u , v u,v  interchanged) or  ϕ ⁡ ( u , w ) = ϕ ⁡ ( v , w ) = 5 \phi(u,w)=\phi(v,w)=5 . Figure  10  shows all cases when the first of these possibilities happens. 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 By Lemma  5.8 , we need to show  ϕ ⁡ ( v , w ) ≤ 4 \phi(v,w)\leq 4  when  ϕ ⁡ ( u , w ) = 6 \phi(u,w)=6 . There are three cases in which a vertex sends charge  6 6  by Lemma  5.8 . In any case,  d ⁡ ( u ) = 6 d(u)=6  or  7 7  and  d ⁡ ( v ) = 5 d(v)=5 . A vertex of degree  5 5  does not send charge  6 6 , so  ϕ ⁡ ( v , w ) ≤ 5 \phi(v,w)\leq 5 . Let  x x  be a vertex that is adjacent to both  v v  and  w w , and that is different from  u u . 
 
 
 The degree of  x x  can be  5 5  only when  u u  sends charge  6 6  in send6(1, 2) since conf(1) appears in all other cases. When send6(1, 2) happens and the degree of  x x  is  5 5 ,  ϕ ⁡ ( v , w ) \phi(v,w)  is at most 4 by Lemma  5.8 . 
 
 
 When the degree of  x x  is  6 6 ,  ϕ ⁡ ( v , w ) \phi(v,w)  is exactly  4 4  when  u u  sends charge  6 6  in send6(1, 1) and in send6(1, 2), but  ϕ ⁡ ( v , w ) \phi(v,w)  is at most  4 4  by Lemma  5.8 . 
 
 
 When the degree of  x x  is at least  7 7 ,  ϕ ⁡ ( v , w ) \phi(v,w)  is at most 3 by Lemma  5.8 . 
 
 
 ∎

 Using these lemmas, we obtain the following result. 
 
 
 Lemma 5.10 . 
 
 Any vertex  v v  of degree at least 12 has final charge at most  0 0 . 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 By Lemma  5.9 , the average charge the vertices around  v v  send to  v v  is at most  5 5 . This implies that  T ⁡ ( v ) ≤ 0 T(v)\leq 0 .

 If degree of  u u  is at least  8 8 , then  ϕ ⁡ ( u , v ) + ϕ ⁡ ( u , w ) = 0 \phi(u,v)+\phi(u,w)=0 . 
 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 We consider the case when degree of  u u  is  5 5 . We denote the neighbors of  u u  other than  v , w v,w  by  x , y , z x,y,z  such that  v , w , x , y , z v,w,x,y,z  appear in the clockwise order of neighbors of  u u .  ϕ ⁡ ( u , v ) , ϕ ⁡ ( u , w ) ≤ 4 \phi(u,v),\phi(u,w)\leq 4  by Lemma  5.8 . So  ϕ ⁡ ( u , v ) + ϕ ⁡ ( u , w ) ≤ 8 \phi(u,v)+\phi(u,w)\leq 8 .
When  ϕ ⁡ ( u , v ) + ϕ ⁡ ( u , w ) ≥ 7 \phi(u,v)+\phi(u,w)\geq 7  either of  ϕ ⁡ ( u , v ) , ϕ ⁡ ( u , w ) \phi(u,v),\phi(u,w)  is 4. We assume  ϕ ⁡ ( u , v ) = 4 \phi(u,v)=4  without loss of generality. The degree of  z z  must be  5 5  and the degree of  y y  must be at most  6 6  by Lemma  5.8 . The degree of  x x  is at most  6 6  otherwise  ϕ ⁡ ( u , w ) = 2 \phi(u,w)=2 , which implies  ϕ ⁡ ( u , v ) + ϕ ⁡ ( u , w ) ≤ 6 \phi(u,v)+\phi(u,w)\leq 6 . 
 
 
 We next consider the case when the degree of  y y  is  5 5 . The degree of  x x  is not  5 5  otherwise, conf(1) appears When the degree of  x x  is  6 6 ,  ϕ ⁡ ( u , v ) = 4 , ϕ ⁡ ( u , w ) = 3 \phi(u,v)=4,\phi(u,w)=3  by our rules and Lemma  5.8 . 
 
 
 We now consider the case when the degree of  y y  is  6 6 . When the degree of  x x  is  5 5 ,  ϕ ⁡ ( u , v ) = ϕ ⁡ ( u , w ) = 4 \phi(u,v)=\phi(u,w)=4 .
When the degree of  x x  is  6 6 ,  ϕ ⁡ ( u , v ) \phi(u,v)  can be exactly  4 4  only when send4(1, 3) happens. Let  s s  be a vertex that is adjacent to both  x , y x,y  and that is different from  u u . When the degree of  s s  is  5 5 ,  ϕ ⁡ ( u , w ) \phi(u,w)  must be  3 3 , but then conf(3) appears Let  t t  be a vertex that is adjacent to  x , s x,s  and that is different from  y y . When the degree of  t t  is  5 5  and the degree of  s s  is  6 6 ,  ϕ ⁡ ( u , w ) \phi(u,w)  must be  3 3 , but then conf(15) appears In other cases,  ϕ ⁡ ( u , w ) \phi(u,w)  is  2 2  so  ϕ ⁡ ( u , v ) + ϕ ⁡ ( u , w ) ≤ 6 \phi(u,v)+\phi(u,w)\leq 6 . 
 
 
 When the degree of  u u  is at least  7 7 ,  ϕ ⁡ ( u , v ) , ϕ ⁡ ( u , w ) ≤ 2 \phi(u,v),\phi(u,w)\leq 2  by Lemma  5.11 . This implies  ϕ ⁡ ( u , v ) + ϕ ⁡ ( u , w ) ≤ 4 \phi(u,v)+\phi(u,w)\leq 4 . 
 
 
 We consider the case when the degree of  u u  is  6 6 . By Lemma  5.11 ,  ϕ ⁡ ( u , v ) , ϕ ⁡ ( u , w ) ≤ 3 \phi(u,v),\phi(u,w)\leq 3 , so we need to show that  ϕ ⁡ ( u , w ) \phi(u,w)  is at most 1 when  ϕ ⁡ ( u , v ) = 3 \phi(u,v)=3 . There are five cases  ϕ ⁡ ( u , v ) \phi(u,v)  is exactly  3 3  as shown in Figure  11 . Let  x 5 x_{5}  be a vertex that is adjacent to  x 3 , x 4 x_{3},x_{4}  and that is different from  u u .  When  d ⁡ ( x 1 ) = 5 d(x_{1})=5  and  d ⁡ ( x 2 ) ≤ 6 d(x_{2})\leq 6 , a reducible configuration appears in each case; (conf(14), conf(15) in case 1, conf(2), conf(3) in case 2, conf(3), conf(15) in case 3, conf(16), conf(17) in case 4) Therefore,  d ⁡ ( x 2 ) ≥ 7 d(x_{2})\geq 7  when  d ⁡ ( x 1 ) = 5 d(x_{1})=5 .  Lemma 5.13 . 
 
 Let  H H  be an internally 6-connected triangulation in the plane (or the projective plane). Assume that none of our reducible configurations in the set  𝒦 \mathcal{K}  is a subgraph of  H H .
Let  u u  be a vertex in  H H  and  v , w , x v,w,x  be the neighbors of  u u  so that  v , w v,w  are adjacent and  w , x w,x  are adjacent. Assume that the degree of  v , w , x v,w,x  is at least  9 9 . 
 
 •  
 
 If the degree of  u u  is  5 5 , then  ϕ ⁡ ( u , v ) + ϕ ⁡ ( u , w ) + ϕ ⁡ ( u , x ) ≤ 10 \phi(u,v)+\phi(u,w)+\phi(u,x)\leq 10 . If the equality holds, they are in Figure  14 . 
 
 •  
 
 If the degree of  u u  is  6 6  ( 7 7 ), then  ϕ ⁡ ( u , v ) + ϕ ⁡ ( u , w ) + ϕ ⁡ ( u , x ) ≤ 6 \phi(u,v)+\phi(u,w)+\phi(u,x)\leq 6  ( 4 4 ). 
 
 •  
 
 If the degree of  u u  is at least 8, then  ϕ ⁡ ( u , v ) + ϕ ⁡ ( u , w ) + ϕ ⁡ ( u , x ) = 0 \phi(u,v)+\phi(u,w)+\phi(u,x)=0 . 
 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 When the degree of  u u  is at least  6 6 ,  ϕ ⁡ ( u , w ) = 0 \phi(u,w)=0 , so Lemma  5.11  implies the lemma. We consider the case when the degree of  u u  is  5 5 . We denote the neighbors of  u u  other than  v , w , x v,w,x  by  y , z y,z  such that  v , w , x , y , z v,w,x,y,z  appear in the clockwise order of neighbors of  u u . Then,  ϕ ⁡ ( u , w ) = 2 \phi(u,w)=2  and  ϕ ⁡ ( u , v ) , ϕ ⁡ ( u , x ) ≤ 4 \phi(u,v),\phi(u,x)\leq 4  by Lemma  5.11 . This implies  ϕ ⁡ ( u , v ) + ϕ ⁡ ( u , w ) + ϕ ⁡ ( u , x ) ≤ 10 \phi(u,v)+\phi(u,w)+\phi(u,x)\leq 10 . If the equality holds,  ϕ ⁡ ( u , v ) = ϕ ⁡ ( u , x ) = 4 \phi(u,v)=\phi(u,x)=4 . In this case, the degree of  y y  and  z z  must be  5 5  by Lemma  5.8 . This is the only case where the equality holds, as shown in Figure  14 .
∎ 
 
 
 
 
 
 6  Looking at the smaller side 

 
 In this section, we show Lemma  6.1 , which states that a reducible configuration appears strictly inside the disk that is bounded by a cycle with some specified conditions.
This lemma is necessary to handle 6,7-edge-cuts in Section  7  and low representativity cases in Section  8 . 
 
 
 Lemma 6.1 . 
 
 Let  T T  be an internally 6-connected near-triangulation in the plane, which is a subgraph of  G ′ G^{\prime} . Let  C C  be an induced cycle in  T T , which bounds the infinite region of  T T . Assume that there is no vertex in  V ⁡ ( T − C ) V(T-C)  which is adjacent to at least four consecutive vertices of  C C  and there are more than  18 5 ⋅ | C | − 12 \frac{18}{5}\cdot|C|-12  edges in  T T  between  C C  and  T − C T-C . Then a reducible configuration in the set  𝒦 \mathcal{K}  appears in  T T . 
 
 
 
 We use the following lemma to show Lemma  6.1 .
We let  n = | C | n=|C|  and denote the number of edges between  C C  and  T − C T-C  by  k k . 
 
 
 Lemma 6.2 . 
 
 

 
 
 ∑ v ∈ V ⁡ ( T ) − V ⁡ ( C ) 10 ⋅ ( 6 − d ⁡ ( v ) ) = 60 − 20 ​ n + 10 ​ k . \sum_{v\in V(T)-V(C)}10\cdot(6-d(v))=60-20n+10k. 
 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 The sum of the size of the faces of  T T  is  3 ​ ( | F ​ ( T ) | − 1 ) + n 3(|F(T)|-1)+n . So  2 ​ | E ⁡ ( T ) | = 3 ​ ( | F ⁡ ( T ) | − 1 ) + n 2|E(T)|=3(|F(T)|-1)+n  holds.
 | V ⁡ ( T ) | − | E ⁡ ( T ) | + | F ⁡ ( T ) | = 2 |V(T)|-|E(T)|+|F(T)|=2  holds by Euler’s formula. The combination of these two formulas implies  | E ⁡ ( T ) | = 3 ​ | V ⁡ ( T ) | − 3 − n |E(T)|=3|V(T)|-3-n , so 
 

 
 
 ∑ v ∈ V ⁡ ( T ) 10 ⋅ ( 6 − d ⁡ ( v ) ) = 60 | V ⁡ ( T ) ​ | − 20 | ​ E ​ ( T ) | = 20 ​ ( 3 + n ) . \sum_{v\in V(T)}10\cdot(6-d(v))=60|V(T)|-20|E(T)|=20(3+n). 
 
 
 The number of edges between  C C  and  T − C T-C  is  k k .
This implies  ∑ v ∈ V ⁡ ( C ) d ⁡ ( v ) = 2 ​ | V ⁡ ( C ) | + k = 2 ​ n + k \sum_{v\in V(C)}d(v)=2|V(C)|+k=2n+k . So 
 

 
 
 ∑ v ∈ V ⁡ ( C ) 10 ⋅ ( 6 − d ⁡ ( v ) ) = 60 ​ | V ⁡ ( C ) | − 10 ​ ( 2 ​ n + k ) = 40 ​ n − 10 ​ k . \sum_{v\in V(C)}10\cdot(6-d(v))=60|V(C)|-10(2n+k)=40n-10k. 
 
 
 Therefore 
 

 
 
 ∑ v ∈ V ⁡ ( T ) ∖ V ⁡ ( C ) 10 ⋅ ( 6 − d ⁡ ( v ) ) = 20 ​ ( 3 + n ) − ( 40 ​ n − 10 ​ k ) = 60 − 20 ​ n + 10 ​ k . \sum_{v\in V(T)\setminus V(C)}10\cdot(6-d(v))=20(3+n)-(40n-10k)=60-20n+10k. 
 
 
 ∎ 
 
 
 
 We extend the embedding of  T T  to an embedding of an internally 6-connected triangulation  T ′ T^{\prime} , by adding more vertices and edges inside the face bounded by  C C , in such a way that every vertex in  V ⁡ ( C ) V(C)  has degree of at least 12 in  T ′ T^{\prime}  and newly added vertices have degree of at least  5 5 . 
 
 
 Let vertices of  C C  be  u 0 ​ u 1 ​ … ​ u n − 1 u_{0}u_{1}\dots u_{n-1}  so that  u i u_{i}  and  u i + 1 u_{i+1}  are adjacent. We sometimes use an index  i i  which is out of range to denote  u i u_{i} , which means the index  i i  module  n n  (e.g.  u n u_{n}  means  u 0 u_{0} .). For each  0 ≤ i < n 0\leq i<n , there is a vertex of  V ⁡ ( T − C ) V(T-C)  that is adjacent to both  u i u_{i}  and  u i + 1 u_{i+1} . We denote these vertices by  v i v_{i}  ( 0 ≤ i < n 0\leq i<n ). We denote a set of vertices of  T − C T-C  that are adjacent to  u i u_{i}  by  v i − 1 = v i , 0 , v i , 1 , … , v i , n i − 1 = v i v_{i-1}=v_{i,0},v_{i,1},\dots,v_{i,n_{i}-1}=v_{i}  so that  v i , j v_{i,j}  and  v i , j + 1 v_{i,j+1}  are adjacent  ( 0 ≤ j < n i − 1 ) (0\leq j<n_{i}-1)  , where  n i n_{i}  is the number of vertices of  T − C T-C  that are adjacent to  u i u_{i} . 
 
 
 If  v i = v i + 1 = v i + 2 v_{i}=v_{i+1}=v_{i+2} ,  v i v_{i}  would be a vertex of  T − C T-C  which is adjacent to at least four consecutive vertices of  C C , which would contradict the hypothesis of Lemma  6.1 . So this would not happen. 
 
 
 We now calculate the total amount of charge sent from  T − C T-C  to  C C . In order to do so, we calculate the amount of charge sent by each edge between  T − C T-C  and  C C , and we compute the sum. We divide into the two cases, depending on whether  v i v_{i}  and  v i + 1 v_{i+1}  are same or not. We only need the upper bound of the amount of charge sent by each edge, which is achieved when degree of  v i v_{i}  is  5 5  here, but later we need the amount of charge sent when degree of  v i v_{i}  is at least  6 6 . So we consider all these cases here. 
 
 
 Lemma 6.3 . 
 
 For any  0 ≤ i < n 0\leq i<n , the following holds. 
 
 (i)  
 
 Assume  v i − 1 ≠ v i v_{i-1}\neq v_{i}  and  v i ≠ v i + 1 v_{i}\neq v_{i+1} . 
 
 •  
 
 If the degree of  v i v_{i}  is  d ≤ 7 d\leq 7 , then
 ϕ ⁡ ( v i , u i ) + ϕ ⁡ ( v i , u i + 1 ) + ∑ j = 1 n i + 1 − 2 ϕ ⁡ ( v i + 1 , j , u i + 1 ) ≤ 5 ⋅ ( n i + 1 − 2 ) + f d \phi(v_{i},u_{i})+\phi(v_{i},u_{i+1})+\sum_{j=1}^{n_{i+1}-2}\phi(v_{i+1,j},u_{i+1})\leq 5\cdot(n_{i+1}-2)+f_{d}  holds, where  f 5 = 8 , f 6 = 4 , f 7 = 4 f_{5}=8,f_{6}=4,f_{7}=4 . 
 
 •  
 
 When the degree of  v i v_{i}  is at least  8 8 ,
 ϕ ⁡ ( v i , u i ) + ϕ ⁡ ( v i , u i + 1 ) + ∑ j = 1 n i + 1 − 2 ϕ ⁡ ( v i + 1 , j , u i + 1 ) ≤ 5 ⋅ ( n i + 1 − 2 ) \phi(v_{i},u_{i})+\phi(v_{i},u_{i+1})+\sum_{j=1}^{n_{i+1}-2}\phi(v_{i+1,j},u_{i+1})\leq 5\cdot(n_{i+1}-2)  holds. 
 
 
 
 (ii)  
 
 Assume  v i − 1 ≠ v i v_{i-1}\neq v_{i}  and  v i = v i + 1 v_{i}=v_{i+1} . 
 
 •  
 
 When the degree of  v i v_{i}  is  d ≤ 7 d\leq 7 ,  ϕ ⁡ ( v i , u i ) + ϕ ⁡ ( v i , u i + 1 ) + ϕ ⁡ ( v i , u i + 2 ) + ∑ j = 1 n i + 2 − 2 ϕ ⁡ ( v i + 2 , j , u i + 2 ) ≤ 5 ⋅ ( n i + 2 − 2 ) + g d \phi(v_{i},u_{i})+\phi(v_{i},u_{i+1})+\phi(v_{i},u_{i+2})+\sum_{j=1}^{n_{i+2}-2}\phi(v_{i+2,j},u_{i+2})\leq 5\cdot(n_{i+2}-2)+g_{d}  holds, where  g 5 = 10 , g 6 = 6 , g 7 = 4 g_{5}=10,g_{6}=6,g_{7}=4 . 
 
 •  
 
 When the degree of  v i v_{i}  is at least  8 8 ,  ϕ ⁡ ( v i , u i ) + ϕ ⁡ ( v i , u i + 1 ) + ϕ ⁡ ( v i , u i + 2 ) + ∑ j = 1 n i + 2 − 2 ϕ ⁡ ( v i + 2 , j , u i + 2 ) ≤ 5 ⋅ ( n i + 2 − 2 ) \phi(v_{i},u_{i})+\phi(v_{i},u_{i+1})+\phi(v_{i},u_{i+2})+\sum_{j=1}^{n_{i+2}-2}\phi(v_{i+2,j},u_{i+2})\leq 5\cdot(n_{i+2}-2)  holds. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 First, we show (i).
We have  ∑ j = 1 n i + 1 − 2 ϕ ⁡ ( v i + 1 , j , u i + 1 ) ≤ 5 ⋅ ( n i + 1 − 2 ) + 1 \sum_{j=1}^{n_{i+1}-2}\phi(v_{i+1,j},u_{i+1})\leq 5\cdot(n_{i+1}-2)+1  by Lemma  5.8  and Lemma  5.9 .
 ∑ j = 1 n i + 1 − 2 ϕ ⁡ ( v i + 1 , j , u i + 1 ) ≤ 5 ⋅ ( n i + 1 − 2 ) \sum_{j=1}^{n_{i+1}-2}\phi(v_{i+1,j},u_{i+1})\leq 5\cdot(n_{i+1}-2)  unless  ϕ ⁡ ( v i + 1 , 1 , u i + 1 ) = 6 \phi(v_{i+1,1},u_{i+1})=6  and  ϕ ⁡ ( v i + 1 , 2 , u i + 1 ) = 4 \phi(v_{i+1,2},u_{i+1})=4 , in which csae
 d ⁡ ( v i ) = 5 d(v_{i})=5 ,  d ⁡ ( v i + 1 , 1 ) = 6 d(v_{i+1,1})=6 ,  d ⁡ ( v i , n i − 2 ) = 5 d(v_{i,n_{i}-2})=5 , and degree of the vertex that is adjacent to  v i , v i + 1 , 1 , v i , n i − 2 v_{i},v_{i+1,1},v_{i,n_{i}-2}  is  5 5 
by Lemma  5.9 . In this case,  ϕ ⁡ ( v i , u i ) + ϕ ⁡ ( v i , u i + 1 ) = 7 \phi(v_{i},u_{i})+\phi(v_{i},u_{i+1})=7  by Lemma  5.12 .
For all other cases,  ϕ ⁡ ( v i , u i ) + ϕ ⁡ ( v i , u i + 1 ) ≤ 8 \phi(v_{i},u_{i})+\phi(v_{i},u_{i+1})\leq 8  by Lemma  5.12 .
So,  ϕ ⁡ ( v i , u i ) + ϕ ⁡ ( v i , u i + 1 ) + ∑ j = 1 n i + 1 − 2 ϕ ⁡ ( v i + 1 , j , u i + 1 ) ≤ 5 ​ ( n i + 1 − 2 ) + 8 \phi(v_{i},u_{i})+\phi(v_{i},u_{i+1})+\sum_{j=1}^{n_{i+1}-2}\phi(v_{i+1,j},u_{i+1})\leq 5(n_{i+1}-2)+8  holds. 
 
 
 When degree of  v i v_{i}  is at least  6 6 ,  ϕ ⁡ ( v i , u i ) + ϕ ⁡ ( v i , u i + 1 ) ≤ f d ⁡ ( v i ) \phi(v_{i},u_{i})+\phi(v_{i},u_{i+1})\leq f_{d(v_{i})}  by Lemma  5.12 . Therefore,  ϕ ⁡ ( v i , u i ) + ϕ ⁡ ( v i , u i + 1 ) + ∑ j = 1 n i + 1 − 2 ϕ ⁡ ( v i + 1 , j , u i + 1 ) ≤ 5 ⋅ ( n i + 1 − 2 ) + f d ⁡ ( v i ) \phi(v_{i},u_{i})+\phi(v_{i},u_{i+1})+\sum_{j=1}^{n_{i+1}-2}\phi(v_{i+1,j},u_{i+1})\leq 5\cdot(n_{i+1}-2)+f_{d(v_{i})} . 
 
 
 Second, we show (ii).
We have  ∑ j = 1 n i + 2 − 2 ϕ ⁡ ( v i + 2 , j , u i + 2 ) ≤ 5 ⋅ ( n i + 2 − 2 ) + 1 \sum_{j=1}^{n_{i+2}-2}\phi(v_{i+2,j},u_{i+2})\leq 5\cdot(n_{i+2}-2)+1  by Lemmas  5.8  and  5.9 .
 ∑ j = 1 n i + 2 − 2 ϕ ⁡ ( v i + 2 , j , u i + 2 ) ≤ 5 ⋅ ( n i + 2 − 2 ) \sum_{j=1}^{n_{i+2}-2}\phi(v_{i+2,j},u_{i+2})\leq 5\cdot(n_{i+2}-2)  unless  ϕ ⁡ ( v i + 2 , 1 , u i + 2 ) = 6 \phi(v_{i+2,1},u_{i+2})=6  and  ϕ ⁡ ( v i + 2 , 2 , u i + 2 ) = 4 \phi(v_{i+2,2},u_{i+2})=4 .
Suppose  ϕ ⁡ ( v i + 2 , 1 , u i + 2 ) = 6 \phi(v_{i+2,1},u_{i+2})=6  and  ϕ ⁡ ( v i + 2 , 2 , u i + 2 ) = 4 \phi(v_{i+2,2},u_{i+2})=4 . By Lemma  5.9 ,  d ⁡ ( v i + 2 , 1 ) = 6 d(v_{i+2,1})=6 ,  d ⁡ ( v i ) = 5 d(v_{i})=5  and either  u i u_{i}  or  u i + 1 u_{i+1}  must be of degree  5 5 , but this does not happen since the degree of  u i , u i + 1 , u i + 2 u_{i},u_{i+1},u_{i+2}  each is at least 12.
 ϕ ⁡ ( v i , u i ) + ϕ ⁡ ( v i , u i + 1 ) + ϕ ⁡ ( v i , u i + 2 ) ≤ 10 \phi(v_{i},u_{i})+\phi(v_{i},u_{i+1})+\phi(v_{i},u_{i+2})\leq 10  by Lemma  5.13 .
So,  ϕ ⁡ ( v i , u i ) + ϕ ⁡ ( v i , u i + 1 ) + ϕ ⁡ ( v i , u i + 2 ) + ∑ j = 1 n i + 2 − 2 ϕ ⁡ ( v i + 2 , j , u i + 2 ) ≤ 5 ⋅ ( n i + 2 − 2 ) + 10 \phi(v_{i},u_{i})+\phi(v_{i},u_{i+1})+\phi(v_{i},u_{i+2})+\sum_{j=1}^{n_{i+2}-2}\phi(v_{i+2,j},u_{i+2})\leq 5\cdot(n_{i+2}-2)+10 . 
 
 
 When the degree of  v i v_{i}  is at least  6 6 , we have  ϕ ⁡ ( v i , u i ) + ϕ ⁡ ( v i , u i + 1 ) + ϕ ⁡ ( v i , u i + 2 ) ≤ g d ⁡ ( v i ) \phi(v_{i},u_{i})+\phi(v_{i},u_{i+1})+\phi(v_{i},u_{i+2})\leq g_{d(v_{i})}  by Lemma  5.13 . Therefore,  ϕ ⁡ ( v i , u i ) + ϕ ⁡ ( v i , u i + 1 ) + ϕ ⁡ ( v i , u i + 2 ) + ∑ j = 1 n i + 2 − 2 ϕ ⁡ ( v i + 2 , j , u i + 2 ) ≤ 5 ⋅ ( n i + 2 − 2 ) + g d ⁡ ( v i ) \phi(v_{i},u_{i})+\phi(v_{i},u_{i+1})+\phi(v_{i},u_{i+2})+\sum_{j=1}^{n_{i+2}-2}\phi(v_{i+2,j},u_{i+2})\leq 5\cdot(n_{i+2}-2)+g_{d(v_{i})} .
∎ 
 
 
 
 Lemma 6.4 . 
 
 Let  a , b a,b  be the number of the integer  i i   ( 0 ≤ i < n ) (0\leq i<n)  so that (i) or (ii) in Lemma  6.3  holds, respectively.
The total amount of charge sent from  T − C T-C  to  C C  by the discharging rules of  ℛ \mathcal{R}  is at most  5 ​ k − 2 ​ n − b 5k-2n-b  if none of our configurations in  𝒦 \mathcal{K}  appears in  T T . 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 We can easily see  a + 2 ​ b = n a+2b=n  holds. We know that the average of the amount of charge sent through each edge is at most  5 5  by the two cases in Lemma  6.3 . For case (i), the total amount of charge is smaller by 2 compared to the case when the average charge through each edge is exactly  5 5 . For case (ii), the total amount of charge is smaller by 5 compared to the case when the average charge through each edge is exactly  5 5 .
Therefore, the amount of the total charge sent from  T − C T-C  to  C C  by the discharging rules of  ℛ \mathcal{R}  is at most  5 ​ k − ( 2 ​ a + 5 ​ b ) = 5 ​ k − 2 ​ n − b 5k-(2a+5b)=5k-2n-b .
∎ 
 
 
 
 Proof of Lemma  6.1 . 
 
 The amount of initial charge accumulated in  T − C T-C  is  60 − 20 ​ n + 10 ​ k 60-20n+10k  by Lemma  6.2 .
The total amount of charge sent from  T − C T-C  to  C C  by the discharging rules of  ℛ \mathcal{R}  is at most  5 ​ k − 2 ​ n − b 5k-2n-b  by Lemma  6.4 .
Thus the sum of charge accumulated in vertices of  T − C T-C  is positive after applying rules since  ( 60 − 20 ​ n + 10 ​ k ) − ( 5 ​ k − 2 ​ n − b ) ≥ 5 ​ k − 18 ​ n + 60 > 0 (60-20n+10k)-(5k-2n-b)\geq 5k-18n+60>0  by the hypothesis of this lemma. This implies that there must be a vertex of positive final charge in  V ⁡ ( T ) − V ⁡ ( C ) V(T)-V(C) , and hence a reducible configuration appears in  T ′ T^{\prime} . None of our reducible configurations has a vertex of degree at least 12, so one of our reducible configurations must appear in  T T .
∎ 
 
 
 
 It turns out that Lemma  6.1  is quite useful for other cases, including the planar, the doublecross, and the apex cases. Indeed, the following holds. 
 
 
 Corollary 6.5 . 
 
 Let  T T  be an internally 6-connected near-triangulation in the plane and let  C C  be an induced cycle in  T T , which bounds the infinite region of  T T . Let  ℛ , 𝒦 \mathcal{R},\mathcal{K}  be rules, reducible configurations for  T T , respectively. 
 
 
 Assume that 
 
 1.  
 
 there is no vertex in  V ⁡ ( T − C ) V(T-C)  which is adjacent to at least four consecutive vertices of  C C , 
 
 2.  
 
 there are more than  18 5 ⋅ | C | − 12 \frac{18}{5}\cdot|C|-12  edges in  T T  between  C C  and  T − C T-C , 
 
 3.  
 
 from  ℛ \mathcal{R} , every vertex that is in  V ⁡ ( T − C ) V(T-C)  sends charge at most five to any vertex of  C C , 
 
 4.  
 
 from  ℛ \mathcal{R} , any vertex in  V ⁡ ( T − C ) V(T-C)  which is adjacent to exactly two consecutive vertices of  C C  sends charge at most 8 together to  C C , 
 
 5.  
 
 no vertex in  𝒦 \mathcal{K}  is of degree at least 12 in  G ′ G^{\prime} , and 
 
 6.  
 
 from  ℛ \mathcal{R} , any vertex in  V ⁡ ( T − C ) V(T-C)  which is adjacent to exactly three consecutive vertices of  C C  sends charge at most 10 together to  C C . 
 
 
 Then, there is a vertex in  V ⁡ ( T − C ) V(T-C)  that ends up with the final charge positive. 
 
 
 
 
 7  Cuts of size 6 or 7 

 
 Let us recall that  G G  is a minimal counterexample and  G ′ G^{\prime}  is the dual of the embedding of  G G . We also assume that the representativity of the embedding of both  G G  and  G ′ G^{\prime}  is at least three. In this section, we will show that one side of  G G  divided by a 6-edge-cut or a 7-edge-cut is relatively small. By Lemma  2.4 , we know that one side of  G G  divided by a cut of order at most five is small.
Let us observe that edges in a cut of  G G  correspond to a circuit  C C  in  G ′ G^{\prime} . Throughout this section, we shall consider  G ′ G^{\prime} . The goal of this section is to prove Lemma  7.4  that roughly says that the disk bounded by a circuit of length six or seven contains at most three or four vertices (strictly inside). 
 
 
 The proof of this lemma needs a lot of case analysis, unfortunately.
The problem is that when we find a  C C -reducible configuration  K K  inside the circuit  C C  in  G ′ G^{\prime} , after contractions in  K K , it is still possible that the resulting graph would end up with  K 6 K_{6} , after low-vertex cut reductions. There are a lot of cases we have to handle, and for some cases, we do need some computer checks, see Subsection  7.1 . 
 
 
 To this end, we first handle conf(1), which is a special case of Lemma  7.4 , because the ring of conf(1) is of length exactly six, and conf(1) consists of four vertices (which would violate Lemma  7.4  below). The configuration conf(1) has two different contractions of size exactly six for C-reducibility. We prove that  G ′ G^{\prime}  would not result in  K 6 K_{6}  after one of the contractions. 
 
 
 Figure 15 :  A free completion of  K K  with ring, where  K K  is a configuration that consists of four vertices of degree five. The bold line represents the contraction used for C-reducibility. 
 
 
 Lemma 7.1 . 
 
 If conf(1) appears in  G ′ G^{\prime} , then after some edge contraction and low-vertex cut reductions,  G ′ G^{\prime}  results in a graph in  𝒫 1 ∖ 𝒫 0 \mathcal{P}_{1}\setminus\mathcal{P}_{0} . 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 The two contractions for C-reducibility of conf(1) are
 C 1 = { v 0 ​ v 6 , v 6 ​ v 7 , v 7 ​ v 2 , v 5 ​ v 9 , v 9 ​ v 8 , v 8 ​ v 3 } C_{1}=\{v_{0}v_{6},v_{6}v_{7},v_{7}v_{2},v_{5}v_{9},v_{9}v_{8},v_{8}v_{3}\}  or  C 2 = { v 1 ​ v 6 , v 6 ​ v 9 , v 9 ​ v 5 , v 2 ​ v 7 , v 7 ​ v 8 , v 8 ​ v 4 } C_{2}=\{v_{1}v_{6},v_{6}v_{9},v_{9}v_{5},v_{2}v_{7},v_{7}v_{8},v_{8}v_{4}\}  in Figure  15 .
We may assume that all vertices of the ring are different since otherwise, the representativity of the embedding fo  G ′ G^{\prime}  would result in at most two by contracting edges of  C 1 C_{1}  or  C 2 C_{2} , which in turn means that the resulting graph would not be in  𝒫 1 ∖ 𝒫 0 \mathcal{P}_{1}\setminus\mathcal{P}_{0} . 
 
 
 We show the following two claims. 
 
 
 Claim 7.2 . 
 
 At least one of  v 0 , v 1 v_{0},v_{1}  is of degree at least 7. 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 The configurations shown in Figure  16  are D-reducible, which can be safely reduced. If both of the degrees of  v 0 , v 1 v_{0},v_{1}  are at most six, one of these configurations appears. We use the reducibility of these configurations, instead of conf(1) in this case.
∎ 
 
 
 
 We may assume that the degree of  v 0 v_{0}  is at least  7 7 . 
 
 
 Claim 7.3 . 
 
 None of the edges  v 0 ​ v 2 , v 0 ​ v 3 v_{0}v_{2},v_{0}v_{3}  exists. 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 Suppose that  v 0 ​ v i v_{0}v_{i}  ( i = 2 , 3 i=2,3 ) exists. Then, the cycle  v 0 ​ v 6 ​ v 7 ​ v i v_{0}v_{6}v_{7}v_{i}  exists. If the cycle is contractible, that would contradict Lemma  2.4 . If the cycle is noncontractible, the representativity of the resulting embedding of  G ′ / C 1 G^{\prime}/C_{1}  would result in at most 2, which certifies  ⟨ G ′ / C 1 ⟩ ≠ K 6 \langle G^{\prime}/C_{1}\rangle\neq K_{6} .
∎ 
 
 
 
 In order to finish our proof, we need to consider the following two cases. 
 
 1.  
 
 The degree of  v 0 v_{0}  is  7 7 .
 We first contract edges of  C 2 C_{2} . If a cycle of length two or three that gets rid of  v 0 v_{0}  in  G ′ / C 2 G^{\prime}/C_{2}  exists, edges of the cycle constitute a contractible cycle of length five or six, with  v 1 ​ v 6 , v 6 ​ v 9 , v 9 ​ v 5 v_{1}v_{6},v_{6}v_{9},v_{9}v_{5}  in  G ′ G^{\prime} . This implies that there is a contractible cycle of length four or five that contains  v 0 ​ v 1 , v 0 ​ v 5 v_{0}v_{1},v_{0}v_{5} . In this case, the degree of  v 0 v_{0}  must be of at most six by Lemma  2.4 , which is a contradiction. Therefore,  v 0 v_{0}  is not deleted in  G ′ / C 2 G^{\prime}/C_{2} . Unless  v 0 v_{0}  is in some 2,3-cycle in  G ′ / C 2 G^{\prime}/C_{2} , the degree of  v 0 v_{0}  is exactly  4 4  in  ⟨ G ′ / C 2 ⟩ \langle G^{\prime}/C_{2}\rangle , which certifies  ⟨ G ′ / C 2 ⟩ ≠ K 6 \langle G^{\prime}/C_{2}\rangle\neq K_{6} . Assume that  v 0 v_{0}  is in some 2,3-cycle in  G ′ / C 2 G^{\prime}/C_{2} . A set of edges of the cycle constitutes a contractible cycle of length five or six, with  v 2 ​ v 7 , v 7 ​ v 8 , v 8 ​ v 4 v_{2}v_{7},v_{7}v_{8},v_{8}v_{4}  in  G ′ G^{\prime} , so the distance between  v 0 v_{0}  and  v 2 v_{2}  is one or two. The case when the distance is exactly one would contradict Claim  7.3 , so assume that the distance is exactly two. There is a cycle  v 0 ​ v 6 ​ v 7 ​ v 2 ​ w v_{0}v_{6}v_{7}v_{2}w  where  w w  is the vertex so that contractible cycle  v 2 ​ v 7 ​ v 8 ​ v 4 ​ v 0 ​ w v_{2}v_{7}v_{8}v_{4}v_{0}w  exists in  G ′ G^{\prime} .
The cycle  v 0 ​ v 6 ​ v 7 ​ v 2 ​ w v_{0}v_{6}v_{7}v_{2}w  is noncontractible since otherwise, this would contradict Lemma  2.4 . 
 
 
 We now contract edges of  C 1 C_{1} . The representativity of the resulting embedding of  G ′ / C 1 G^{\prime}/C_{1}  is at most two, so  ⟨ G ′ / C 1 ⟩ ≠ K 6 \langle G^{\prime}/C_{1}\rangle\neq K_{6} . 
 
 2.  
 
 The degree of  v 0 v_{0}  is at least  8 8 .
 We contract edges of  C 1 C_{1} . Suppose that  v 0 v_{0}  is in a contractible cycle of length five or six, that contains edges  v 3 ​ v 8 , v 8 ​ v 9 , v 9 ​ v 5 v_{3}v_{8},v_{8}v_{9},v_{9}v_{5} . By Claim  7.3 , we only need to consider the case when the cycle  v 0 ​ v 5 ​ v 9 ​ v 8 ​ v 3 ​ w v_{0}v_{5}v_{9}v_{8}v_{3}w  exists, where  w w  is the vertex so that contractible cycle  v 5 ​ v 9 ​ v 8 ​ v 3 ​ w ​ v 0 v_{5}v_{9}v_{8}v_{3}wv_{0}  exists. If the cycle is contractible, the cycle  v 0 ​ v 6 ​ v 8 ​ v 3 ​ w v_{0}v_{6}v_{8}v_{3}w  would contradict Lemma  2.4 . Otherwise, the representativity of the resulting embedding of  G ′ G^{\prime}  is at most two, which is a contradiction. 
 
 
 The vertex  v 0 ( , v 2 ) v_{0}(,v_{2})  is adjacent to at least four(, two) vertices outside  S = { v 0 , … , v 9 } S=\{v_{0},\dots,v_{9}\}  since the degree of  v 0 ( , v 2 ) v_{0}(,v_{2})  is at least eight(, five). Hence the degree of  v 0 v_{0}  in  G ′ / C 1 G^{\prime}/C_{1}  is at least eight. Suppose  ⟨ G ′ / C 1 ⟩ = K 6 \langle G^{\prime}/C_{1}\rangle=K_{6} . Then a contractible cycle of length five or six that contains edges of  v 0 ​ v 6 , v 6 ​ v 7 , v 7 ​ v 2 v_{0}v_{6},v_{6}v_{7},v_{7}v_{2}  in  G ′ G^{\prime} , which would result in a cycle of length two or three in  G ′ / C 1 G^{\prime}/C_{1}  that would reduce the degree of  v 0 v_{0} , exists. 
 
 
 When the length of the cycle is six, the cycle of length five that contains  v 0 ​ v 1 , v 1 ​ v 2 v_{0}v_{1},v_{1}v_{2}  exists. By Lemma  2.4 , we only need to consider the following cases: (1) two edges that incidents with vertices of the cycle exist, or (2) a vertex that is adjacent to all vertices on the cycle.
In case (1), at most one of the two edges incident with  v 0 v_{0} , or  v 2 v_{2}  exists, since the degree of  v 1 v_{1}  in  G ′ G^{\prime}  is at least five.
Therefore, the degree of  v 0 v_{0}  in  ⟨ G ′ / C 1 ⟩ \langle G^{\prime}/C_{1}\rangle  is at least six.
In case (2), it is possible that the degree of  v 0 v_{0}  in  ⟨ G ′ / C 1 ⟩ \langle G^{\prime}/C_{1}\rangle  is five. That would occur when the degree of  v 0 , v 2 v_{0},v_{2}  in  G ′ G^{\prime}  is exactly  8 , 5 8,5  respectively. Assuming  ⟨ G ′ / C 1 ⟩ = K 6 \langle G^{\prime}/C_{1}\rangle=K_{6} ,  G ′ G^{\prime}  is one of the following graphs in Figure  17 . The vertex of degree exactly four or, the configuration conf(3), whose contraction size is 1, appears in  G ′ G^{\prime} . In the second case, we use the configuration instead of conf(1). 
 
 
 Finally, when the length of the cycle is five, the vertex  w w  that is adjacent to  v 0 , v 1 , v 2 v_{0},v_{1},v_{2}  exists by Lemma  2.4 , since the degree of  v 1 v_{1}  in  G ′ G^{\prime}  is at least five. In this case, the degree of  v 1 v_{1}  in  ⟨ G ′ / C 1 ⟩ \langle G^{\prime}/C_{1}\rangle  is at least six, which certifies  ⟨ G ′ / C 1 ⟩ ≠ K 6 \langle G^{\prime}/C_{1}\rangle\neq K_{6} . 
 
 
 ∎

The bold line represents edges to be contracted. The dotted line shows the vertex of degree 4 in the left picture and conf(3) in the right picture. 
 
 
 Lemma 7.4 . 
 
 Let  C C  be a separating circuit of length  l ∈ { 6 , 7 } l\in\{6,7\}  in  G ′ G^{\prime}  bounding a disk  D D . Suppose that no edge in  G ′ ​ [ C ] G^{\prime}[C]  inside  D D  is separating vertices inside  D D . 
 
 •  
 
 If two of the connected components of  G ′ − C G^{\prime}-C  have at least  l − 2 l-2  vertices each, then a reducible configuration  K K  appears in  G ′ G^{\prime}  and the graph obtained from  G ′ G^{\prime}  by contracting edges of  c ⁡ ( K ) c(K)  is in  𝒫 1 ∖ 𝒫 0 \mathcal{P}_{1}\setminus\mathcal{P}_{0} . 
 
 •  
 
 If at most  l − 3 l-3  vertices are strictly inside the disk  D D , then  D D  is one of the graphs in Figure  18  ( l = 6 l=6 ) or Figure  19  ( l = 7 l=7 ). 
 
 
 
 
 
 Figure 18 :  The disks bounded by a separating circuit of length six. 
 
 
 Figure 19 :  The disks bounded by a separating circuit of length seven. 
 
 
 Lemma  7.4  states that one component of  G ′ G^{\prime}  divided by a circuit of length  l = 6 , 7 l=6,7  has at most  l − 3 l-3  vertices. Indeed, if the component is embedded inside the disk, we have the precise characterization.
In the rest of this section, we denote vertices of  C C  by  u i u_{i}   ( 0 ≤ i < l ) (0\leq i<l) . 
 
 
 7.1  Computer check 

 
 Now we explain the use of computer in the proof of Lemma  7.4 . In our computer check, we assume that  C C  is a cycle such that the disk bounded by  C C  is minimal (in terms of the number of vertices contained in this disk) while satisfying the first condition of Lemma  7.4  (i.e., at least  l − 2 l-2  vertices in each component of  G ′ − C G^{\prime}-C ). Note that the length of  C C  can be six or seven. The minimality condition implies all cycles of length  k k  (where  k = 6 k=6  or  7 7 ) in the disk bounded by  C C  bounds the disk with at most  k − 3 k-3  vertices strictly inside.
We use Lemma  6.1  to find a reducible configuration  W W  in the disk bounded by  C C  in  G ′ G^{\prime}  later in this section, but we have to be careful not to get  K 6 K_{6}  in  G ′ G^{\prime}  after contractions in  K K  (when  K K  is C-reducible) and the low-vertex cut reductions in the resulting graph.
We want to know whether or not low-vertex cut reductions that get rid of vertices outside the disk bounded by  C C  occur.
In order to figure out, we check the resulting distance between any two vertices of  C C  in the disk after contractions in  K K . If the distance of some two vertices of  C C  is 0 or 1, such low-vertex cut reductions may happen.
When some two vertices of  C C  become identified with each other after contractions in  K K , these two vertices are in the ring of  K K .
When some vertices on  C C  become adjacent after contractions in  K K , these two vertices are in the ring of  K K , or one of them is in the ring, and the other is adjacent to a vertex of the ring. 
 
 
 Let us explain how to check the argument above, using a specific example. In fact, this example corresponds to (6cut-1) enumerated in Table  2 . We use  d G ​ ( ⋅ , ⋅ ) d_{G}(\cdot,\cdot)  to denote the distance between two vertices in a graph  G G . We sometimes omit  G G  when the underlying graph  G G  is clear.
Let us assume  | C | = 6 |C|=6  and  d ⁡ ( u 0 , u 2 ) = d ⁡ ( u 3 , u 5 ) = 0 d(u_{0},u_{2})=d(u_{3},u_{5})=0  after contraction in  K K .
Note that these four vertices belong to a ring of  K K .
For each configuration  K ∈ 𝒦 K\in\mathcal{K} , we enumerate four vertices  a , b , c , d a,b,c,d  in the ring such that  a , b , c , d a,b,c,d  are in the clockwise order listed in the ring of  K K , and  d ⁡ ( a , b ) = d ⁡ ( c , d ) = 0 d(a,b)=d(c,d)=0  after contracting edges of  c ⁡ ( K ) c(K) .
In this case, we have several constraints that two vertices of the ring of  K K  are connected by a path of specified length outside  K K  (e.g. a path of length two or four between  a a  and  b b  exists). We check by computer whether or not all of such constraints are valid. 
 
 
 In doing so, we now explain two criteria to ensure validity of the process.
Let  K K  be a configuration. Let  S S  be a free completion of  K K  with the ring. We assume that a path  P P  of length  k k  between two vertices  u , v u,v  in the ring (and also in  C C ) exists outside  K K . The path  P P  is the part of  C C . There are two paths that connect  u u  and  v v  along the ring of  K K . One of them constitutes the cycle with  P P  that bounds the disk where no vertex of the configuration  K K  exists strictly inside. We denote the path by  Q Q  and  q = | Q | q=|Q| .
The symbol  R R  denotes one of the shortest paths between  u u  and  v v  in  S S  and  m = | R | m=|R| . We consider two cycles  D = C − P + Q D=C-P+Q  and  E = P + R E=P+R . 
 
 
 Two criteria we use are the following. 
 
 
 
 •  
 
 The cycle contained in  D D  or  E E  contradicts Lemma  2.4 . 
 
 •  
 
 The cycle contained in  D D  or  E E  contradicts the minimality of  C C , but we do not apply it if the length of  C C  is six, and the length of  D D  or  E E  is seven. 
 
 
 By definition,  D D  is a cycle.
When  E E  is not a cycle but a circuit, it is a contractible circuit, so it contains several cycles. The length of such cycles is at most  | E | − 2 |E|-2 .
In the case where  | E | = 7 |E|=7  and the length of such cycle is five, at most one vertex is strictly inside the disk bounded by  E E  by Lemma  2.4 . In other cases (i.e.  | E | ≤ 6 |E|\leq 6 ), the length of the cycle is at most four, so no vertex is strictly inside the disk bounded by  E E . Hence, we can get a contradiction by assuming  E E  is a cycle and counting the number of vertices in the disk bounded by  E E , so we assume  D D  and  E E  is a cycle later. 
 
 
 In the second criterion, we add the condition that the length of  C C  is not six, or the length of  D D  or  E E  is not seven. We need this condition in the first part of the proof in Section  7.4 . 
 
 
 We now explain the two criteria more precisely. We call the subroutine  forbiddenCycle ( u u ,  v v ,  k k ). The subroutine returns true if and only if there is a cycle that leads to a contradiction. 
 
 •  
 
 When  q = k q=k , it is possible  P P  is  Q Q , so return false. 
 
 •  
 
 When  q < k q<k , the length of  D D  is shorter than that of  C C . Since the length of  C C  is  l l , the length of  D D  is at most  l − 1 ≤ 6 l-1\leq 6 . The size of components divided by  D D  is at least four since  D D  includes all vertices of the configuration  K K  in one component and the size of the other component is larger than that of the component divided by  C C , which is at least  l − 2 ≥ 4 l-2\geq 4 .
When  l = 6 l=6 ,  D D  contradicts Lemma  2.4 . When  l = 7 l=7 ,  D D  contradicts the minimality of  C C . 
 
 •  
 
 When  q > k q>k , we use  Δ \Delta  to denote the disk bounded by  E E . We check whether or not the order of  E E  is at most  l ≤ 7 l\leq 7 . Also, we calculate the size of vertices strictly inside  Δ \Delta .
Let  s s  and  t t  be the number of vertices in  Δ \Delta  that are in the ring of  K K , and in  K K  respectively. At least  ⌈ ( s − ( k − 1 ) ) / 2 ⌉ + t \lceil(s-(k-1))/2\rceil+t  vertices are in  Δ \Delta  since some vertices in the ring may belong to  P P , and a vertex in the ring may be same as some other vertex in the ring. The order of the other component divided by  E E  is larger than that of a component divided by  C C , which is at least  l − 2 l-2 . The cycle  E E  would contradict Lemma  2.4  or the minimality of  C C  unless  C = E C=E  depending on the order of  E E  calculated. 
 
 
 
 
 We now explain another case when the criteria can be applied.
Let  K K  be a configuration, and  S S  be a free completion of  K K  with the ring. Let  u , v u,v  be two vertices in the ring. We assume  u ∈ V ⁡ ( C ) , v ∉ V ⁡ ( C ) u\in V(C),v\not\in V(C) , and  v v  is adjacent to  w w , which is not in  S S  but in  C C . We also assume that a path  P P  of length  k k  between two vertices  u , w u,w  exists outside  K K . The path  P P  is the part of  C C . There are two paths that connect  u u  and  v v  along the ring of  K K . One of them constitutes the cycle with  P + v ​ w P+vw  that bounds the disk where no vertex of the configuration is strictly inside.
We denote the path by  Q Q  and  q = | Q | q=|Q| .
The symbol  R R  denotes one of the shortest paths between  u u  and  v v  in  S S  and  m = | R | m=|R| . We use two cycles  D = C − P + Q + v ​ w , E = P + R + v ​ w D=C-P+Q+vw,E=P+R+vw . Again even if  E E  is not a cycle but a circuit, we can get a contradiction by assuming  E E  is a cycle and calculating the number of vertices.
We use the same criteria described before.
The important point here is that both  D D  and  E E  are contained in the disk bounded by  C C  since  v v  is strictly inside the disk bounded by  C C , while  v ∈ V ⁡ ( D ) , v ∈ V ⁡ ( E ) v\in V(D),v\in V(E) . This leads to a contradiction of the minimality of  C C , depending on the number calculated as before.
We call the subroutine  forbiddenCycleOneEdge ( u u ,  v v ,  k k ) used in this situation. 
 
 
 We check the combinations of the distance of vertices of  C C  in  G ′ G^{\prime}  enumerated as Table  2 , from (6cut-1) to (7cut-4). We show the visual representations of each case in Figure  20 .
The graph  H ′ H^{\prime}  represents the disk bounded by  C C , which is formally defined later.
We also show which contraction for each configuration leads to such combinations of the distances. When we use  r i r_{i}  to denote a vertex of the ring of  K K , it means  K K  appears and  r i = u i r_{i}=u_{i} . Now, we explain the kinds of subroutines called to check validity in each situation. 
 
 
 When  u i u_{i}  and  u j u_{j}  ( i ≠ j i\neq j ) belong to the ring of  K K , there are two paths of length  | i − j | |i-j| ,  l − | i − j | l-|i-j|  between  r i r_{i}  and  r j r_{j}  outside  K K .
Therefore, we run two subroutines fobiddenCycle( r i r_{i} ,  r j r_{j} ,  | i − j | |i-j| ), fobiddenCycle( r i r_{i} ,  r j r_{j} ,  l − | i − j | l-|i-j| ) for  K K . If one of them returns true, it is not valid. 
 
 
 When  u i u_{i}  belongs to the ring of a configuration  K K  and  u j u_{j}  is incident with a vertex  v v  in the ring of  K K , there are two paths of length  | i − j | , l − | i − j | |i-j|,l-|i-j|  between  r i r_{i}  and  u j u_{j} . Therefore we run two subroutines fobiddenCycleOneEdge( r i r_{i} ,  v v ,  | i − j | |i-j| ), fobiddenCycleOneEdge( r i r_{i} ,  v v ,  l − | i − j | l-|i-j| ) for  K K . If one of them returns true, it is not valid.

 Claim 7.5 . 
 
 The combinations of distance enumerated in Table  2  from (6cut-1) to (7cut-4) in  G ′ / c ⁡ ( K ) G^{\prime}/c(K)  would not occur for  K ∈ 𝒦 K\in\mathcal{K}  except  c ​ o ​ n ​ f ​ ( 1 ) conf(1) . 
 
 
 
 
 7.2  The common part of the proof for  l = 6 , 7 l=6,7 . 

 
 This section contains the common part of the proof of Lemma  7.4  for  l = 6 , 7 l=6,7 . After that, we describe a specific part of the proof for each case. 
 
 
 We consider the two cases depending on whether or not  C C  is a cycle, but we discuss the common things first. After that, we discuss the case where  C C  is not a cycle, which is easier, and the other case separately.
We obtain the graph  H ′ , H ′′ H^{\prime},H^{\prime\prime}  in the following way whether  C C  is a cycle or not. 
 
 
 
 (i)  
 
 The case that  C C  is a cycle. 
 When  C C  is a cycle,  C C  is contractible. There are two graphs obtained from  G ′ G^{\prime}  divided by  C C . One of them is planar, and the other one is projective planar. Let  A , B A,B  be the planar, and the projective planar one respectively. The graph  H ′ H^{\prime}  is  A + C A+C ,  H ′′ H^{\prime\prime}  is  B + C B+C . When  | A | ≥ l − 2 |A|\geq l-2 , we choose  C C  such that  C C  is minimal subject to this condition. (i.e. no cycle of length  k ( = 6 , or 7 ) k(=6,\text{or}7)  contained in the disk bounded by  C C  bounds the disk at least  k − 2 k-2  vertices are strictly inside.). 
 
 (ii)  
 
 The case that  C C  is not a cycle. 
 When  C C  contains no noncontractible cycle,  C C  consists of several cycles of length at most five. That contradicts the hypothesis that no proper subset of  C C  is separating. 
 
 
 When  C C  contains a noncontractible cycle, the vertex  u i u_{i}  cannot be same as  u i + 1 u_{i+1} , nor  u i + 2 u_{i+2}  since it implies that the representativity of the embedding of  G ′ G^{\prime}  is at most two, which contradicts the assumption that the representativity of  G ′ G^{\prime}  is at least three.
We assume  u 0 = u 3 u_{0}=u_{3}  without loss of generality.
Suppose that other two vertices  u i , u j u_{i},u_{j}  of  C C  are same such that  0 < i < 3 < j 0<i<3<j . When  i = 1 , j = 4 i=1,j=4 , circuit  u 0 ​ u 5 ​ u 1 ​ u 2 u_{0}u_{5}u_{1}u_{2}  for  l = 6 l=6 , or  u 0 ​ u 5 ​ u 5 ​ u 1 ​ u 2 u_{0}u_{5}u_{5}u_{1}u_{2}  for  l = 7 l=7  is separating. Otherwise, the circuit which consists of the walk from  u i u_{i}  to  u 0 u_{0}  in  C C  and the walk from  u j u_{j}  to  u 3 u_{3}  in  C C  is separating.
This contradicts the hypothesis that no proper subset of  C C  is separating.
Therefore, we can assume a circuit  C C  such that  u 0 = u 3 u_{0}=u_{3}  and no other vertices are same.
There are two planar graphs obtained from  G ′ G^{\prime}  by deleting  C C .
Let  A , B A,B  be the planar graphs such that  | V ⁡ ( A ) | ≤ | V ⁡ ( B ) | |V(A)|\leq|V(B)| . The graph  H ′ H^{\prime}  is  A + C A+C ,  H ′′ H^{\prime\prime}  is  B + C B+C .
The graph  H ′ H^{\prime}  is not planar, but splitting the vertex  u 0 ( = u 3 ) u_{0}(=u_{3})  into  u 0 ′ u^{\prime}_{0}  and  u 3 ′ u^{\prime}_{3}  such that  u 0 ′ u^{\prime}_{0}  is adjacent to  u 5 , u 1 u_{5},u_{1}  and  u 3 ′ u^{\prime}_{3}  is adjacent to  u 2 , u 4 u_{2},u_{4}  makes  H ′ H^{\prime}  a planar near-triangulation. Now, we consider the four cases regarding  H ′ H^{\prime} . Note that when we refer to  H ′ H^{\prime}  in the case analysis below, we use the planar near-triangulation obtained in this way instead of  H ′ H^{\prime} . 
 
 
 
 
 The graph  H ′ H^{\prime}  can be regarded as an internally 6-connected near-triangulation in the plane, and  C C  is the cycle of  H ′ H^{\prime}  that bounds the infinite region of  H ′ H^{\prime} .
We have the following four cases. Three of them are easy. The last one is the most difficult. 
 
 1.  
 
 C C  is not an induced cycle in  H ′ H^{\prime} .
 There are two kinds of chords in  C C : a chord between two vertices of distance two or three in  C C .
A chord between two vertices of distance two(, or three) implies  C C  consists of a cycle of length  l − 1 l-1 (,  l − 2 l-2 ) and of length three(, four), respectively. The cycles are also in  G ′ G^{\prime} . We use Lemma  2.4  or the minimality of  C C . In case (ii), by the hypothesis  | V ⁡ ( A ) | ≤ | V ⁡ ( B ) | |V(A)|\leq|V(B)| ,  H ′ H^{\prime}  is characterized. In case (i), we can characterize  H ′ H^{\prime}  unless  l = 7 l=7  and a chord exists between two vertices of distance two. However, if that happens, a cycle of length six contradicts the minimality of  C C . 
 
 2.  
 
 Case 1 does not occur, and there is a vertex in  H ′ − C H^{\prime}-C  which is adjacent to at least four consecutive vertices of  C C .
 A cycle of length  l − 1 l-1  exists in  H ′ H^{\prime} . That characterizes  H ′ H^{\prime}  by the same discussion as in case 1. 
 
 3.  
 
 Neither case 1 nor 2 occurs, and there are at most  18 5 ​ l − 12 \frac{18}{5}l-12  edges between  C C  and  H ′ − C H^{\prime}-C .
 When  l = 6 l=6 , there are at most nine edges between  C C  and  H ′ − C H^{\prime}-C . By the condition that neither case 1 nor 2, there is only one possibility of  H ′ H^{\prime} , which is the last one in Figure  18 .
When  l = 7 l=7 , there are at most  13 13  edges between  C C  and  H ′ − C H^{\prime}-C . By the condition that neither case 1 nor 2 occurs, there is a cycle of length at most six in  H ′ H^{\prime} , which characterizes  H ′ H^{\prime} . We have several candidates for  H ′ H^{\prime} . When a reducible configuration appears in  H ′ H^{\prime} , we go to case 4 (and we deal with this situation there). Only one case remains, which is the last one in Figure  19 . 
 
 4.  
 
 Neither case 1 nor 2 occurs, and there are more than  18 5 ​ l − 12 \frac{18}{5}l-12  edges between  C C  and  H ′ − C H^{\prime}-C .
 We use Lemma  6.1 . We can find a reducible configuration in  H ′ H^{\prime} . We get a smaller graph and apply induction by contracting several edges of the reducible configuration.
However, we have to be careful not to get a self-loop or  K 6 K_{6}  after low-vertex cut reductions by contracting edges. (A self-loop corresponds to a bridge, and  K 6 K_{6}  corresponds to the Petersen graph in  G G .) We discuss this case below. 
 
 
 In cases 1,2,3, we know the characterization of  H ′ H^{\prime} . We enumerate all characterizations in Figure  18 ,  19 . All graphs in Figure  18 ,  19  have at most  l − 3 l-3  vertices excluding vertices of the cycle. 
 
 
 Now we discuss case 4. All of our reducible configurations have at least four vertices, so the number of vertices in  H ′ H^{\prime}  excluding  C C  is at least four. When  l = 7 l=7 , only conf(1) of our configurations has just 4 vertices, but we have already handled conf(1) in Lemma  7.1 . So we assume that at least five vertices are in  H ′ H^{\prime}  excluding  C C . Also, we do not have to consider the case when the number of vertices in  G ′ − H ′ G^{\prime}-H^{\prime}  is at most  l − 3 l-3  in case (i) since  G ′ − H ′ G^{\prime}-H^{\prime}  is projective planar. We can also assume the same thing in case (ii) by the hypothesis  | V ⁡ ( A ) | ≤ | V ⁡ ( B ) | |V(A)|\leq|V(B)| . In what follows, we assume that 
 
 G ′ − H ′ G^{\prime}-H^{\prime}  has at least  l − 2 l-2  vertices. 
 
 
 
 We enumerate all of the possible low-vertex-cut reductions after contracting edges. A contractible cycle of length two or three after contracting edges leads to a low-vertex cut reduction.
The edges to be contacted are only in  H ′ H^{\prime}  and not the edges of  C C  by Lemma  6.1 .
We only consider a 2,3-vertex cut that contains vertices of  G ′ ∖ H ′ G^{\prime}\setminus H^{\prime} .
We show an example. Let  a , b a,b  be two vertices of  C C .
When the path between  a a  and  b b  in  H ′ H^{\prime}  after contracting edges and the path between  a a  and  b b  in  H ′′ H^{\prime\prime}  constitutes a contractible cycle of length two or three, then a low-vertex cut reduction happens.
Therefore, we analyze how two vertices of  C C  are connected in  H ′′ H^{\prime\prime} . We can easily show the following claims. 
 
 
 Claim 7.6 . 
 
 A chord between two vertices of  C C  on  H ′′ H^{\prime\prime} , which possibly becomes a 2,3-vertex cut after contraction, exists only when  l = 7 l=7  and the distance between endpoints of the chord is exactly two in  C C . 
 
 
 
 The chord that is not described above leads to 2,3,4, or 5-vertex-cut in  G ′ G^{\prime} , which contradicts Lemma  2.4  so Claim  7.6  holds. 
 
 
 The result of the computer check in Section  9  implies the following claim. 
 
 
 Claim 7.7 . 
 
 For a C-reducible configuration  K ∈ 𝒦 K\in\mathcal{K} , assume that  K K  appears in  G ′ G^{\prime} , and a loop appears after contracting edges of  c ⁡ ( K ) c(K) . Then, a contractible cycle of length  k ∈ { 6 , 7 } k\in\{6,7\}  with the following properties exists. 
 
 •  
 
 The cycle contains an edge in  G ′ G^{\prime}  that becomes a loop after contraction, and 
 
 •  
 
 the cycle separates  G ′ G^{\prime}  into two components, each of which has at least  k − 2 k-2  vertices. 
 
 
 
 
 
 Claim 7.8 . 
 
 After the contraction of the edges of a configuration that appears in the disk bounded by the cycle  C C  of length 6 or 7,  G ′ G^{\prime}  becomes a loopless graph. 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 The edges to be contracted are strictly contained in the disk bounded by  C C . So the edge in  G ′ G^{\prime}  that becomes a loop after contraction must be one of the following: (i) an edge that yields a chord of  C C  in  H ′′ H^{\prime\prime} , (ii) an edge that is contained in the disk bounded by  C C . Let  e e  denote the edge described in (i) or (ii).
A cycle in  G ′ G^{\prime}  that contains  e e  and other edges of the cycle are contracted, must exist in both the cases, which is denoted by  D D .
By Claim 7.7 ,  D D  is of length  k ( = 6 , 7 ) k(=6,7)  and separates  G ′ G^{\prime}  into two components, each of which has at least  k − 2 k-2  vertices.
In case (i),  e e  is a chord between two vertices of distance exactly two in  C C  by Claim 7.6 . The edges of  D D , except  e e , are strictly contained in the disk bounded by  C C . Hence the order of vertices strictly inside the disk bounded  D D  is smaller than that of  C C , which contradicts the minimality of  C C .
In case (ii), the contradiction occurs in the same way as case (i).
∎ 
 
 
 
 Indeed, this is the only place that we need the minimality of  C C  for the proof of Lemma  7.4  (i.e, we take the cycle  C C  that satisfies the second condition in Lemma  7.4 , and subject to that, the number of vertices inside the disk  D D  bounded by  C C  is as small as possible). Thus we may obtain a new cycle  C ′ C^{\prime}  that would lead to a contradiction to the minimality of  C C  only when Claim  7.6  occurs. 
 
 
 By the same argument as used in the proof of Claim  7.8 , we can justify using Lemma  7.4  to prove Lemma  3.16  in Section  3.1 . 
 
 
 Claim 7.9 . 
 
 Assuming that a loop does not appear after contraction in  G ′ G^{\prime} ,  u i u_{i}  and  u i + 1 u_{i+1}  are not identified after contraction. 
 
 
 
 The following claims show the criteria to detect  ⟨ G ′ / c ⁡ ( K ) ⟩ \langle G^{\prime}/c(K)\rangle  is  K 6 K_{6}  easily. Let us remind that  C C  is of length  l = 6 , 7 l=6,7 . 
 
 
 Claim 7.10 . 
 
 Assume that  C C  is a cycle in  G ′ G^{\prime} . In order for the resulting graph of  G ′ G^{\prime}  to be  K 6 K_{6} , at least  2 ​ l − 8 2l-8  vertices of  C C  and  G ′ − H ′ G^{\prime}-H^{\prime}  must be identified by contraction or deleted by low-vertex cut reductions. 
 
 
 
 The number of vertices in  C C  is  l l  and at least  l − 2 l-2  vertices are in  G ′ − H ′ G^{\prime}-H^{\prime}  by the assumption, so at least  2 ​ l − 2 2l-2  vertices are in  G ′ G^{\prime} . The order of  K 6 K_{6}  is six, so Claim  7.10  holds.
We use the following claim to count the number of vertices identified by contraction. 
 
 
 Claim 7.11 . 
 
 Let  D D  be a cycle of length  k k   ( 3 ≤ k ≤ 7 ) (3\leq k\leq 7)  in  G ′ G^{\prime} . The maximum number of vertices in  D D  that are identified by contraction, such that no adjacent vertices of  D D  are identified, is  ⌊ k / 2 ⌋ − 1 \lfloor k/2\rfloor-1 . 
 
 
 
 The following claim also shows the criteria to detect  ⟨ G ′ / c ⁡ ( K ) ⟩ \langle G^{\prime}/c(K)\rangle  is  K 6 K_{6} . 
 
 
 Claim 7.12 . 
 
 Let  D = d 0 ​ d 1 ​ d 2 ​ d 3 ​ d 4 D=d_{0}d_{1}d_{2}d_{3}d_{4}  be a contractible cycle in  G ′ G^{\prime}  such that the number of vertices that are strictly contained in the disk  Δ \Delta  bounded by  D D  is at least two. By Lemma  2.4 , the edge  d i ​ d i + 2 d_{i}d_{i+2}  passes through the crosscap for each  i i ,  0 ≤ i < 5 0\leq i<5 . 
 
 
 If  d ⁡ ( d i , d i + 2 ) d(d_{i},d_{i+2})  in  G ′ G^{\prime}  is at most one in  Δ \Delta  after contraction and low-vertex cut reductions,  G ′ G^{\prime}  does not become  K 6 K_{6} . 
 
 
 
 We finished explaining the common things of cases (i) and (ii).
In the rest of this section, the symbol  K K  denotes the configuration that appears in  H ′ H^{\prime} . 
 
 
 
 7.3  6-cuts 

 
 Proof of Lemma  7.4  for  l = 6 l=6 . 
 
 We will first discuss the case when  C C  is not a cycle.
From the discussion above,  C C  is a noncontractible closed walk so that  u 0 = u 3 u_{0}=u_{3}  and other vertices are distinct.
Suppose  ⟨ G ′ / c ⁡ ( K ) ⟩ = K 6 \langle G^{\prime}/c(K)\rangle=K_{6} .
No two vertices of  C C  are of distance at most one and constitute a 2,3-vertex cut with a path in  H ′′ H^{\prime\prime}  since the representativity of the resulting embedding of  G ′ G^{\prime}  becomes at most two if that happens.
Therefore, no 2,3-vertex cut that contains vertices of  G ′ − H ′ G^{\prime}-H^{\prime}  appears. By the hypothesis that there are at least four vertices in  G ′ − H ′ G^{\prime}-H^{\prime} , the number of vertices of  H ′ H^{\prime}  must become two, but at least three vertices remain after contraction by Claim  7.11 . 
 
 
 Suppose now that  C C  is a cycle.

We show the claims about how two vertices of  C C  are connected by a path of length two in  H ′′ H^{\prime\prime} . We also use Claim  7.13  in the proof of the case when  l = 7 l=7 . 
 
 
 Claim 7.13 . 
 
 Suppose that there is a vertex  v ∈ G ′ − H ′ v\in G^{\prime}-H^{\prime}  such that  v v  is adjacent to  u i u_{i}  and  u i + 2 u_{i+2}  and there is a contractible 4-cycle  v ​ u i ​ u i + 1 ​ u i + 2 vu_{i}u_{i+1}u_{i+2} . The cycle bounds a disk that has no vertex strictly inside since it is impossible that the cycle bounds a disk that contains  H ′ H^{\prime}  by Lemma  2.4 . By Claim  7.6 , the edge  v ​ u i + 1 vu_{i+1}  exists. 
 
 
 
 Claim 7.14 . 
 
 Suppose that there is a vertex  v ∈ G ′ − H ′ v\in G^{\prime}-H^{\prime}  such that  v v  is adjacent to  u i u_{i}  and  u i + 3 u_{i+3}  and there are two contractible 5-cycles:  v ​ u i ​ u i + 1 ​ u i + 2 ​ u i + 3 vu_{i}u_{i+1}u_{i+2}u_{i+3}  and  v ​ u i + 3 ​ u i + 4 ​ u i + 5 ​ u i vu_{i+3}u_{i+4}u_{i+5}u_{i} . There are at most two vertices in  G ′ − H ′ G^{\prime}-H^{\prime}  to meet Lemma  2.4 . That contradicts the hypothesis that at least four vertices are in  G ′ − H ′ G^{\prime}-H^{\prime} . 
 
 
 
 Based on the claims above, we divide the proof into the following five cases. We set  i i  as some value between  0 0  and  5 5  in the cases. We describe the cases in Figure  21 . 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 ((a))   The two cases explained in case (b). 
  
 
 ((b))   The case explained in case (c). 
 
 
 
 
 ((c))   The two cases explained in case (d). 
 
 
 
 Figure 21 :  The figures that describe the case analysis in Section  7.3 . The hexagon represents  C C , where  u i u_{i}  is the vertex on the upper left,  u i + 1 , u i + 2 , … u_{i+1},u_{i+2},...  are in counter-clockwise order. The bold line represents the path that consists of contraction edges in  H ′ H^{\prime} . The dotted line represents the path in  H ′′ H^{\prime\prime} . 
 
 
 
 (a)  
 
 There are no 2,3-vertex cuts that contain vertices of  G ′ − H ′ G^{\prime}-H^{\prime} .
 The graph  G ′ G^{\prime}  does not become  K 6 K_{6}  after contraction since at most two vertices are identified by Claim  7.11 , but at least four vertices must be deleted or identified by Claim  7.9 . 
 
 (b)  
 
 A path of length two between  u i u_{i}  and  u i + 2 u_{i+2}  in  H ′′ H^{\prime\prime}  exists, and a cycle of length two, or three that contains the path appears after contraction. No other 2,3-vertex cut appears.
 The vertex that is adjacent to  u i , u i + 2 u_{i},u_{i+2}  is denoted by  v v . The edge  v ​ u i + 1 vu_{i+1}  exists by Claim  7.13 . 
 
 
 When  u i + 2 u_{i+2}  is identified with  u i u_{i}  and the cycle  v ​ u i vu_{i}  appears after contraction,  u i + 1 u_{i+1}  is deleted. In addition, at most one vertex is identified by Claim  7.11 , so at most three vertices are deleted or identified, so  ⟨ G ′ / c ⁡ ( K ) ⟩ ≠ K 6 \langle G^{\prime}/c(K)\rangle\neq K_{6}  by Claim  7.10 . 
 
 
 When the cycle  v ​ u i ​ u i + 2 vu_{i}u_{i+2}  appears after contraction,  u i + 1 u_{i+1}  are deleted. In addition, at most one vertex is identified by Claim  7.11 , so at most two vertices are deleted or identified, so  ⟨ G ′ / c ⁡ ( K ) ⟩ ≠ K 6 \langle G^{\prime}/c(K)\rangle\neq K_{6}  by Claim  7.10 . 
 
 (c)  
 
 A path of length three between  u i u_{i}  and  u i + 2 u_{i+2}  in  H ′′ H^{\prime\prime}  exists, and a cycle of length three that contains the path appears after contraction. No other 2,3-cut appear.
 The path is denoted by  u i ​ v 1 ​ v 2 ​ u i + 2 u_{i}v_{1}v_{2}u_{i+2} . In this case  u i + 2 u_{i+2}  is identified with  u i u_{i} .
The cycle  u i ​ u i + 1 ​ u i + 2 ​ v 2 ​ v 1 u_{i}u_{i+1}u_{i+2}v_{2}v_{1}  exists, so  ⟨ G ′ / c ⁡ ( K ) ⟩ ≠ K 6 \langle G^{\prime}/c(K)\rangle\neq K_{6}  by Claim  7.12 . 
 
 
 Therefore, the cycle  u i ​ u i + 1 ​ u i + 2 ​ v 2 ​ v 1 u_{i}u_{i+1}u_{i+2}v_{2}v_{1}  bounds the planar region that consists of at most one vertex by Lemma  2.4 . The possible pairs of vertices that may be identified in this situation are  ( u i + 3 , u i + 5 ) (u_{i+3},u_{i+5})  and  ( u i , u i + 4 ) (u_{i},u_{i+4})  by Claim  7.9  but neither of them is identified by (6cut-1), (6cut-2) in Claim  7.5 . Therefore,  u i + 2 u_{i+2}  is the only vertex of  C C  that is identified, and  u i + 1 u_{i+1}  and at most one vertex of  G ′ − H ′ G^{\prime}-H^{\prime}  are deleted by the three-vertex cut. Hence at most three vertices are deleted or identified, so  ⟨ G ′ / c ⁡ ( K ) ⟩ ≠ K 6 \langle G^{\prime}/c(K)\rangle\neq K_{6}  by Claim  7.10 . 
 
 (d)  
 
 A path of length three between  u i u_{i}  and  u i + 3 u_{i+3}  in  H ′′ H^{\prime\prime}  exists, and a cycle of length three that contains the path appears after contraction.
 The path is denoted by  u i ​ v 1 ​ v 2 ​ u i + 3 u_{i}v_{1}v_{2}u_{i+3} . In this case,  u i + 3 u_{i+3}  is identified with  u i u_{i} , and no other vertices of  C C  are not identified by Claim  7.9 . Suppose  ⟨ G ′ / c ⁡ ( K ) ⟩ = K 6 \langle G^{\prime}/c(K)\rangle=K_{6} . The degree of  u i u_{i}  after contraction is five. The vertex  u i u_{i}  is adjacent to four vertices  u i + 4 , u i + 5 , v 1 , v 2 u_{i+4},u_{i+5},v_{1},v_{2}  in  ⟨ G ′ / c ⁡ ( K ) ⟩ \langle G^{\prime}/c(K)\rangle , so exactly one of the edges  v 1 ​ u i + 5 v_{1}u_{i+5}  or  v 2 ​ u i + 4 v_{2}u_{i+4}  exists. We assume that  v 1 ​ u i + 5 v_{1}u_{i+5}  exists, .w.l.o.g.
The cycle  v 1 ​ v 2 ​ u i + 3 ​ u i + 4 ​ u i + 5 v_{1}v_{2}u_{i+3}u_{i+4}u_{i+5}  exists, so  ⟨ G ′ / c ⁡ ( K ) ⟩ ≠ K 6 \langle G^{\prime}/c(K)\rangle\neq K_{6}  by Claim  7.12 . 
 
 
 When another 2,3-vertex cut exists, this is a cycle of length three that contains another path  u i ​ v 3 ​ v 4 ​ u i + 3 u_{i}v_{3}v_{4}u_{i+3}  in  H ′′ H^{\prime\prime}  such that the cycle  v 1 ​ v 2 ​ u i + 3 ​ v 4 ​ v 3 ​ u i v_{1}v_{2}u_{i+3}v_{4}v_{3}u_{i}  bounds the disk that contains  H ′ H^{\prime}  by Claim  7.9  and Claim  7.14 . (If  v 1 v_{1}  or  v 2 v_{2}  equals  v 3 v_{3}  or  v 4 v_{4} , this contradicts Lemma  2.4  or the representativity becomes at most two after contraction, so we do not have to deal with these cases). In the same discussion before, exactly one of the edges  v 1 ​ v 3 v_{1}v_{3}  or  v 2 ​ v 4 v_{2}v_{4}  exists. We assume that  v 1 ​ v 3 v_{1}v_{3}  exists, w.l.o.g. The cycle  v 1 ​ v 2 ​ u i + 3 ​ v 4 ​ v 3 v_{1}v_{2}u_{i+3}v_{4}v_{3}  exists, so  ⟨ G ′ / c ⁡ ( K ) ⟩ ≠ K 6 \langle G^{\prime}/c(K)\rangle\neq K_{6}  by Claim  7.12 . 
 
 (e)  
 
 The number of 2,3-vertex cuts that contain vertices of  G ′ − H ′ G^{\prime}-H^{\prime}  is at least two.
 From the discussion before, pairs of vertices that are connected by a path in  H ′′ H^{\prime\prime} , which becomes 2,3-cut are
(I)  ( u i , u i + 2 ) , ( u i + 2 , u i + 4 ) , ( u i + 4 , u i + 6 ) (u_{i},u_{i+2}),(u_{i+2},u_{i+4}),(u_{i+4},u_{i+6}) ,
(II)  ( u i , u i + 2 ) , ( u i + 2 , u i + 4 ) (u_{i},u_{i+2}),(u_{i+2},u_{i+4}) , or
(III)  ( u i , u i + 2 ) , ( u i + 3 , u i + 5 ) (u_{i},u_{i+2}),(u_{i+3},u_{i+5}) .
Suppose  ⟨ G ′ / c ⁡ ( K ) ⟩ = K 6 \langle G^{\prime}/c(K)\rangle=K_{6} . 
 
 
 When the vertices of  C C  that remain in  ⟨ G ′ / c ⁡ ( K ) ⟩ \langle G^{\prime}/c(K)\rangle  are contained in a triangle, there is a noncontractible cycle in  ⟨ G ′ / c ⁡ ( K ) ⟩ ( = K 6 ) \langle G^{\prime}/c(K)\rangle(=K_{6})  that consists of vertices that were originally in  G ′ − H ′ G^{\prime}-H^{\prime} .
A path of length two(, three) between  u i , u i + 2 u_{i},u_{i+2}  constitutes a four(, five) -cycle with the path  u i ​ u i + 1 ​ u i + 2 u_{i}u_{i+1}u_{i+2} , so 2,3-vertex cut that contains such a path becomes at most one(, two) edges that incident with vertices of  G ′ − H ′ G^{\prime}-H^{\prime}  respectively.
Therefore, unless three paths of length three exist in case (I), which is excluded by (6cut-2) of Claim  7.5 , at most five edges that incident with vertices of  G ′ − H ′ G^{\prime}-H^{\prime}  are deleted.
By combining the fact that the degree of all vertices of  K 6 K_{6}  is five, the noncontractible cycle of length three, whose sum of degree is at most 15 exists in  G ′ G^{\prime} . In fact,  G G  belongs to  Γ 3 k ​ ( 3 ≤ k ≤ 8 ) \Gamma_{3}^{k}(3\leq k\leq 8) . We already handled  Γ 3 k \Gamma_{3}^{k}  in Section  4 . 
 
 
 Otherwise, two 3-vertex cuts each of which contains a path in  H ′′ H^{\prime\prime}  of length two appear in cases (II), and (III). In these cases, at most three vertices are deleted or identified so  ⟨ G ′ / c ⁡ ( K ) ⟩ ≠ K 6 \langle G^{\prime}/c(K)\rangle\neq K_{6}  by Claim  7.10 . 
 
 
 ∎ 
 
 
 
 
 7.4  7-cuts 

 
 Proof of Lemma  7.4  for  l = 7 l=7 . 
 
 When a chord of  C C  exists in  H ′′ H^{\prime\prime} , the distance between endpoints of the chord in  C C  is exactly two by Claim  7.6 , so there is a cycle of length six. In this case, we apply the same case analysis explained in Section 7.3  to this cycle.
The different point from Section 7.3  is that the cycle is not minimal since  C C  is minimal. However, we use the minimality only when we execute computer checks. When we check the cases (6cut-1), and (6cut-2), we do not use the minimality of cycles of length seven. Hence we can apply the same case analysis here. 
 
 
 We will first discuss the case when  C C  is not a cycle.

Pairs of vertices of  C C  so that the distance between them in  H ′ H^{\prime}  may possibly become at most one and constitutes a contractible cycle by combining a path in  H ′′ H^{\prime\prime}  after contraction, are  ( u 4 , u 6 ) (u_{4},u_{6})  or  ( u 0 , u 5 ) (u_{0},u_{5})  since the representativity becomes at most two if other pair of vertices is at distance one.
These pairs of vertices are not identified for the same reason.
When a vertex  v ∈ H ′′ v\in H^{\prime\prime}  is adjacent to  u 4 , u 6 u_{4},u_{6} , the edge  v ​ u 5 vu_{5}  exists.
When the cycle  v ​ u 4 ​ u 6 vu_{4}u_{6}  appears after contraction,  u 5 u_{5}  is deleted and in addition, at most two vertices are deleted by Claim  7.11 , so at most three vertices are deleted or identified. It is same for the case  ( u 0 , u 5 ) (u_{0},u_{5}) .
When no 2,3-vertex cut appears, at most two vertices are deleted by Claim  7.11 . In each case,  ⟨ G ′ / c ⁡ ( K ) ⟩ ≠ K 6 \langle G^{\prime}/c(K)\rangle\neq K_{6}  since at least five vertices of  C C  and  G ′ − H ′ G^{\prime}-H^{\prime}  must be deleted or identified.

Therefore, unless paths of length three in  H ′′ H^{\prime\prime}  exist between  u i + 3 u_{i+3}  and  u i + 5 u_{i+5}  and between  u i + 5 u_{i+5}  and  u i + 7 u_{i+7}  in case (I), which is excluded by (7cut-1) in Claim  7.5 , at most five edges incident with vertices of  G ′ − H ′ G^{\prime}-H^{\prime}  are deleted.

When the number is two, case (II), (III), (V), or (VI). In these cases, at most five(, five, three, or three) vertices are deleted or identified so  ⟨ G ′ / c ⁡ ( K ) ⟩ ≠ K 6 \langle G^{\prime}/c(K)\rangle\neq K_{6}  by Claim  7.10 .
