 We execute a discharge algorithm for  d = 7 , 8 , 9 , 10 , 11 d=7,8,9,10,11  and confirm returned set  𝒲 = ∅ \mathcal{W}=\emptyset .
The algorithm described here is a simplified version of a discharging algorithm implemented.
We explain some detailed implementation here.
We use the common result of Algorithm 8  when we execute a discharging algorithm for  d = 7 , 8 , 9 , 10 , 11 d=7,8,9,10,11 . We calculate the result of Algorithm 8  in advance to reduce time.
Also, we execute a discharging program in parallel. First, we enumerate wheels by searching all combinations of degrees of neighbors of hub. The range of hub degree is from  7 7  to  11 11  and the range of neighbors of the hub is  5 , 6 , 7 , 8 , 5,6,7,8, or  [ 9 , ∞ ] [9,\infty] . After enumerating wheels, we execute a discharging program for each wheel in parallel. 
 
 
 We explain the pruning method of searching for combinations of degrees of cartwheel in the discharging algorithm. We adopt three methods. First method is pruning a cartwheel that contains a reducible configuration for each loop as described in Algorithm  9 . Second method is pruning the cartwheel when an edge  v ​ w vw  has already overlapped some range-configuration  G G  with  ( G , s , t , n ) (G,s,t,n)  and the amount of charge that is sent along an edge  v ​ w vw  is larger than  n n  for some neighbor  v v . By overlapping a range-configuration  G G  with  ( G , s , t , n ) (G,s,t,n) , we search the case the amount of charge is exactly  n n , so if it is larger than  n n , we do not need to search it. We search it by overlapping another conf. Third method is to prune the cartwheel so that the amount of charge accumulated in the hub is already known as at most 0. We calculate the largest possible amount of charge that can be sent from  v v  for each neighbor. Also, we calculate the amount of charge that is sent from  w w  to  v v  for each neighbor only by decided degree. The largest possible value of the charge is calculated by these information and initial charge of the hub. If the value is at most 0, we prune it. 
 
 
 
 C.4  Pseudo code for checking cycle of length 6, or 7 

 
 In Section  7.1 , we use two subroutines  forbiddenCycle ,  forbiddenCycleOneEdge . We show the pseudo-code that corresponds to these subroutines in Algorithm  10 ,  11 . Note that we have to implement other codes that enumerate all tuples of vertices that have a specified distance after contracting edges in its free completion of the configuration as in Table  2 . This is easy to implement since the task is calculating the shortest path distance, so we omit it here.
We give a few remarks on the Algorithm  10 ,  11 . The symbol  K K  denotes a configuration, and  S S  denotes free completion of  K K  with its ring.
We calculate the number of vertices in the Algorithm as we discussed in Section  7.1 . For example, in line 12 in Algorithm  10 ,  x = ⌈ ( s − ( k − 1 ) ) / 2 ⌉ + t x=\lceil(s-(k-1))/2\rceil+t , where  s , t s,t  is the number of vertices of the component of  S − R S-R  such that  P + R P+R  bounds in the ring of  K K , and in  K K  respectively. We use the same calculation method in other parts of the Algorithms.
Another remark is about the choice of  Q Q .
The choice of  Q Q  depends on the assumption that how  P P  is embedded outside  K K .
For example, (6cut-1) in Table  2 , we enumerate four vertices  a , b , c , d a,b,c,d  in the ring such that  a , b , c , d a,b,c,d  are in the clockwise order listed in the ring of  K K , and  d ⁡ ( a , b ) = d ⁡ ( c , d ) = 0 d(a,b)=d(c,d)=0  after contracting edges of  c ⁡ ( K ) c(K) .
One of the subroutines we call is forbiddenCycle( K K ,  a a ,  b b , 2, 6). There are two candidates for  Q Q . In this case, we choose a candidate that does not contain  c , d c,d  as  Q Q .
Another subroutine we call is forbiddenCycle( K K ,  b b ,  a a , 4, 6). In this case, we choose the path that contains  c , d c,d  as  Q Q . These choices come from the assumption that there are paths of length 2, 1, 2, 1 between  a a  and  b b ,  b b  and  c c ,  c c  and  d d ,  d d  and  a a  respectively such that these paths constitute a cycle of length 6 that surrounds  K K . In our practical implementation, we distinguish two choices of  Q Q  by switching  u u  and  v v .
The last remark is about the condition of 7cut. The condition of 7cut is used only when  cutsize   = 7 =7  since we cannot always use the minimality condition of 7cut in checking  cutsize   = 6 =6 .
More specified information is in the first part of Section  7.4 . Please refer to it if the reader is interested. 
 
 
 Algorithm 10    forbiddenCycle( K K ,  u u ,  v v ,  k k ,  cutsize )

 C.5  Pseudocode for checking configurations with contraction size  ≥ 5 \geq 5 

 
 In section  9 , we introduce the method of proving the validity of a configuration  W W  of large contraction size by enumerating all possible outskirts of  W W  and of obtaining a smaller graph  W − W^{-}  which consists of vertices that can never be deleted by a low cut reduction.
We show the pseudocode of the algorithm that we implemented. 
 
 
 In the algorithm, we use the result of Lemma  7.4  to determine which one of the connected components separated by some vertex separation  C C  could be reduced.
If  8 ≤ | C | 8\leq|C| , both of the connected components are subject to reduction no matter what.
If  5 ≤ | C | ≤ 7 5\leq|C|\leq 7 , if the size of the connected component is larger than a certain threshold, the reduction would not occur.
The specific criteria are shown in the pseudocode below. 
 
 
 Algorithm 12    GetLowCutReducable( C 1 C_{1} ,  C 2 C_{2} ,  l l ) 
 
 
               1: 
              
              
            Two connected components  C 1 , C 2 C_{1},C_{2} , an integer  5 ≤ l ≤ 8 5\leq l\leq 8 

 
 
               2:

             if   l ≤ 5 l\leq 5   then 

 
 
               4:

               5: 
              
              
             end   if 
 
 
               6: 
              
              
             if   l ≥ 8 l\geq 8   then 

 
 
               7: 
              
              
               return   C 1 + C 2 C_{1}+C_{2} 

 
 
               8: 
              
              
             end   if 
 
 
               9: 
              
              
             if   l = 6 , min ⁡ { | C 1 | , | C 2 | } > 3 l=6,\min\{|C_{1}|,|C_{2}|\}>3  or  l = 7 , min ⁡ { | C 1 | , | C 2 | } > 4 l=7,\min\{|C_{1}|,|C_{2}|\}>4   then

               13: 
              
              
               return   C 1 + C 2 C_{1}+C_{2} 

 
 
               14: 
              
              
             end   if 
 
 
               15: 
              
              
             return  if  | C 1 | < | C 2 | |C_{1}|<|C_{2}|  then  C 1 C_{1}  else  C 2 C_{2} 

 
 
 
 
 We now check for all possible vertex separations  C C , which by contraction turns into a low-cut reduction, and obtain the set of vertices that could be reduced.
However, finding such a vertex separation  C C  is generally an exponential-time task, and we compromise by precalculating shortest distance paths.
The specific algorithm is shown below.

 Appendix D  Bigger implies smaller: Reducibility 

 
 In this section, we show that the reducibility of some configurations is a consequence of the reducibility of other configurations. 
 
 
 Let us first recall the basic notation used to define reducibility. 
 
 
 Let  I I  be an island. We add a new incident edge to each vertex of degree 2 in  I I  and denote the set of all added edges by  R R .
Let  𝒞 ∗ \mathscr{C}^{\ast}  be the set of all colorings  { 1 , … , | R | } → { 0 , 1 , 2 } \{1,\dots,|R|\}\rightarrow\{0,1,2\}  that satisfies parity.
Let  𝒞 0 \mathscr{C_{0}}  be the set of all  3 3 -edge-colorings of  R R  that are restrictions of  3 3 -edge-colorings of  I ∪ R I\cup R .
Let  𝒞 ′ \mathscr{C}^{\prime}  be the maximal consistent subset of  𝒞 ∗ − 𝒞 0 \mathscr{C}^{\ast}-\mathscr{C_{0}} .
The island  I I  is D-reducible if  𝒞 ′ = ∅ \mathscr{C}^{\prime}=\emptyset .
Recall that a set  𝒞 \mathscr{C}  of colorings  { 1 , … , k } → { 0 , 1 , 2 } \{1,\dots,k\}\rightarrow\{0,1,2\}  that satisfy parity is  consistent  if for every  κ ∈ 𝒞 \kappa\in\mathscr{C}  and every  θ ∈ { 0 , 1 , 2 } \theta\in\{0,1,2\}  there is a projective signed matching  M M  such that  κ \kappa   θ \theta -fits  M M , and  𝒞 \mathscr{C}  contains every coloring that  θ \theta -fits  M M . 
 
 
 The C-reducibility is defined analogously, except that  𝒞 ∗ \mathscr{C}^{\ast}  is a smaller set, containing only those 3-edge-colorings of  R R  that arise from 3-edge-colorings of  I ′ ∪ R I^{\prime}\cup R , see Definition  3.12 . 
 
 
 To test reducibility, one has to determine if maximal consistent subset of  𝒞 ∗ − 𝒞 0 \mathscr{C}^{\ast}-\mathscr{C_{0}}  is empty or not. This is done via the following process. 
 
 
 Let  𝒞 0 \mathscr{C}_{0}  be as above, and for  i ≥ 1 i\geq 1 , let  𝒞 \mathscr{C}  be all 3-edge-colorings of  R R  in  𝒞 ∗ \mathscr{C}^{\ast}  that are not in  ∪ j = 0 i − 1 𝒞 j \cup_{j=0}^{i-1}\mathscr{C}_{j} . The we form  𝒞 i \mathscr{C}_{i}  by taking all colorings  κ ∈ 𝒞 \kappa\in\mathscr{C}  for which there is a  θ ∈ { 0 , 1 , 2 } \theta\in\{0,1,2\}  such that for every projective signed matching  M M  of  { e ∈ R ∣ κ ⁡ ( e ) ≠ θ } \{e\in R\mid\kappa(e)\neq\theta\}  ( κ \kappa   θ \theta -fits  M M ), there is a coloring  κ ′ \kappa^{\prime}  of  R R  that  θ \theta -fits  M M  and belongs to  ∪ j = 0 i − 1 𝒞 j \cup_{j=0}^{i-1}\mathscr{C}_{j} . We will say that colorings in  𝒞 i \mathscr{C}_{i}  form level  i i  and that colorings at level 0  extend to  I I . We have the following corollary of the construction that allows us testing if a configuration is reducible. 
 
 
 Lemma D.1 . 
 
 Let  t t  be the smallest integer such that  𝒞 t = ∅ \mathscr{C}_{t}=\varnothing . Then  𝒞 ′ = 𝒞 ∗ − ∪ i = 0 t 𝒞 i \mathscr{C}^{\prime}=\mathscr{C}^{\ast}-\cup_{i=0}^{t}\mathscr{C}_{i} . 
 
 
 
 Theorem D.2 . 
 
 Suppose that  I I  is an island that has a path  a ​ b ​ c ​ d abcd  on its outer face, where vertices  b b  and  c c  have degree  2 2  in  I I . Let  J J  be the island obtained from  I I  by contracting the edge  b ​ c bc . If  I I  is reducible and in the case that it is C-reducible not both of the edges  a ​ b ab  and  c ​ d cd  are deleted, nor after deleting edges (to reduce)  b ​ c bc  is not isolated, then  J J  is also reducible. 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 Let us observe that the difference between D-reducibility and C-reducibility is only in the definition of  𝒞 ∗ \mathscr{C}^{\ast} . Here we are dealing with two configurations,  I I  and  J = I / b ​ c J=I/bc , and we will use  𝒟 ∗ \mathscr{D}^{\ast} ,  𝒟 i \mathscr{D}_{i} , and  𝒟 ′ \mathscr{D}^{\prime}  when referring to the corresponding sets of edge-colorings related to  J J . 
 
 
 Our goal is to show that  J J  is reducible. Let  Q Q  be the corresponding edge-cut in  J J . It contains an edge  q ∈ Q q\in Q  that is incident with the vertex  b ​ c bc  corresponding to the contracted edge  b ​ c bc . Note that the edge-cut of  I I  is  R = ( Q ∖ { q } ) ∪ { r b , r c } R=(Q\setminus\{q\})\cup\{r_{b},r_{c}\} , where  r b r_{b}  and  r c r_{c}  are incident with  b b  and  c c  in  I I , respectively. Let  κ ′ : Q → { 0 , 1 , 2 } \kappa^{\prime}:Q\to\{0,1,2\}  be a coloring that satisfies the parity condition, i.e.  κ ′ ∈ 𝒟 ∗ \kappa^{\prime}\in\mathscr{D}^{\ast} . Then we define a coloring  κ : R → { 0 , 1 , 2 } \kappa:R\to\{0,1,2\}  by having  κ \kappa  to agree with  κ ′ \kappa^{\prime}  on  R ∖ { r b , r c } R\setminus\{r_{b},r_{c}\} , and setting  κ ⁡ ( r b ) = κ ′ ​ ( q ) + 1 ( mod 3 ) \kappa(r_{b})=\kappa^{\prime}(q)+1\pmod{3}  and  κ ⁡ ( r c ) = κ ′ ​ ( q ) + 2 ( mod 3 ) \kappa(r_{c})=\kappa^{\prime}(q)+2\pmod{3} . Then it is clear that  κ ∈ 𝒞 ∗ \kappa\in\mathscr{C}^{\ast} . By denoting this injective correspondence by  φ : 𝒟 ∗ → 𝒞 ∗ \varphi:\mathscr{D}^{\ast}\to\mathscr{C}^{\ast} , we note 
 

 
 
 φ ⁡ ( 𝒟 ∗ ) = { κ ∈ 𝒞 ∗ ∣ κ ⁡ ( r c ) = κ ⁡ ( r b ) + 1 ( mod 3 ) } . \varphi(\mathscr{D}^{\ast})=\{\kappa\in\mathscr{C}^{\ast}\mid\kappa(r_{c})=\kappa(r_{b})+1\pmod{3}\}. 
 
 
 
 
 We now claim that for each  i ≥ 0 i\geq 0 , 
 

 
 
 𝒟 0 ∪ ⋯ ∪ 𝒟 i ⊇ φ − 1 ​ ( 𝒞 0 ∪ ⋯ ∪ 𝒞 i ) . \mathscr{D}_{0}\cup\cdots\cup\mathscr{D}_{i}\supseteq\varphi^{-1}(\mathscr{C}_{0}\cup\cdots\cup\mathscr{C}_{i}). 
 
 (4.2) 
 
 The proof will be by induction, and we first consider the base case, when  i = 0 i=0 . Suppose that  κ ′ = φ − 1 ​ ( κ ) \kappa^{\prime}=\varphi^{-1}(\kappa) , where  κ ∈ 𝒞 0 \kappa\in\mathscr{C}_{0} . If  α \alpha  is a 3-edge-coloring of  I I  that is consistent with  κ \kappa , then we have that  α ⁡ ( a ​ b ) = α ⁡ ( r c ) \alpha(ab)=\alpha(r_{c})  and  α ⁡ ( c ​ d ) = α ⁡ ( r b ) \alpha(cd)=\alpha(r_{b}) . Since  κ ⁡ ( r b ) ≠ κ ⁡ ( r c ) \kappa(r_{b})\neq\kappa(r_{c}) , it is clear that this yields a 3-edge-coloring of  J J  extending  κ ′ \kappa^{\prime} . Thus  κ ′ ∈ 𝒟 0 \kappa^{\prime}\in\mathscr{D}_{0} . This proves the base of induction. 
 
 
 For the inductive step, suppose that  φ ⁡ ( κ ′ ) = κ ∈ 𝒞 i \varphi(\kappa^{\prime})=\kappa\in\mathscr{C}_{i} . Let  M ′ M^{\prime}  be a signed projective matching that  θ \theta -fits  κ ′ \kappa^{\prime} . Let  M M  be the signed projective matching on  R R  that coincides with  M ′ M^{\prime}  on all elements that do not contain  q q . If there is an element  ( { q , r } , σ ) ∈ M ′ (\{q,r\},\sigma)\in M^{\prime} , then we replace it in  M M  with a match that is defined as follows. If  κ ′ ​ ( q ) ≠ κ ′ ​ ( r ) \kappa^{\prime}(q)\neq\kappa^{\prime}(r) , then  σ = − 1 \sigma=-1  and  κ ′ ​ ( q ) ≠ θ ≠ κ ′ ​ ( r ) \kappa^{\prime}(q)\neq\theta\neq\kappa^{\prime}(r)  since  M ′ M^{\prime}   θ \theta -fits  κ ′ \kappa^{\prime} . In this case we add to  M M  the match  ( { r b , r } , + 1 ) (\{r_{b},r\},+1)  or  ( { r c , r } , + 1 ) (\{r_{c},r\},+1) , depending on whether  κ ⁡ ( r b ) = κ ′ ​ ( r ) \kappa(r_{b})=\kappa^{\prime}(r)  or  κ ⁡ ( r c ) = κ ′ ​ ( r ) \kappa(r_{c})=\kappa^{\prime}(r) , respectively. Clearly,  M M   θ \theta -fits  κ \kappa . The other possibility is that  σ = + 1 \sigma=+1 . Then  κ ′ ​ ( q ) = κ ′ ​ ( r ) ≠ θ \kappa^{\prime}(q)=\kappa^{\prime}(r)\neq\theta  since  M ′ M^{\prime}   θ \theta -fits  M ′ M^{\prime} . In this case, we add to  M M  the match  ( { r b , r } , − 1 ) (\{r_{b},r\},-1)  or  ( { r c , r } , − 1 ) (\{r_{c},r\},-1) , depending on whether  κ ⁡ ( r c ) = θ \kappa(r_{c})=\theta  or  κ ⁡ ( r b ) = θ \kappa(r_{b})=\theta , respectively. Then, again,  M M   θ \theta -fits  κ \kappa . 
 
 
 Now, since  κ ∈ 𝒞 i \kappa\in\mathscr{C}_{i}  and  M M   θ \theta -fits  M M , there is a coloring  κ ¯ \bar{\kappa} 
that  θ \theta -fits  M M  and belongs to  𝒞 0 ∪ ⋯ ∪ 𝒞 i − 1 \mathscr{C}_{0}\cup\cdots\cup\mathscr{C}_{i-1} . By the induction hypothesis,  φ − 1 ​ ( κ ¯ ) ∈ 𝒟 0 ∪ ⋯ ∪ 𝒟 i − 1 \varphi^{-1}(\bar{\kappa})\in\mathscr{D}_{0}\cup\cdots\cup\mathscr{D}_{i-1} . Since  M ′ M^{\prime}  was arbitrary, this proves that  κ ′ ∈ 𝒟 i \kappa^{\prime}\in\mathscr{D}_{i} , and hence we get ( 4.2 ). 
 
 
 Since  I I  is reducible, we have that  𝒞 ∗ = 𝒞 0 ∪ ⋯ ∪ 𝒞 i \mathscr{C}^{\ast}=\mathscr{C}_{0}\cup\cdots\cup\mathscr{C}_{i}  for some  i i . Then we conclude that  𝒟 ∗ = 𝒟 0 ∪ ⋯ ∪ 𝒟 i \mathscr{D}^{\ast}=\mathscr{D}_{0}\cup\cdots\cup\mathscr{D}_{i} .
∎ 
 
 
 
 The situation of the island in Theorem  D.2  described on the corresponding configurations  K ⁡ ( I ) K(I)  and  K ⁡ ( J ) K(J)  is as follows. We start with the assumption that a vertex  v v  in  K ⁡ ( I ) K(I)  has degree  d d  and  γ K ⁡ ( I ) ​ ( v ) = d + 3 \gamma_{K(I)}(v)=d+3 . The corresponding configuration  K ⁡ ( J ) K(J)  is isomorphic to  K ⁡ ( I ) K(I)  except that  γ K ⁡ ( J ) ​ ( v ) = d + 2 \gamma_{K(J)}(v)=d+2 . The condition that possible C-reducibility should not delete both  a ​ b ab  and  c ​ d cd  is saying that two nonconsecutive edges incident with  v v  that lead to the ring of the free completion should not both be contracted in the triangulation. There are examples that show that this condition is necessary. 
 
 
 Corollary D.3 . 
 
 Suppose that  K K  is a configuration in  G ′ G^{\prime}  and  v ∈ V ⁡ ( K ) v\in V(K)  is such that  γ K ​ ( v ) = d K ​ ( v ) + 3 \gamma_{K}(v)=d_{K}(v)+3 . Let  x , y , z x,y,z  be the neighbors of  v v  on the ring of the free completion of  K K  and suppose that  K K  is reducible such that in the case of C-reducibility not both of the edges  v ​ x vx  and  v ​ z vz  are contracted nor  x , z x,z  are identified. Then the configuration  K ′ K^{\prime}  obtained from  K K  by changing  γ K ​ ( v ) \gamma_{K}(v)  to  γ K ​ ( v ) − 1 \gamma_{K}(v)-1  is also reducible. 
 
 
 
 Theorem  D.2  and Corollary  D.3  do not work when  γ K ​ ( v ) = d K ​ ( v ) + 2 \gamma_{K}(v)=d_{K}(v)+2 . They can be proved when  γ K ​ ( v ) = d K ​ ( v ) + 1 \gamma_{K}(v)=d_{K}(v)+1 , but we do not need this case. 
 
 
 D.1  Bigger implies smaller: revisited 

 
 Proof of Claim  3.18 
 
 
 Let  v v  be a vertex of  V 0 V_{0} . The symbol  r 1 , r 2 , r 3 r_{1},r_{2},r_{3}  denotes three vertices of the ring of  K K  that are adjacent to  v v  in the clockwise order listed along the ring.
A set  V 1 ⊆ V 0 V_{1}\subseteq V_{0}  consists of a vertex  v v  such that the edge  v ​ r 2 vr_{2}  is in  c ⁡ ( K ) c(K) .
A set  V 2 ⊆ V 0 V_{2}\subseteq V_{0}  consists of a vertex  v v  such that  r 1 , r 3 r_{1},r_{3}  is identified by the contraction  c ⁡ ( K ) c(K) .
Note that vertices of  V 2 V_{2}  do not satisfy the condition of contractions needed for applying Corollary  D.3 . Also, we need attention when  c ⁡ ( K ) c(K)  equals  { v ​ r 2 ∣ v ∈ V 1 } \{vr_{2}\mid v\in V_{1}\}  and  S ⊇ V 1 S\supseteq V_{1} . We can apply Corollary  D.3 , but we cannot get a smaller graph than a minimal counterexample by contracting edges of  c ⁡ ( K ) c(K) . Hence, we fail to apply induction. This observation leads to the choice of  𝒦 K , imply1 \mathcal{K}_{K,\text{imply1}}  defined below. 
 
 
 A set  𝒦 K , imply1 \mathcal{K}_{K,\text{imply1}}  consists of  K S K_{S}  for all  S ∩ ( V 1 ∪ V 2 ) = ∅ S\cap(V_{1}\cup V_{2})=\emptyset . A configuration in  𝒦 K , imply1 \mathcal{K}_{K,\text{imply1}}  is shown to be reducible by Corollary  D.3 .
We have to check the reducibility of  K S K_{S} , which does not belong to  𝒦 K , imply1 \mathcal{K}_{K,\text{imply1}} , but
the number of such configurations for all  K ∈ 𝒦 K\in\mathcal{K}  is more than 7000, which is too large.
Hence, we devise the following strategy.
A set  𝒦 K , 1 \mathcal{K}_{K,1}  consists of  K S K_{S}  for all  S ⊆ V 1 ∪ V 2 S\subseteq V_{1}\cup V_{2} .
The reducibility of  K S K_{S}  in  𝒦 K , 1 \mathcal{K}_{K,1}  can imply  K S ∪ T K_{S\cup T}  for all  T ⊆ V 0 ∖ ( V 1 ∪ V 2 ) T\subseteq V_{0}\setminus(V_{1}\cup V_{2})  if the constraint of the contraction is satisfied, so we check the reducibility of  K S K_{S}  in  𝒦 K , 1 \mathcal{K}_{K,1}  and check whether or not the contraction of  K S K_{S}  is valid for applying Corollary  D.3 . All configurations in  𝒦 K , 1 \mathcal{K}_{K,1}  are reducible.
A set  𝒦 K , imply2 \mathcal{K}_{K,\text{imply2}}  consists of  K S ∪ T K_{S\cup T}  for all  T ⊆ V 0 ∖ ( V 1 ∪ V 2 ) T\subseteq V_{0}\setminus(V_{1}\cup V_{2})  such that the contraction of  K S K_{S}  is valid.
A set  𝒦 K , 2 \mathcal{K}_{K,2}  consists of  K S ∪ T K_{S\cup T}  for all  T ⊆ V 0 ∖ ( V 1 ∪ V 2 ) T\subseteq V_{0}\setminus(V_{1}\cup V_{2})  such that the contraction of  K S K_{S}  is not valid.
We check the reducibility of all configurations in  𝒦 K , 2 \mathcal{K}_{K,2} . All configurations in  𝒦 K , 2 \mathcal{K}_{K,2}  are reducible. 
 
 
 A set  𝒦 imply1 , 𝒦 imply2 , 𝒦 1 , 𝒦 2 \mathcal{K}_{\text{imply1}},\mathcal{K}_{\text{imply2}},\mathcal{K}_{1},\mathcal{K}_{2}  is the union of  𝒦 K , imply1 , 𝒦 K , imply2 , 𝒦 K , 1 , 𝒦 K , 2 \mathcal{K}_{K,\text{imply1}},\mathcal{K}_{K,\text{imply2}},\mathcal{K}_{K,1},\mathcal{K}_{K,2}  for all  K ∈ 𝒦 K\in\mathcal{K}  respectively.
We use several computer checks to make sure that a configuration  K K  does not result in  K 6 K_{6}  after contraction and low-vertex-cut reductions in the previous section of this paper (e.g. Section  7.1 , Section  9 ). When doing so, we check all configurations in  𝒦 , 𝒦 1 , 𝒦 2 \mathcal{K},\mathcal{K}_{1},\mathcal{K}_{2} .
In addition, we have to check a configuration in  𝒦 imply1 , 𝒦 imply2 \mathcal{K}_{\text{imply1}},\mathcal{K}_{\text{imply2}} .
A set  𝒦 imply1 ∗ ⊆ 𝒦 imply1 \mathcal{K}_{\text{imply1}}^{\ast}\subseteq\mathcal{K}_{\text{imply1}}  is the union of  𝒦 K , imply1 \mathcal{K}_{K,\text{imply1}}  such that the contraction size of  K K  is at least  5 5 .
For  L ∈ 𝒦 imply1 ∗ L\in\mathcal{K}_{\text{imply1}}^{\ast} , we check the reducibility of  L L  and get contraction of  L L . The configuration  L L  is C-reducible, but we need contraction of  L L  to check whether or not the resulting graph results in  K 6 K_{6} .
Otherwise, we do not have to check  L L  since the contraction size of  L L  is at most 4 and hence Lemma  4.6  holds for this  L L .
We do the same thing to  𝒦 imply2 \mathcal{K}_{\text{imply2}} . In other words, we generate a set  𝒦 imply2 ∗ \mathcal{K}_{\text{imply2}}^{\ast} , and check whether or not a configuration in  𝒦 imply2 ∗ \mathcal{K}_{\text{imply2}}^{\ast}  results in  K 6 K_{6} , as above. By computer-check in Section  7.1 ,  9 , several configuration remains in  𝒦 1 , 𝒦 2 \mathcal{K}_{1},\mathcal{K}_{2} . However, they contain conf(1), since we do not check if a configuration in  𝒦 1 , 𝒦 2 , 𝒦 imply1 , 𝒦 imply2 \mathcal{K}_{1},\mathcal{K}_{2},\mathcal{K}_{\text{imply1}},\mathcal{K}_{\text{imply2}}  contains a configuration in  𝒦 \mathcal{K}  as a subgraph. The configuration conf(1) is already handled in Lemma  7.1 , so we do not need to handle them. 
 
 
 Also, we have to check all configurations in  𝒦 imply1 , 𝒦 imply2 , 𝒦 1 , 𝒦 2 \mathcal{K}_{\text{imply1}},\mathcal{K}_{\text{imply2}},\mathcal{K}_{1},\mathcal{K}_{2}  to prove Lemma  3.16 . Almost all configurations that have diameter five in  𝒦 \mathcal{K}  have a cutvertex, but we have to check a few configurations that remain.
We handle this issue by checking  K ∈ 𝒦 K\in\mathcal{K}  with a stronger constraint. The length of the path used in the proof of Lemma  3.16  does not change. We have to be careful to count the number of vertices. We use a different method from the method described in Lemma  3.16 .
We calculate the number of the vertices of the ring by choosing one vertex of  K K , which is denoted as  u u , and then counting vertices in the ring that are adjacent to  u u , and subtracting 2 from the value when the counting value is 3, subtracting 1 otherwise for safety. The subtraction is needed to ensure a vertex that has a distance of five may incident with one less ring vertices. When the counting value is 3,  u u  may also be in the same situation, so the subtraction value is 2. By this computer check, one configuration remains, which is denoted as  K K . We handle this one case by generating  K S K_{S}  for all  S ⊆ V 0 S\subseteq V_{0} , and checking  K S K_{S}  with an original constraint described in the proof of Lemma  3.16 .

 Appendix E  Small edge-cuts in minimal counterexamples 

 
 In this section, we show a proof for coloring a projective graph  G G  with a valid edge-cut  F ∈ E ⁡ ( G ) F\in E(G)  which satisfies one of the following: 
 
 
 
 •  
 
 2 ≤ | F | ≤ 4 2\leq|F|\leq 4 
 
 •  
 
 | F | = 5 |F|=5 , and neither of the two connected components of  G − F G-F  is a 5-cycle. 
 
 
 
 
 We introduce a proof for a broader theorem, speaking of islands embedded in any surface including the projective plane. 
 
 
 Theorem E.1 . 
 
 Let  I I  be an island that is embeddable to the plane so that all vertices of degree 2 are adjacent to the outerplane.
(We call such islands  disk islands .)
Let  F F  be its ring.
 I I  is reducible under a class of embeddable graphs on any surface if one of the following holds. 
 
 •  
 
 2 ≤ | F | ≤ 4 2\leq|F|\leq 4 
 
 •  
 
 | F | = 5 |F|=5  and  | V ⁡ ( I ) | > 5 |V(I)|>5 
 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 Due to the smallness of  F F , there are at most only two Kempe chains that contain the edges in  F F .
Therefore, the case where the outerplane contains one crosscap (i.e. the surface is a projective plane) is enough for the proof. 
 
 
 When  | F | = 2 , 3 |F|=2,3 , all possible ring colorings that satisfy the parity condition are equivalent.
So,  𝒞 ⁡ ( I ) \mathcal{C}(I)  must be equal to  ∅ \varnothing  or  𝒞 valid \mathcal{C}^{\text{valid}} .
If  | F | = 2 |F|=2 , let  G G  be a cubic graph obtained by connecting the two vertices in  I I  which are of degree two with a new edge, and if  | F | = 3 |F|=3 , let  G G  be a cubic graph obtained by identifying the three vertices in  I + F I+F  which are degree one.
In either case,  G G  is planar and by 4CT this graph is colorable.
Therefore,  𝒞 ⁡ ( I ) = 𝒞 valid \mathcal{C}(I)=\mathcal{C}^{\text{valid}} , and  I I  is reducible. 
 
 
 Next, when  | F | = 4 |F|=4 , let us name the four vertices of  I I  having degree 2 in clockwise order as  v 1 , v 2 , v 3 , v 4 v_{1},v_{2},v_{3},v_{4} .
We construct six cubic graphs from  I I  as follows. ( u , w u,w  are new vertices.) 
 
 
 
 •  
 
 G 12 A = I + v 1 ​ v 2 + v 3 ​ v 4 G^{A}_{12}=I+v_{1}v_{2}+v_{3}v_{4} 
 
 •  
 
 G 13 A = I + v 1 ​ v 3 + v 2 ​ v 4 G^{A}_{13}=I+v_{1}v_{3}+v_{2}v_{4} 
 
 •  
 
 G 14 A = I + v 1 ​ v 4 + v 2 ​ v 3 G^{A}_{14}=I+v_{1}v_{4}+v_{2}v_{3} 
 
 •  
 
 G 12 B = I + v 1 ​ u + v 2 ​ u + v 3 ​ w + v 4 ​ w + u ​ w G^{B}_{12}=I+v_{1}u+v_{2}u+v_{3}w+v_{4}w+uw 
 
 •  
 
 G 13 B = I + v 1 ​ u + v 3 ​ u + v 2 ​ w + v 4 ​ w + u ​ w G^{B}_{13}=I+v_{1}u+v_{3}u+v_{2}w+v_{4}w+uw 
 
 •  
 
 G 14 B = I + v 1 ​ u + v 4 ​ u + v 2 ​ w + v 3 ​ w + u ​ w G^{B}_{14}=I+v_{1}u+v_{4}u+v_{2}w+v_{3}w+uw 
 
 
 
 
 G 12 A , G 14 A , G 12 B , G 14 B G^{A}_{12},G^{A}_{14},G^{B}_{12},G^{B}_{14}  are planar and  G 13 A , G 13 B G^{A}_{13},G^{B}_{13}  are single-cross, so they are all colorable.
The multiset expression of the restriction of a coloring of  G i ​ j A G^{A}_{ij}  to  F F  must be  { { i , j , k , l } , ∅ , ∅ } \{\{i,j,k,l\},\varnothing,\varnothing\}  or  { { i , j } , { k , l } , ∅ } \{\{i,j\},\{k,l\},\varnothing\} .
The multiset expression of the restriction of a coloring of  G i ​ j B G^{B}_{ij}  to  F F  must be  { { i , k } , { j , l } , ∅ } \{\{i,k\},\{j,l\},\varnothing\}  or  { { i , l } , { j , k } , ∅ } \{\{i,l\},\{j,k\},\varnothing\} .
Note that there are four unique (in the sense that they are pairwise nonequivalent) valid  F F -colorings.
Since the intersection of  𝒞 ⁡ ( I ) \mathcal{C}(I)  and the coloring pair above must not be empty,  𝒞 ⁡ ( I ) \mathcal{C}(I)  must contain at least three unique elements.
The remaining unique  F F -coloring must necessarily be a ring coloring of  I I , and thus  𝒞 ∗ ​ ( I ) = 𝒞 valid \mathcal{C}^{*}(I)=\mathcal{C}^{\text{valid}} .
Therefore,  I I  is reducible. 
 
 
 Finally, when  | F | = 5 |F|=5 , we construct 21 graphs as follows. 
 
 
 
 •  
 
 G i ​ j C = I + v i ​ v j + v k ​ u + v l ​ u + v m ​ u G^{C}_{ij}=I+v_{i}v_{j}+v_{k}u+v_{l}u+v_{m}u  ( { i , j , k , l , m } = { 1 , 2 , 3 , 4 , 5 } \{i,j,k,l,m\}=\{1,2,3,4,5\} ,  i < j i<j ) 
 
 •  
 
 G i ​ j , k ​ l D = I + v i ​ u + v j ​ u + v k ​ w + v l ​ w + u ​ v + w ​ v + v m ​ v G^{D}_{ij,kl}=I+v_{i}u+v_{j}u+v_{k}w+v_{l}w+uv+wv+v_{m}v  ( { i , j , k , l , m } = { 1 , 2 , 3 , 4 , 5 } \{i,j,k,l,m\}=\{1,2,3,4,5\} ,  i < j i<j ,  i < k i<k ) 
 
 •  
 
 G E = I + v 1 ​ u + v 2 ​ v + v 3 ​ w + v 4 ​ x + v 5 ​ y + u ​ v + v ​ w + w ​ x + x ​ y + y ​ u G^{E}=I+v_{1}u+v_{2}v+v_{3}w+v_{4}x+v_{5}y+uv+vw+wx+xy+yu 
 
 
 
 
 All of  G i ​ j C G^{C}_{ij}  and  G i ​ j , k ​ l D G^{D}_{ij,kl}  and  G E G^{E}  are planar, apex, or singlecross, so every graph here is colorable.
Note that there are ten unique valid  F F  colorings and that the corresponding multiset for them must be in the form of  { { i } , { j } , { k , l , m } } \{\{i\},\{j\},\{k,l,m\}\} .
Let us denote it by  ϕ i ​ j 5 \phi^{5}_{ij} .
The restriction of a coloring of  G i ​ j C G^{C}_{ij}  must be one of  ϕ k ​ l 5 , ϕ k ​ m 5 , ϕ l ​ m 5 \phi^{5}_{kl},\phi^{5}_{km},\phi^{5}_{lm} .
The restriction of a coloring of  G i ​ j , k ​ l D G^{D}_{ij,kl}  must be one of  ϕ i ​ k 5 , ϕ i ​ l 5 , ϕ j ​ k 5 , ϕ j ​ l 5 \phi^{5}_{ik},\phi^{5}_{il},\phi^{5}_{jk},\phi^{5}_{jl} .
The restriction of a coloring of  G i ​ j , k ​ l E G^{E}_{ij,kl}  must be one of  ϕ 12 , ϕ 23 , ϕ 34 , ϕ 45 , ϕ 15 \phi_{12},\phi_{23},\phi_{34},\phi_{45},\phi_{15} . 
 
 
 Using Kempe chains, the following can be said. 
 
 
 Proposition E.2 . 
 
 Let some coloring  ϕ \phi  exist for  I I , and let  ϕ i ​ j 5 \phi^{5}_{ij}  be its ring coloring.
Then, there exists a  k ∉ { i , j } k\notin\{i,j\}  such that  ϕ i ​ k 5 \phi^{5}_{ik}  is a ring coloring of  I I . 
 
 
 
 Subproof. 
 
 We fix a color  κ = ϕ i ​ j 5 ​ ( f i ) \kappa=\phi^{5}_{ij}(f_{i})  and obtain a Kempe chain in  I I .
We Kempe change the coloring using the Kempe chain connecting  f j f_{j}  and some other ring edge, which we call  f k f_{k} .
Then, the newly created coloring of  I I  has a ring coloring  ϕ i ​ k 5 \phi^{5}_{ik} .
∎ 
 
 
 
 The above three conditions, together with the proposition, imply that  𝒞 ⁡ ( I ) \mathcal{C}(I)  contains  ϕ 12 5 , ϕ 23 5 , ϕ 34 5 , ϕ 45 5 , ϕ 15 5 \phi^{5}_{12},\phi^{5}_{23},\phi^{5}_{34},\phi^{5}_{45},\phi^{5}_{15} . 
 
 
 Let  J J  be a 5-cycle embedded in the plane.  J J  is an island and  𝒞 ⁡ ( J ) \mathcal{C}(J)  is equal to the above set. Since  I I  must be strictly larger than  J J ,  I I  is C-reducible.
∎

 Appendix H  Strengthening Tutte’s 4-Flow Conjecture for projective planar graphs 

 
 An important consequence of this paper is that the Tutte 4-Flow Conjecture can be strengthened for projective planar graphs. This is a direct consequence of our main result, Theorem  1.2  when we consider cubic graphs. In this section we prove that a minimum counterexample would need to be cubic, thus reducing the full conjecture to cubic graphs. 
 
 
 Figure 27 :  The graph  G 1 G_{1}  after splitting off the edges  v ​ x , v ​ y vx,vy  could become Petersen-like. 
 
 
 Let us extend the notion of a  minimum counterexample   G G  to be a 2-edge-connected projective planar graph  G G  with minimum  | V ⁡ ( G ) | + | E ⁡ ( G ) | |V(G)|+|E(G)|  that is not Petersen-like and does not admit a nowhere-zero 4-flow. 
 
 
 Lemma H.1 . 
 
 A minimum counterexample is a 3-connected cubic graph. 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 Suppose that  G G  is a minimum counterexample that is not cubic. If  G G  is not 2-connected, then  G = G 1 ∪ G 2 G=G_{1}\cup G_{2}  where  G 1 ∩ G 2 G_{1}\cap G_{2}  is a cutvertex. Since  G G  has no cutedges, both  G 1 G_{1}  and  G 2 G_{2}  are 2-edge-connected. If they both admit 4-flows, so does  G G . So, one of them must be Petersen-like (as otherwise we would contradict that  G G  is a minimum counterexample). However, in that case, the other one must be planar, and it it easy to see that  G G  itself would be Petersen-like. 
 
 
 Similarly, if  G G  has a 2-vertex-separator  { x , y } \{x,y\} . In that case,  G G  would be a 2-sum of two graphs  G 1 G_{1}  and  G 2 G_{2} , both containing the edge  e = x ​ y e=xy . Let  ϕ i \phi_{i}  be a nowhere-zero 4-flow in  G i G_{i}  for  i = 1 , 2 i=1,2 . If  e ∉ E ⁡ ( G ) e\notin E(G) , then we change  ϕ 2 \phi_{2}  so that  ϕ 2 ​ ( x ​ y ) = − ϕ 1 ​ ( x ​ y ) \phi_{2}(xy)=-\phi_{1}(xy) . Then it is clear that  ϕ 1 + ϕ 2 \phi_{1}+\phi_{2}  gives rise to a nowhere-zero-flow in  G G . On the other hand, if  e ∈ E ⁡ ( G ) e\in E(G) , then we change  ϕ 2 \phi_{2}  so that  ϕ 2 ​ ( x ​ y ) ≠ − ϕ 1 ​ ( x ​ y ) \phi_{2}(xy)\neq-\phi_{1}(xy) . Then, again,  ϕ 1 + ϕ 2 \phi_{1}+\phi_{2}  gives rise to a nowhere-zero-flow in  G G . 
 
 
 This leaves us with the possibility that  G 1 G_{1}  (say) is Petersen-like. In that case,  G 2 G_{2}  must be planar. Since  G G  itself is not Petersen-like, the edge  x ​ y ∈ E ⁡ ( G 1 ) xy\in E(G_{1})  is one of the 15 edges confirming that  G 1 G_{1}  is Petersen-like. In that case, we let  ϕ 1 \phi_{1}  be a nowhere-zero 4-flow of  G 1 − x ​ y G_{1}-xy  (which is 2-edge-connected since otherwise it can be shown that  G G  would be Petersen-like). Now, taking a nowhere-zero 4-flow  ϕ 2 \phi_{2}  of  G 2 G_{2}  (or of  G 2 − x ​ y G_{2}-xy  if  x ​ y ∉ E ⁡ ( G ) xy\notin E(G) ), the sum  ϕ 1 + ϕ 2 \phi_{1}+\phi_{2}  is a nowhere-zero 4-flow of  G G , a contradiction. 
 
 
 This confirms that  G G  is 3-connected. If it has a vertex  v v  of degree more than 3, consider splitting off a pair of consecutive edges  v ​ x , v ​ y vx,vy  in the clockwise rotation around  v v . The resulting graph  H H  is 2-connected since  G G  is 3-connected. A flow in  H H  gives a flow in  G G . As  H H  is smaller (it has fewer edges than  G G ), it must be Petersen-like. Let  v ′ v^{\prime}  be the vertex of degree 2 with neighbors  x , y x,y  obtained from  v v  after splitting. Since the edges  v ​ x , v ​ y vx,vy  are consecutive around  v v , the vertices  v v  and  v ′ v^{\prime}  are on the same face in the projective plane embedding of  H H , and since  G G  is not Petersen-like, they belong to distinct graphs when building  H H  from the Petersen graph. See Figure  27 . Suppose that the consecutive neighbors around  v v  are  x ′ , x , y , y ′ , … x^{\prime},x,y,y^{\prime},\dots . Now, it is easy to see that we could have split off the edges  v ​ x ′ , v ​ x vx^{\prime},vx  or the edges  v ​ y , v ​ y ′ vy,vy^{\prime}  and then  G 1 G_{1}  would not be Petersen-like, thus yielding a contradiction. 
 
 
 The above case shows that the minimum counterexample is cubic. This completes the proof.
∎
